abstract_pt: "Os mapas têm um papel essencial para o dia a dia, apoiando diversos\
  \ interesses da sociedade moderna, como a navegação inteligente no espaço ou a comunicação\
  \ de informação complexa. Focando no último, as áreas de comunicação visual e visualização\
  \ de informação fornecem métodos e técnicas para a apresentação de informação. Além\
  \ disso, a área de visualização geográfica expande esses métodos aos dados espaciais,\
  \ permitindo a exploração e análise de informação relacionada com espaço geográfico.\
  \ Como surgimento dos mapas temáticos, que adicionam uma camada estatística aos\
  \ mapas, a comunicação da informação geográfica tornou-se ainda mais efectiva. As\
  \ técnicas de mapeamento temático permitem a representação de informação e fenómenos\
  \ espaciais conhecidos, possibilitando a exploração, análise e apresentação eficiente\
  \ de dados. Adicionalmente, o avanço tecnológico em poder computacional e armazenamento,\
  \ tem permitido novas possibilidades de aquisição e processamento de informação.\
  \  Assim, a quantidade crescente de dados e a facilidade de acesso à informação,\
  \ junto com os novos métodos de representação gráfica, aumentaram a procura por\
  \ visualização geográfica, abrindo um nicho para novos desafios e possibilidades.\r\
  \nInvestigação recente em visualização adaptativa mostrou maneiras promissoras de\
  \ tornar o processo de visualização de dados transparente, permitindo uma representação\
  \ mais centrada no humano. Os métodos de visualização adaptativa, visam, em primeiro\
  \ lugar, criar uma experiência de exploração e análise de dados eficiente para uma\
  \ gama de utilizadores finais mais ampla. Embora esses métodos apresentem resultados\
  \ promissores, há alguns aspectos importantes que foram subvalorizados ou que ainda\
  \ não foram devidamente explorados. As visualizações adaptativas são geralmente\
  \ desenvolvidas para aplicação numa ampla gama de dados, independente- mente das\
  \ características específicas do domínio, o que limita o espectro potencial de designs\
  \ específicos do domínio. Além disso, na maioria dos casos, as visualizações adaptativas\
  \ implementam métodos de visualização, layouts visuais ou elementos gráficos conhecidos,\
  \ ignorando novos métodos de representação gráfica. Outra observação importante\
  \ indica que em geral os mecanismos de adaptação são baseados na descrição semântica\
  \ formal dos modelos, enquanto outros métodos de adaptação, por exemplo, encontrados\
  \ na natureza ou inspirados em outros campos científicos, podem ser concebidos.\
  \ Por último, mas não menos importante, na maioria das abordagens de visualização\
  \ adaptativa, a transformação de dados em representação visual falha em considerar\
  \ os estudos sobre a percepção humana, especialmente os mais recentes, e os efeitos\
  \ dos métodos visuais na percepção visual humana. \r\nNesta tese exploramos as possibilidades\
  \ de mapas temáticos em contexto dinâmico aplicados sobre dados geográficos complexos\
  \ e de grande volume, e o nosso trabalho fornece uma investigação inicial. Em particular,\
  \ os mapas temáticos são investigados em termos de objetos adaptáveis, e são propostas\
  \ diferentes técnicas que adaptam as variáveis visuais ou o seu layout. O nosso\
  \ trabalho inclui: desenvolvimento de novas técnicas de visualização; aplicação\
  \ de princípios de visualização dinâmica, nomeadamente no que diz respeito  a consultas\
  \ dinâmicas e representação de dados em tempo real; desenvolvimento de visualizações\
  \ capazes de adaptar a representação gráfica a um determinado contexto usando métodos\
  \ de visualização adaptativa; exploração de diferentes métodos de adaptação semântica,\
  \ métodos inspirados na natureza e aqueles baseados em abordagens de geometria computacional.\
  \ O trabalho aqui apresentado resulta em várias contribuições inovadoras importantes;\
  \ para além de uma extensa revisão da literatura, destacamos os métodos para: adaptação\
  \ de mapas hexagonais; adaptação de mapas de trânsito; agrupamento de arestas aplicado\
  \ a mapas de fluxo; procura automática de parâmetros para algoritmos de agrupamento\
  \ de arestas.\r\nNo que diz respeito ao método de adaptação de mapas hexagonais,\
  \ este é baseado na ideia de subpartição espacial semelhante a Quadtrees, resultando\
  \ em um binning hexagonal dinâmico. Propomos um algoritmo que constrói uma estrutura\
  \ hierárquica e calcula os bins das formas poligonais em cada nível da árvore. O\
  \ algoritmo é totalmente integrado ao zoom dinâmico, adaptando a granularidade e\
  \ as variáveis visuais de acordo com o nível de zoom e o conjunto de dados escolhido.\
  \ Este método foi implementado numa aplicação analítica, que também integra o nosso\
  \ modelo para diagramas de Voronoi e mapas coroplético adaptativos. No que diz respeito\
  \ ao método de adaptação de mapas de trânsito, propomos um algoritmo que transforma\
  \ mapas esquemático em mapas geográficos de forma contínua e animada em resposta\
  \ ao nível de zoom. Este método combina a nossa abordagem para representação de\
  \ pontos que tem capacidade de renderizar grandes quantidades de dados baseados\
  \ em tempo real. Adicionalmente, propomos um método para escalonamento reactivo\
  \ de elementos gráficos em resposta ao contexto de visualização. No que diz respeito\
  \ ao método de empacotamento de arestas, este é baseado em processos de auto-organização\
  \ encontrados na natureza, mais especificamente comportamento de enxame e estigmergia.\
  \ O nosso modelo vai além dos métodos tradicionais baseados na geometria computacional\
  \ e considera as propriedades dos dados, a topologia do grafo e o contexto de visualização.\
  \ Por último, mas não menos importante, desenvolvemos um método baseado em algoritmos\
  \ genéticos, que procura parâmetros de um algoritmo de agrupamento de arestas de\
  \ acordo com as métricas de qualidade do gráfico predefinidas e as preferências\
  \ do utilizador."
abstract_en: "Maps play an essential role for everyday life, assisting diverse interests\
  \ of modern society, such as intelligent navigation in space or communication of\
  \ complex information. Focussing on the latter, the fields of visual communication\
  \ and information visualization provide exceptional methods and techniques for efficient\
  \ conveyance of complex information. Furthermore, the field of geographic visualization\
  \ expands these methods to heterogeneous geographic data, enabling the exploration\
  \ and analysis of information related to geographic space. With the appearance of\
  \ thematic maps, which add a statistical layer to maps, the communication of geographic\
  \ information becomes even more effective. The well established techniques for thematic\
  \ mapping enable representation of known spatial phenomena and information, enabling\
  \ efficient exploration, analysis, and presentation of data. Moreover, the technological\
  \ advances in computational power and storage, have enabled new possibilities for\
  \ information acquisition and processing. As such, the constantly growing amounts\
  \ of data and the easy of access to information, along with novel methods for graphical\
  \ representation, increased the demand for geographic visualizations, opening a\
  \ niche for new challenges and possibilities.\r\n\r\nRecent research in adaptive\
  \ visualization shows promising ways of making the process of data visualization\
  \ transparent, enabling a more human-centered representation. The methods for adaptive\
  \ visualization, in the first place, aim at creating an efficient data exploration\
  \ and analysis experience for a broader range of end users. Although these methods\
  \ present promising results, some important aspects are under-explored and under-evaluated.\
  \ The adaptive visualizations are commonly developed for application on a broad\
  \ range of data disregarding domain specific characteristics, which limits the potential\
  \ spectrum of domain specific designs. Furthermore, in most cases adaptive visualizations\
  \ implement established visualization methods, visual layouts, or graphical elements,\
  \ neglecting novel methods for graphical representation. Another important observation\
  \ indicates that in general the adaptation mechanisms used in such systems are based\
  \ on formal semantic description of the member models, while other adaptation methods\
  \ found in nature or inspired on other scientific fields can be conceived. Last,\
  \ but not least, in most adaptive visualization approaches, the transformation of\
  \ data to visual representation fails to fully consider the studies on human perception,\
  \ specially recent ones, and the effects of visual methods on human perception.\r\
  \n\r\nIn this thesis we explore the possibilities of thematic maps in dynamic context\
  \ applied on large and complex geographic data. In particular, thematic maps are\
  \ investigated in terms of adaptable objects, and different techniques that adapt\
  \ visual variables or its layout in response to influencing factors are developed.\
  \ Our work includes: development of novel visualization techniques; application\
  \ of principles of dynamic visualization, particularly in what concerns dynamic\
  \ queries and real-time representation of data; development of visualizations capable\
  \ to adapt graphical representation to a given context using methods of adaptive\
  \ visualization and geovisualization; exploration of different from semantic adaptation\
  \ methods inspired on nature and those based on computational geometry approaches.\
  \ The work presented herein results in several novel contributions, in addition\
  \ to the extensive literature review, namely: a method for adaptation of hexagonal\
  \ maps; a method for adaptation of transit maps; a method for adaptive edge bundling\
  \ applied to flow maps; and, a method for automatic search of parameters for edge\
  \ bundling algorithms.  \r\n\r\nIn what regards the method for adaptation of hexagonal\
  \ maps, this is based on the idea of spacial subpartition similar to Quadtrees,\
  \ resulting in dynamic hexagonal binning. We propose an algorithm that builds hierarchical\
  \ structure and computes the polygonal forms bins at each level of the tree. The\
  \ algorithm is tightly integrated to dynamic zooming, adapting the granularity and\
  \ visual variables according to the level of zoom and the chosen dataset. This method\
  \ is implemented in an analytic application, which also integrates our model for\
  \ adaptive Voronoi diagrams and Choropleth maps. Concerning the method for transit\
  \ maps adaptation, we proposed an algorithm that transforms schematic maps into\
  \ geographic maps in continuous and animated fashion in response to zoom level.\
  \ This method combines our approach for point representation that is capable of\
  \ rendering high amounts of point-based data in real-time. Additionally, we propose\
  \ a method for reactive scaling of graphical elements in response to visualization\
  \ context. Regarding the method for swarm-based edge bundling, this is based on\
  \ self-organizing processes found in nature, more specifically swarm behavior and\
  \ stigmergy. Our model goes beyond traditional methods based on computation geometry,\
  \ and considers the properties of data, graph topology, and the context of visualization.\
  \ Last, but not least, we developed a method based on genetic algorithms, which\
  \ searches for parameters of a bundling algorithm according to graph quality metrics\
  \ and user preferences."

# Thematic Cartography for Adaptive Visualization Systems

Abstract
Maps play an essential role for everyday life, assisting diverse interests of modern
society, such as intelligent navigation in space or communication of complex infor-
mation. Focusing on the latter, the ￿elds of visual communication and information
visualization provide exceptional methods and techniques for e￿cient conveyance
of complex information. Furthermore, the ￿eld of geographic visualization ex-
pands these methods to heterogeneous geographic data, enabling the exploration
and analysis of information related to geographic space. With the appearance
of thematic maps, which add a statistical layer to maps, the communication of
geographic information becomes even more e￿ective. ￿e well-established tech-
niques for thematic mapping enable representation of known spatial phenomena
and information, enabling e￿cient exploration, analysis, and presentation of data.
Moreover, the technological advances in computational power and storage, have
enabled new possibilities for information acquisition and processing. As such,
the constantly growing amounts of data and the easy of access to information,
along with novel methods for graphical representation, increased the demand for
geographic visualizations, opening a niche for new challenges and possibilities.
Recent research in adaptive visualization shows promising ways of making
the process of data visualization transparent, enabling a more human-centered
representation. ￿e methods for adaptive visualization, in the ￿rst place, aim at
creating an e￿cient data exploration and analysis experience for a broader range
of end users. Although these methods present promising results, some important
aspects are under-explored and under-evaluated. ￿e adaptive visualizations are
commonly developed for application on a broad range of data disregarding domain
speci￿c characteristics, which limits the potential spectrum of domain speci￿c
designs. Furthermore, in most cases adaptive visualizations implement established
visualization methods, visual layouts, or graphical elements, neglecting novel meth-
ods for graphical representation. Another important observation indicates that
in general the adaptation mechanisms used in such systems are based on formal
semantic description of the member models, while other adaptation methods
found in nature or inspired on other scienti￿c ￿elds can be conceived. Last, but
not least, in most adaptive visualization approaches, the transformation of data
to visual representation fails to fully consider the studies on human perception,
specially recent ones, and the e￿ects of visual methods on human perception.
In this thesis we explore the possibilities of thematic maps in dynamic context
applied on large and complex geographic data. In particular, thematic maps are
investigated in terms of adaptable objects, and di￿erent techniques that adapt
visual variables or its layout in response to in￿uencing factors are developed.
Our work includes: development of novel visualization techniques; application of
principles of dynamic visualization, particularly in what concerns dynamic queries

and real-time representation of data; development of visualizations capable to adapt
graphical representation to a given context using methods of adaptive visualization
and geovisualization; exploration of di￿erent from semantic adaptation methods
inspired on nature and those based on computational geometry approaches. ￿e
work presented herein results in several novel contributions, in addition to the
extensive literature review, namely: a method for adaptation of hexagonal maps;
a method for adaptation of transit maps; a method for adaptive edge bundling
applied to ￿ow maps; and, a method for automatic search of parameters for edge
bundling algorithms.
In what regards the method for adaptation of hexagonal maps, this is based
on the idea of spacial subpartition similar to Quadtrees, resulting in dynamic
hexagonal binning. W e propose an algorithm that builds hierarchical structure
and computes the polygonal forms bins at each level of the tree. ￿e algorithm
is tightly integrated to dynamic zooming, adapting the granularity and visual
variables according to the level of zoom and the chosen dataset. ￿is method
is implemented in an analytic application, which also integrates our model for
adaptive V oronoi diagrams and Choropleth maps. Concerning the method for
transit maps adaptation, we proposed an algorithm that transforms schematic
maps into geographic maps in continuous and animated fashion in response to
zoom level. ￿is method combines our approach for point representation that is
capable of rendering high amounts of point-based data in real-time. Additionally,
we propose a method for reactive scaling of graphical elements in response to
visualization context. Regarding the method for swarm-based edge bundling, this
is based on self-organizing processes found in nature, more speci￿cally swarm
behavior and stigmergy. Our model goes beyond traditional methods based on
computation geometry, and considers the properties of data, graph topology, and
the context of visualization. Last, but not least, we developed a method based
on genetic algorithms, which searches for parameters of a bundling algorithm
according to graph quality metrics and user preferences.

Resumo
Os mapas têm um papel essencial para o dia a dia, apoiando diversos interesses da
sociedade moderna, como a navegação inteligente no espaço ou a comunicação
de informação complexa. Focando no último, as areas de comunicação visual e
visualização de informação fornecem métodos e técnicas para a apresentação de in-
formação. Além disso, a área de visualização geográ￿ca expande esses métodos aos
dados espaciais, permitindo a exploração e análise de informação relacionada com
espaço geográ￿co. Com o surgimento dos mapas temáticos, que adicionam uma
camada estatística aos mapas, a comunicação da informação geográ￿ca tornou-se
ainda mais efectiva. As técnicas de mapeamento temático permitem a representa-
ção de informação e fenómenos espaciais conhecidos, possibilitando a exploração,
análise e apresentação e￿ciente de dados. Adicionalmente, o avanço tecnológico
em poder computacional e armazenamento, tem permitido novas possibilidades
de aquisição e processamento de informação. Assim, a quantidade crescente de
dados e a facilidade de acesso à informação, junto com os novos métodos de repre-
sentação grá￿ca, aumentaram a procura por visualização geográ￿ca, abrindo um
nicho para novos desa￿os e possibilidades.
Investigação recente em visualização adaptativa mostrou maneiras promisso-
ras de tornar o processo de visualização de dados transparente, permitindo uma
representação mais centrada no humano. Os métodos de visualização adaptativa,
visam, em primeiro lugar, criar uma experiência de exploração e análise de dados
e￿ciente para uma gama de utilizadores ￿nais mais ampla. Embora esses métodos
apresentem resultados promissores, há alguns aspectos importantes que foram
subvalorizados ou que ainda não foram devidamente explorados. As visualizações
adaptativas são geralmente desenvolvidas para aplicação numa ampla gama de
dados, independentemente das características especí￿cas do domínio, o que limita
o espectro potencial de designs especí￿cos do domínio. Além disso, na maioria dos
casos, as visualizações adaptativas implementam métodos de visualização, layouts
visuais ou elementos grá￿cos conhecidos, ignorando novos métodos de represen-
tação grá￿ca. Outra observação importante indica que em geral os mecanismos
de adaptação são baseados na descrição semântica formal dos modelos, enquanto
outros métodos de adaptação, por exemplo, encontrados na natureza ou inspirados
em outros campos cientí￿cos, podem ser concebidos. Por último, mas não menos
importante, na maioria das abordagens de visualização adaptativa, a transformação
de dados em representação visual falha em considerar os estudos sobre a percep-
ção humana, especialmente os mais recentes, e os efeitos dos métodos visuais na
percepção visual humana.
Nesta tese exploramos as possibilidades de mapas temáticos em contexto di-
nâmico aplicados sobre dados geográ￿cos complexos e de grande volume, e o
nosso trabalho fornece uma investigação inicial. Em particular, os mapas temáti-

cos são investigados em termos de objetos adaptáveis, e são propostas diferentes
técnicas que adaptam as variáveis visuais ou o seu layout. O nosso trabalho inclui:
desenvolvimento de novas técnicas de visualização; aplicação de princípios de
visualização dinâmica, nomeadamente no que diz respeito a consultas dinâmicas e
representação de dados em tempo real; desenvolvimento de visualizações capazes
de adaptar a representação grá￿ca a um determinado contexto usando métodos de
visualização adaptativa; exploração de diferentes métodos de adaptação semântica,
métodos inspirados na natureza e aqueles baseados em abordagens de geometria
computacional. O trabalho aqui apresentado resulta em várias contribuições ino-
vadoras importantes; para além de uma extensa revisão da literatura, destacamos
os métodos para: adaptação de mapas hexagonais; adaptação de mapas de trân-
sito; agrupamento de arestas aplicado a mapas de ￿uxo; procura automática de
parâmetros para algoritmos de agrupamento de arestas.
No que diz respeito ao método de adaptação de mapas hexagonais, este é base-
ado na ideia de subpartição espacial semelhante a Quadtrees, resultando em um
binning hexagonal dinâmico. Propomos um algoritmo que constrói uma estrutura
hierárquica e calcula os bins das formas poligonais em cada nível da árvore. O
algoritmo é totalmente integrado ao zoom dinâmico, adaptando a granularidade e
as variáveis visuais de acordo com o nível de zoom e o conjunto de dados escolhido.
Este método foi implementado numa aplicação analítica, que também integra o
nosso modelo para diagramas de V oronoi e mapas coroplético adaptativos. No
que diz respeito ao método de adaptação de mapas de trânsito, propomos um
algoritmo que transforma mapas esquemático em mapas geográ￿cos de forma
contínua e animada em resposta ao nível de zoom. Este método combina a nossa
abordagem para representação de pontos que tem capacidade de renderizar gran-
des quantidades de dados baseados em tempo real. Adicionalmente, propomos
um método para escalonamento reactivo de elementos grá￿cos em resposta ao
contexto de visualização. No que diz respeito ao método de empacotamento de
arestas, este é baseado em processos de auto-organização encontrados na natureza,
mais especi￿camente comportamento de enxame e estigmergia. O nosso modelo
vai além dos métodos tradicionais baseados na geometria computacional e consi-
dera as propriedades dos dados, a topologia do grafo e o contexto de visualização.
Por último, mas não menos importante, desenvolvemos um método baseado em
algoritmos genéticos, que procura parâmetros de um algoritmo de agrupamento
de arestas de acordo com as métricas de qualidade do grá￿co prede￿nidas e as
preferências do utilizador.

all his trust in me and my work, for his ability to guide me without even explicitly
making so, and most of all for allowing me vast freedom in my research, which

In the same extent I am grateful to Professor Dr. Francisco Câmara Pereira,
whose research gave me the idea for this dissertation. I also would like to thank
Professor Dr. Carlos Bento, for providing conditions that made initial explorations
possible, and whose ideas allowed me consolidating the proposal for this research.
￿is research was conducted at the Department of Informatics Engineering of
the University of Coimbra, in particular Cognitive and Media Systems Group of
the Centre for Informatics and Systems. I’m grateful for all the support and encour-
agement. I would like to mention Rui Gomes, for his revision and contribution,
as well as Francisco Nibau Antunes, for helping with formulations in the initial
stages.
I also wish to thank my colleagues at the CDV Lab, who are close friends
outside the working environment. Special thanks go to Catarina Maçãs and Filipe
Assunção for their direct contribution to this thesis. In the same extent I would
like to mention João Correia, João Cunha, Daniel Lopes, Pedro Silva, and António
Cruz, with whom I had pleasure to spend hours and hours of cracking challenging
problems.
A special gratitude to my parents, Andrei and Elena, who had trust in all my
beginnings, made it possible for me to receive education, and supported me along
the way. T o my sister Alina, for long hours of discussion about random subjects,
her ability to inspire and encourage.
I am indebted to my wife Olesia for supporting me all these years, her superior
patience, her true love, and for being the key to stay in touch with reality. A special
thanks for giving birth to our son Artur.
￿ank you all!

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿–￿
Introduction
Maps play an important role in modern society. Maps are used to ￿nd ways from
one place to another, to locate points of interest, or to provide understanding of
surrounding environment. Despite the fact that maps help navigate in geographic
space, they are also an e￿ective tool for communicating other types of information
(e.g., usage of urban space, tra￿c conditions, weather conditions, social distribu-
tion, among others) helping in analysis, decision-making and communication of
high level information. For example, maps representing tra￿c data can be used
to avoid jams by displaying current status of road network; the results of national
elections can be communicated through the maps; socioeconomic data can be
plotted on a map to help make informative decisions by urban planners.
T echnological advances in graphics and computational power, as well as the
dissemination of ￿￿￿ devices that generate large amounts of geo-referenced data,
have made geovisualization highly demanded. ￿e constantly growing amounts of
spatial data and the ease of access to information play an increasingly important
role in the everyday life. Besides the increasing amount of spatial data, its structure
and complexity brought a new emergent term “Big Data” , which is characterized
by high volume, high variety, and high veracity (W ang et al.,￿￿￿￿). ￿e challenges
of the vast and increasing amounts of data lie in data retrieval, storage, analysis,
and managing, which is commonly achieved through the advances in hardware
technologies. However, the key challenge for Big Data is visualization, which heavily
relies on the human factors (e.g., perception, cognition).
￿e ￿eld of information visualization and the related ￿elds of Visual Analytics
and Geographic visualization investigates the human processing of visual infor-
mation, providing methods and techniques to explore, analyze and present data,
supporting the process of high-level information acquisition, insight gathering,
and decision-making. While information visualization focuses on the questions of
how to represent data visually that amplify cognition to support the information
acquisition process (Card et al., ￿￿￿￿), Visual Analytics investigates the transfor-
mation of data to provide the possibility to detect expected patterns and discover
unexpected knowledge (Keim et al., ￿￿￿￿). Furthermore, geovisualization inte-
grates approaches from multiple research ￿elds to provide methods and tools for
visual exploration, synthesis, and presentation of geospatial data (MacEachren and
Kraak, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e process of information acquisition proceeds in an exploratory
way, in which the user has an active role in choosing and ￿ne-tuning the right tools
to learn from data. T o support the user in this task a new interdisciplinary area of

￿ Introduction
adaptive information visualization (Ahn, ￿￿￿￿; Nazemi, ￿￿￿￿) emerged from the
￿elds of adaptive systems and information visualization, which lately was transited
to geographic visualization (Reichenbacher, ￿￿￿￿).
Adaptive information visualization provides personalized and meaningful vi-
sualization and information acquisition. Although recent research on adaptive
visualization shows promising ways of representing data dynamically in response
to the variation of user pro￿les, context, and the data, some important aspects are
under-explored and under-evaluated. Generally, adaptive visualization systems
are meant to be applied on a broad range of data disregarding domain speci￿c
characteristics, which limits potential spectrum of sophisticated designs. ￿e sec-
ond limitation of adaptive systems is the fact that data tends to be depicted using
established visualization methods, visual layouts, and graphical elements for the
data type at hand. T o our best knowledge, adaptive visualization has not investi-
gated novel approaches, designed concretely for domain speci￿c goals, which can
be integrated in adaptive visualization systems. Another important observation
indicates that in general the adaptation mechanisms used in such systems are based
on formal semantic description of the member models (e.g., user interaction model,
layout model, data model), while disregarding other methods that for instance can
be found in nature, or be inspired on other scienti￿c ￿elds. Last, but not least, the
transformation of data to visual representation in adaptive visualization approaches
does not consider in all the capacity the vast studies on human perception, the
e￿ects of visual methods on human perception, and the majority of the work done
in the past decades (e.g., Bertin (￿￿￿￿), Card et al. (￿￿￿￿), and Cleveland and McGill
(￿￿￿￿)). Additionally, our bibliographic review clearly indicates that little research
was done in the area of adaptive geovisualization.
￿e purpose of the work present in this thesis is to explore the possibilities
of thematic maps in dynamic context applied on Big Data. Although adaptive
visualization and the related ￿eld of adaptive geovisualization provided promising
approaches and presented coherent models for adaptive systems, there is still a
need for investigating speci￿c geovisualization techniques capable of working in
adaptive systems. In particular, geovisualization methods and thematic maps need
to be investigated in terms of adaptable objects, developing techniques that adapt
visual variables and/or its layout in response to in￿uencing factors. ￿is includes
development of novel visualization techniques; application of principles of dy-
namic visualization, particularly in what concerns dynamic queries and real-time
representation of data; development of visualizations capable to adapt graphical
representation to a given context (e.g., given data types, zoom levels, time-intervals,
etc.) using methods of adaptive visualization and geovisualization. Last, but not
least, the secondary goal of this work is to explore di￿erent from semantic adap-
tation methods inspired on nature and those based on computational geometry
approaches.
Finally, the work of the present thesis was developed within the context of
two areas of application of geographic visualization and Visual Analytics (V A):
(i) Urban mobility and (ii) Business Intelligence (BI). In what concerns urban

￿.￿ Research Goals ￿
mobility, we developed a blob-based model for proportional symbol maps. ￿e
model was then improved by the addition of layout adaptation mechanisms and
adaptive scaling of visual elements. Further, the model was used as a part of visual
analytics application for studying the usage of public transportation networks. ￿e
visualization was tested with the data provided by the Transport for London (TfL)
￿￿￿. In regard to business intelligence, the work was developed under a project
with the biggest retail chain in Portugal – ￿￿￿￿￿. Provided with anonymized data
of customers’ transactions, we were able to develop and test multiple visualization
models, each one having its goals and purpose of application in the analysis of
customer behavior and relation between the locations of customers residence and
the supermarkets. It is to note that urban mobility is the only context in which we
could work on transit maps, while the other types of thematic maps were studied
under the ￿￿￿￿￿ project.
￿.￿. Research Goals
￿e overall objective of the present thesis is to explore alternative approaches for
adaptive geovisualization in what regards adaptive layout and adaptive visual vari-
ables, within the context of dynamic visual analytics applications. ￿is research in-
tends to draw attention to the need for deeper studies on adaptive geovisualization,
more speci￿cally on models for thematic maps applied to adaptive visualization
systems. Additionally, it explores di￿erent nature-inspired and approaches based
on computational geometry for adaptive visualization, in addition to the existing
semantic based methods. Finally, this thesis aims at studying domain-speci￿c
adaptive visualization, rather than creating complex domain-independent adaptive
visualization systems for general purposes.
With that said, the main goal is to advance adaptive visualization, contribut-
ing with methods for thematic mapping that can be incorporated into analytic
visualization applications or in adaptive visualization environments attaining the
ultimate goal to explore, analyze, or present geographic information. T o achieve
this goal the research work was divided into several objectives:
• Provide su￿cient background on the areas of Information Visualization, Vi-
sual Analytics, geographic visualization and thematic mapping. Also, provide
extensive review of the methods for adaptive visualization and geovisualization,
including de￿nitions, taxonomies, and processes of adaptation;
• Develop adaptation methods for thematic maps that have been previously
identi￿ed in the context of Urban Mobility and Business Intelligence. In speci￿c,
develop methods for adaptive layout and visual variables adaptation applied
in thematic mapping. T o ful￿ll this objective we de￿ne speci￿c objectives as
following:
– Identify alternative mechanisms for adaptation, di￿erent from the ones
based on semantics;
– Revise exiting adaptation methods applied in maps (e.g., semantic zooming),
and develop methods for a more context-aware visualization;

￿ Introduction
– Develop and demonstrate the potential of nature-inspired methods and
those based on computational geometry applied in adaptive visualization;
– Assess the impact of developed methods both in terms e￿ciency and e￿cacy.
￿e development of adaptive visualization systems containing all of its com-
ponents (e.g., user modeling, context modeling) is out of the scope of this thesis.
Likewise, the development and study of visual content adaptation and interface
adaptation is outside the scope of this thesis.
￿.￿. Contributions
￿is thesis investigates the adaptation of visual representation, in particular visual
variables and layout adaptation, applied in the context of geographic visualization.
￿e main objective is to contribute with the initial study on the methods for adaptive
geovisualization based on existing models, and to study alternative approaches of
adaptation mechanisms. ￿is section outlines the main contributions resulting
from the work done in the context of this research.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿: Our extensive literature review of the state of the art
goes beyond the main pillars of this thesis, which is adaptive visualization and
adaptive geovisualization. It includes principal topics on information visualiza-
tion, geovisualization and Visual Analytics, providing detailed overview of the
modern de￿nitions, taxonomies, and methods for each corresponding areas. In
structured and comprehensive manner we put together theoretical background
on the information visualization, Visual Analytics, and geographic visualization.
￿e perceptual characteristics of visual mapping and the semiology were also
reviewed, as well as the tasks and visual interaction in information visualization. Fi-
nally, detailed revision of the adaptive visualization and geovisualization, including
de￿nitions, taxonomies and methods, was provided (see Part I).
￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿ Based on the reading level
and the tasks performed at certain zoom level the proposed method transforms,
in continuous and animated fashion, a transit map. In particular, a metro map
layout, originally proposed by Harry Beck (Garland, ￿￿￿￿), transforms into a ge-
ographically accurate map, retaining visual connection of the stations in both
representations. ￿e method takes zoom level and the bounding box as the param-
eters, and uses prede￿ned set of high-level tasks that are performed at di￿erent
instances of representation (e.g., identifying daily patterns of the ￿ows of passen-
gers, or analyzing the usage of a speci￿c metro station) (see Chapter ￿).
￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿ ￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿ Most modern
edge-bundling techniques compute bundles based on geometric properties of
graphs and/or the underlying data. In order to compute bundles in an adaptive
way we applied models inspired on nature. In particular, the proposed method
takes advantage of steering model proposed by Reynolds ( ￿￿￿￿) and ant-colony

￿.￿ Related Publications ￿
optimization mechanisms (Dorigo and Gambardella, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e proposed iterative
method adapts the generation of bundles based on the data characteristics, the
topology and geometry of the graph, and the layout at each iteration instance up to
the convergence to the ￿nal result. Our approach was tested on di￿erent datasets,
including non-georeferenced graphs (see Chapter ￿).
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿ ￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿
￿e parameters for swarm-based edge bundling yield di￿erent results for the
￿nal layout, ranging from rigorous geometric-looking visualization of a graph,
to expressive, data-art like, representations. In order to approximate the layout
to the desired outcome, which depends on the user intentions or target public,
we built a system based on Genetic Algorithms, that is capable to learn from the
preferences of the user, and ￿nd parameters that lead to the desired representation
style. W e tested the algorithm on our Swarm-based Edge-bundling method, as
well as on Force-Directed Edge bundling (Holten and Wijk, ￿￿￿￿). Last, but not
least, we implemented and tested the system based on single- and multi-objective
approaches, and evaluated the performance of each one (see Chapter ￿).
￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿ A visual-
ization method for multi-variate geo-referenced data to display its distribution on
the geographical space through the means of hexagonal binning. ￿e method uses
hierarchical partition of space and adaptive granularity adjustments, providing
balanced trade-o￿ between the e￿ectiveness of reading and graphical error, as well
as rendering performance, at each zoom level. Instead of abruptly changing the
representation while zooming a map, our method continuously adjusts the grid,
so the changes in the layout are hardly perceptible, allowing smooth experiences
of data analysis and exploration. ￿is method was implemented as an analytical
tool, alongside with additional visual techniques detailed in Chapter ￿.

￿.￿. ￿esis structure
￿is chapter introduces the challenges and the motivation for the present research
on adaptive visualization and outlines the goals and contributions of this work, as
well as the related publications.
In Part I we start with the state of the art. Chapter ￿ reviews existing concepts
and de￿nitions of information visualization, Visual Analytics, geographic visual-
ization, and thematic maps. Further, theories on visual mapping and graphical

￿.￿ ￿esis structure ￿
perception are described, followed by tasks and interaction in information visualiza-
tion. In the end of the chapter, existing techniques and methods for geovisualization
and thematic mapping are reviewed. Chapter ￿ introduces the areas of adaptive
information visualization and geovisualization, starting with the de￿nition, the
processes and the classi￿cation. Finally, the outline of the methods and technique
is presented, providing extensive review on state-of-the-art approaches.
In Part II of the current thesis we present our work on adaptive methods for
thematic maps. In particular, Chapter ￿ describes a visualization method for multi-
variate geo-referenced data to display its distribution on the geographical space
through the means of hexagonal binning. Also, additional visual layers are detailed
in this chapter. Further, in Chapter ￿, we present our method for dynamically
transform a transit map depending on the tasks and the reading levels. ￿en, in
Chapter ￿, we proceed with a method for computing edge bundles in an adaptive
way, based on nature inspired models. Finally, Chapter ￿ details our approach
on adaptive layout generation of an edge-bundling algorithm, taking as a basis
user preferences, the data and the context. In the end, in Chapter ￿ we perform
a summary of the work done, drawing overall conclusions and discussing future
work.

Part I
LITERATURE REVIEW AND STATE OF THE ART
￿is part of the thesis presents an extensive literature review of the
state of the art on adaptive visualization and adaptive geovisualization.
In this part we put together theoretical background on the areas of
information visualization, Visual Analytics, and geographic visualiza-
tion. Our literature review includes detailed overview of the modern
de￿nitions, taxonomies, and methods for each corresponding areas.
￿e perceptual characteristics of visual mapping and the semiology
of graphics, as well as the tasks and visual interaction in information
visualization, are also covered in this part. At the end, detailed re-
vision of the adaptive visualization and geovisualization, including
de￿nitions, taxonomies, and methods, are provided.

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿–￿￿
Information Visualization and
￿ematic Cartography
In the early ￿￿￿￿s the Bauhaus school performed studies of forms in terms of basic
geometric elements based on their beliefs that this language would be universal
both in the way of describing forms and its signi￿cance (Lupton and Miller, ￿￿￿￿).
T oday, for instance, user interface designers apply this idea of creating universal
graphical languages in so￿ware design. In particular, icons, menus, ￿lters, layouts,
and so on, are designed and applied in similar ways across di￿erent so￿ware tools,
making them intuitive and simple to use.
Earlier the similar had happened in the ￿eld of information visualization. Start-
ing with the studies of Jacques Bertin (Bertin, ￿￿￿￿), a French cartographer and
theorist, who created an universal visual language with its variables and the rules of
usage, providing a basis for the most of today’ s works. Later, his ideas were extended
and transferred to the digital world (see e.g., Mackinlay (￿￿￿￿), MacEachren (￿￿￿￿)),
attempting to provide guidelines for automating the design of graphical represen-
tation and thematic mapping. ￿e following sections concern the de￿nition of
information visualization and the related ￿elds of Visual Analytics and ￿ematic
Cartography (Section ￿.￿), visual mapping and graphical perception (Section ￿.￿),
tasks and interaction (Sections ￿.￿ and ￿.￿). Lastly, methods and techniques for
geovisualization are reviewed (Section ￿.￿).
￿.￿. De￿nition and Disambiguation
￿is section provides a de￿nition of Information Visualization, Visual Analytics,
and Geographic Visualization, in particular the de￿nition of thematic maps. A
detailed description of each thematic mapping technique follows in Section ￿.￿.
￿.￿.￿. Information Visualization and Visual Analytics
Nowadays there are numerous terminological de￿nitions of information visualiza-
tion, each one complementing each other. ￿e most common de￿nition shared by
many researchers is given by Card et al. (￿￿￿￿)– “￿e use of computer-supported,
interactive, visual representations of abstract data to amplify cognition”.F r o mt h i s
de￿nition the information visualization is seen as a means to provide insights on
data and not only pictures, whereas, the cognition is seen as an external resource

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
for thinking. Presented in graphical form, visualization takes the advantage of
human vision, the most powerful of all human senses, to convey information. Addi-
tionally, by analyzing the work of Hanson it is possible to see that it is important to
distinguish “visualization from pretty pictures” (Hanson,￿￿￿￿). He worked out this
idea saying that one must distinguish between scienti￿c and artistic visualization,
valid graphical representation of the truth and appealing images. It is to note that
Hanson’ s argument is built towards scienti￿c visualization. For some authors scien-
ti￿c visualization should be distinguished from information visualization, whereas
the later typically deals with “nonnumeric, nonspatial and high-dimensional data”
(Chen, ￿￿￿￿), and both “come from di￿erent cultures (science and design, respec-
tively)” (Manovich,￿￿￿￿). Nevertheless, scienti￿c visualization is out of the scope
of this work.
Kosara in his “visualization criticism” outlines three minimal criteria of visual-
ization (Kosara, ￿￿￿￿):
• visualization should be based on non-visual, abstract, data;
• the output from visualization should be an image;
• the result must be readable and recognizable, which means that a visualization
must provide means to extract useful information.
￿ese are the criteria that should be ful￿lled for a visualization to be considered,
as he termed, a pragmatic visualization. ￿e notion of pragmatic visualization is
related to the visualization techniques that are used to analyze data, with the goal to
explore, analyze or present information in understandable manner. ￿e key aspects
of pragmatic visualization are the visual e￿ciency, i.e., convey the information as
quickly and e￿ortless as possible, and the generalized models, i.e., the visualization
techniques that are scalable to di￿erent datasets.
Similarly, Mackinlay (￿￿￿￿) de￿nes two major criteria that any visualization
should aim to satisfy: (i) expressiveness and (ii) e￿ectiveness. Expressiveness refers
to showing the information exactly as it presented in data, which in some sense is
similar to T u￿e’ s principals of graphical integrity (T u￿e and Graves-Morris,￿￿￿￿).
E￿ectiveness addresses the cognitive aspects of human visual systems, taking into
consideration the task at hand, the domain of application, and additional context
information, with an ultimate goal to achieve interpretable and recognizable visual
representation.
Another point of view, proposed by Munzner (￿￿￿￿), suggests that visualiza-
tion should be a computer program that visually represents abstract data helping
the user to e￿ectively accomplish his/her tasks. More precisely Munzner de￿nes
visualization as follows: “Computer-based visualization systems provide visual repre-
sentations of datasets intended to help people carry out some tasks better”. Moreover,
Munzner’ s de￿nition suggests that visualizations are o￿en interactive, and should
provide means to learn from data overcoming human limits (e.g., perceptual, cog-
nitive), as well as the limits of computers and displays. Additionally, the design
space is enormous and full of trade-o￿s, with many ine￿ective possibilities.
Keim et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) add an important point to the de￿nition of information
visualization saying that “information visualization is communication of abstract

￿.￿ De￿nition and Disambiguation ￿￿
data ... through the use of interactive interfaces” . ￿e key aspect of this de￿nition is
that visualization should provide means to interact with data to communicate ana-
lytical results (we detail the interaction in information visualization in Section ￿.￿).
Purchase et al. (￿￿￿￿) share this idea, stating that the interaction, alongside with
computer graphics, serves the purpose to support users in problem solving.
Summarizing the de￿nitions presented above, the goal of information visualiza-
tion is to combine human ability to perceive information visually with the power
of modern computers to process data, with an ultimate goal to support users in
exploring, analyzing, and communicating the data, information and knowledge.
￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ Recently, the research ￿eld of Visual Analytics emerged
from Information Visualization. ￿e early and probably the most prominent de￿ni-
tion was provided by Cook and ￿omas ( ￿￿￿￿)– “Visual analytics is the science of
analytical reasoning by interactive visual interfaces”. Authors emphasize the use of
visual analytics tools and techniques to acquire information and insights from mas-
sive, ambiguous, and dynamic data, as well as to provide timely and understandable
assessments, and communicate assessment e￿ectively for action, in particular for
security related analysis tasks. Ultimately, the main focus of Visual Analytics is to
“detect the expected and discover unexpected”(Cook and ￿omas, ￿￿￿￿). In their
book “Illuminating the Path” , ￿omas and Cook point out that visual analytics is a
interdisciplinary ￿eld, and the main focus areas of which are:
• Analytical reasoning techniques: used to obtain insights and support analytical
tasks such as planning and decision-making;
• Visual representations and interaction techniques: used to enable users to explore
and understand large amounts of data, taking the advantage of humans visual
perception;
• Data representations and transformations: used to convert all types of con￿icting
and dynamic data to support visualization and analysis;
• Production, presentation and dissemination: techniques that are used to pro-
vide the ability to communicate information and analysis results to a broader
audience in the appropriate manner.
Later, the de￿nition of Visual Analytics was adjusted by other researchers
to get a more precise formulation (see e.g., Andrienko et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), Heer and
Agrawala (￿￿￿￿)). However, Keim et al. (￿￿￿￿) noted that it is hard to get a precise
de￿nition of such an interdisciplinary ￿eld, and provided, in our opinion, a closer
to precise de￿nition – “Visual analytics combines automated analysis techniques
with interactive visualizations for an e￿ective understanding, reasoning and decision-
making on the basis of very large and complex data sets. ”. First, the authors make a
distinction between Visual Analytics and information visualization, stating that
visual analytics go beyond simple visualization. Information visualizations do not
necessarily use advanced data analysis algorithms, nor do they necessarily deal
with analytic tasks. In contrast, Visual Analytics make a tight combination of
visualization, human factors (e.g., cognition and perception), and data analysis
algorithms and techniques.

Figure ￿.￿ Visual Analytics integrates diverse disciplines, such as, Scienti￿c and Informa-
tion Visualization (in the middle), Data Management and analysis, spatio-temporal data,
human cognition and perception, which depends on availability of infrastructures and
evaluation facilities (adapted from (Keim et al., ￿￿￿￿a)).
Figure ￿.￿ displays integrant disciplines of Visual Analytics. In this diagram
Visualization is in the core of Visual Analytics, which a￿er Keim is divided into
Scienti￿c and Information visualization (Keim et al., ￿￿￿￿a). Data Management
is o￿en related with how we store, query and retrieve data, and as the data gets
complex and bigger the database technologies try to address all the rising problems
to handle massive heterogeneous data. Data Mining ￿led deals with the methods
to extract valuable information from raw data using automatic analysis algorithms
(Hand, ￿￿￿￿). Perception and Cognition component focuses on the human and
contributes from diverse research ￿elds, such as Psychology, Sociology, Neuro-
science and Design to study how humans perceive and understand information
and learn from it, and to assess the implementation of e￿ective visual information
systems (detailed in section ￿.￿). Spatio-temporal Data Analysisinvolves data with
the reference in the real world, usually geographic space, that can be presented
on a map, consisting of spatial and/or temporal features. Evaluation, which only
recently have gained attention of researchers, focus on a set of methods to support
assess the e￿ectiveness, e￿ciency, and user acceptance of visualization methods,
models, and techniques. Finally, Infrastructure, in the context of Visual Analytics,
concerns with providing ground for all the processes through the means of so￿ware
infrastructure, which usually consists of custom-made applications, libraries, and
reusable modules.
￿e Visual Analytics process, summarized in Figure ￿.￿, involves acquiring
knowledge through the means of automatic and visual analysis methods in com-
bination with human interaction (Keim et al., ￿￿￿￿a). Each stage in the process
is represented by an oval. ￿e process starts with the data, which usually is pre-
processed (e.g., cleaning, normalization, grouping, integration of heterogeneous
data source, etc.), which is indicated by a Transformationarrow. In the next step
the user may choose to apply visual or automatic methods. In Automatic analysis
the user applies data mining methods to generate models, and re￿nes the model
by manipulating its parameters, which can be done through visualization. Also,

visualization can be used to verify the ￿ndings of the generated models. On the
other hand, performing Visual data exploration ￿rst allows the user to con￿rm
hypotheses, which can be generated by automated analysis. ￿is stage involves user
interaction in order to reveal insights and high level information. Also, ￿ndings in
the visualization can be used to ￿ne-tune or build a model in the automatic analy-
sis. Finally, knowledge can be gained from visualization and automatic analysis,
providing feedback loop for other tasks at hand.
Our work is mainly based on the de￿nition of information visualization pro-
vided by Card et al. ( ￿￿￿￿). In particular, the presented visualizations incorporate
various aspects of visual Analytics de￿ned by Keim et al. (￿￿￿￿), including how the
proposed methods support decision-making, data analysis, visual data exploration,
and the way how the visualizations can be applied on di￿erent datasets.
￿.￿.￿. Geovisualization and ￿ematic Cartography
Geovisualization (short for Geographic Visualization) is an emergent ￿eld that
“integrates approaches from visualization in scienti￿c computing (ViSC), cartography,
image analysis, information visualization, exploratory data analysis (￿￿￿), and ge-
ographic information system (￿￿￿) to provide theory, methods, and tools for visual
exploration, synthesis, and presentation of geospatial data (any data having geospa-
tial referencing)”(MacEachren and Kraak, ￿￿￿￿). Earlier in ￿￿￿￿ the same authors,
MacEachren et al. (￿￿￿￿), have de￿ned geovisualization as the use of visual represen-
tation, either on paper or on screen, to make spatial problems and context visible
through the use of vision (MacEachren et al., ￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿￿). A more human-centered
view for geovisualization can be described as “the creation and the use of visual

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
representations to facilitate thinking, understanding, and knowledge construction
about geospatial data” (Longley et al., ￿￿￿￿), or as the use of geospatial displays
“to explore geospatial data, and the exploration process can generate hypotheses,
develop problem solutions and ultimately construct knowledge” (Kraak, ￿￿￿￿).
Altogether, geovisualization integrates approaches from many ￿elds including
information visualization, cartography, geographic information systems, among
others, providing methods and techniques to facilitate thinking, understanding
and communication of geospatial data.
Research and practice in geovisualization, however, starts earlier with Bertin’ s
ideas for data exploration and representation (Bertin, ￿￿￿￿, originally published in
french in ￿￿￿￿). ￿e termgeographic visualization,a sw e l la st h er e l a t e dcartographic
visualization, has originated later from the ￿￿￿￿ report on visualization in scienti￿c
computing by McCormick et al. (￿￿￿￿). In this period the works in geovisualization
have been focusing on the role of map-based representations as a basis for scienti￿c
insights (DiBiase, ￿￿￿￿), and the emerging methods were used to facilitate the
work in scienti￿c research (MacEachren and Ganter, ￿￿￿￿).
￿e modern de￿nition of geovisualization was proposed by MacEachren (￿￿￿￿),
and is based on the purpose of maps and how these are used. MacEachren ( ￿￿￿￿)
used the term (Cartography) ￿ to de￿ne geovisualization in terms of map use ,
rather than map-making or research approaches to cartography ￿.￿ em a pu s e
cube, which is shown in Figure ￿.￿, characterizes visualization goals with respect
to three dimensions – level of interaction (e.g., highly interactive visualization, no
interaction at all); audience (e.g., single user, public audience); and the addressed
tasks, ranging from revealing unknown to construction of knowledge to sharing
existing knowledge. ￿e four visualization goals, which are exploration, analysis,
synthesis and presentation(identi￿ed in (MacEachren and Kraak, ￿￿￿￿)), are placed
on the diagonal of the cube. One extreme of the cube can be seen as an exploratory
visualization, which is a private, highly interactive visualization, where user can
generate and test hypothesis, as well as generate new insights. While, the other
extreme can be seen as the presentation of information in a low interactive manner
to the target audience. Ultimately, the two extremes can be seen as visual thinking
and visual communication, with visual thinking being involved in data exploration
and visual communication being concerned with presenting data (DiBiase, ￿￿￿￿).
In our work we intend to focus on the extremes of the cube developing visual-
izations that either are exploratory, or intended to communicate information to
the target audience.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿ematic cartography has a long and complex his-
tory. ￿e terminological de￿nitions, methods, and techniques have been su￿ering
changes since the ￿￿th century. In particular, thematic maps￿ emerged mainly in the
￿ ￿e superscript ￿ is a part of the original term
￿ ￿e adjective “thematic” was not used in earlier literature, instead this kind of maps were referred to
as special subject, special-purpose, statistical or data maps

Figure ￿.￿ ￿e map use cube characterizing geo-visualization objectives in a three-
dimensional space a￿er MacEachren. ￿e ￿rst version of this representation appeared
in (MacEachren, ￿￿￿￿), and later redrawn in (MacEachren et al., ￿￿￿￿) (adapted from
(MacEachren et al., ￿￿￿￿)).
second half of the seventeenth century in natural sciences (W ood, ￿￿￿￿). Edmond
Halley is recognized as originator of thematic cartography, and his maps of the
oceanic winds and monsoons (produced in ￿￿￿￿) (Halley, ￿￿￿￿) and magnetic
declination (produced in ￿￿￿￿) (Halley, ￿￿￿￿) have pioneered thematic cartography.
An early contemporary view on thematic maps, however, have appeared later
in Robinson’ s work “￿e Look of Maps” (Robinson,￿￿￿￿, ￿rst published in ￿￿￿￿).
￿e map is now seen as a channel of communication, focusing on the reader of the
map and the human vision system (Robinson and Petchenik, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e research
on the meaning of the symbols and their perception is prioritized. Later, in ￿￿￿￿
Bertin publishes his work on “Semiology of Graphics” , where he studies graphical
system and establishes a set of rules and adequacies for graphical symbols and
their application, therefore pushing forward the research and practice on thematic
maps (Bertin, ￿￿￿￿, originally published in french in ￿￿￿￿).
In the context of the present document we are not going into historical detail,
which is out of the scope of this thesis. In order to get a deeper understanding of
the historical development of thematic cartography the reader can take a look at
the work of Cauvin et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), or the structured study on the milestones in the
history of thematic maps by Friendly and Denis ( ￿￿￿￿). Instead, we proceed with
the de￿nition.
An early de￿nition of thematic cartography was provided by Robinson (￿￿￿￿)
in his book Elements of Cartography:“ [￿ematic map] is concerned with portraying

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
Map Types
Mental Tangible Virtual
Reference Thematic
Qualitative Quantitative
Single variable Multi-variable
Figure ￿.￿ Hierarchical organization and classi￿cation of di￿erent types of maps (adapted
from (Dent, ￿￿￿￿)).
the overall form of a given geographical distribution. It is the structural relationship of
each part to the whole that is important. Such a map is a kind of graphic essay dealing
with the spatial variations and interrelationships of some geographical distribution.”
.
￿e community of cartographers usually distinguishes between reference maps
(a.k.a. topographic, or general-reference, or general-usage maps) from thematic
maps (a.k.a. special-purpose or statistical maps). For Slocum ( ￿￿￿￿) thematic
maps highlight spatial patterns of geographic attributes or variables, in contrast to
reference maps, which emphasize location of spatial phenomena (Slocum, ￿￿￿￿,
p. ￿). ￿e thematic maps di￿er from the reference maps showing not only geo-
graphic features, but the attribute data – qualitative or quantitative. In other words,
a map can be seen as an integration of a mathematical space of locations XY and
a thematic space of attributes Z. As can be seen in this de￿nition the two com-
ponents – longitude and latitude – are reserved by the geographic location of the
element being represented. Consequently, these cannot be used to represent any
information, but the position in space (Bertin, ￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿).
Figure ￿.￿ displays the classi￿cation of map types. In the root maps are divided
in two categories: Mental maps, i.e., the mental images of the surrounding space;
and Tangible and Virtual maps, i.e., the maps printed on the paper or displayed on
the computer screen, respectively. ￿en, as already mentioned, maps are divided
into reference and thematic, and the former is subdivided into qualitative and
quantitative representations.
According to Dent (￿￿￿￿) thematic maps can be subdivided into two groups –
qualitativeand quantitative. Maps of the ￿rst group are designed to show the spatial
distribution or location of nominal data (e.g., land use, points of interest, election
results, etc.). Maps of the second group, on the other hand, portray numerical data
in spatial context. ￿ese data can be of the ordinal scale or the interval/ratio scale
(Bertin, ￿￿￿￿). Finally, there is a distinction between single-variable and multi-
variable maps, which are used in uni- and multi-variable analysis, respectively.

￿.￿ De￿nition and Disambiguation ￿￿
￿e graphical methods used in thematic maps for representing any kind of
quantitative and qualitative data can take several common forms: dot maps, choro-
pleth maps, proportional and graduated symbol maps, isarithmic maps, ￿ow maps,
area and distance cartograms (Dent, ￿￿￿￿). A more detailed description of each
graphical method can be found in Section ￿.￿.
Finally, thematic maps can be used in di￿erent ways. Slocum ( ￿￿￿￿) de￿nes
three basic ways of map usage: to provide speci￿c information about particular
locations; to provide general information about spatial patterns; and to compare
patterns on di￿erent maps.
Dent (￿￿￿￿) proposes a model of map communication, in which the process
starts from the private realm of visualization and exploration and ends in the ￿nal
map used for communication. Based on the models for geovisualization introduced
by DiBiase (￿￿￿￿) and MacEachren (￿￿￿￿) Dent states that map communication
should be an integrant part of thematic mapping process, and should go beyond just
a part of the process, without disregarding, however, the fact that map is a useful
tool for visualization and exploration of data. ￿erefore, Dent proposes a model
with the primary focus on communication, in which two sequential components
“cartographic thinking”a n d“cartographic communication” are recognized. ￿e
former component involves activities of visualization of data to reveal patterns
and relationships, which happens in private realm. ￿e second major component
of the model is “cartographic communication” , and it involves the preparation of
the ￿nal map for the presentation to the broader general public. Dent’s model
of thematic map communication is represented on Figure ￿.￿. Additionally, this
picture shows that going from private realm of unstructured symbolization to
the structured symbolization requires cartographic generalization, which involves
selection, classi￿cation, simpli￿cationand symbolization. Although Dent refers to
these particular methods of cartographic generalization, there are other approaches
(see for example Li ( ￿￿￿￿) and Shea and McMaster ( ￿￿￿￿)). Finally, this model
requires prior knowledge of the end user to convey the information in the most
e￿cient and e￿ective way possible, providing the feedback loop in the design
process.
￿e view held in this thesis, in general, is similar to the Dent’ s idea of map visu-
alization and communication (Dent, ￿￿￿￿). ￿ematic maps are a powerful tool for
communication of ￿ndings to the general public. However, in our understanding
the end goal of thematic maps should go beyond the communication of results.
Depending on the tasks at hand and the objectives of the visualization, thematic
maps can end up in the private realm of data exploration and analysis, similarly
to the methods of geovisualization (DiBiase, ￿￿￿￿; MacEachren, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e work
presented in this thesis resides on both corners of the map-use cube, having ex-
ploratory and analytical properties, as well as, serving the goal of presenting the
results to the general audience.

￿.￿. Visual Mapping and Perception of Information
In most, if not all, de￿nitions of information visualization the key term is abstract
data, which means that the data should be of abstract nature, i.e., non-visual data,
where no obvious spacial reference should be provided. ￿is leads to the visual
mapping problem, i.e., mapping data values to e￿ective visuals (Card et al., ￿￿￿￿).
Gee et al. (￿￿￿￿)n o t e dt h a t“information visualizations attempt to e￿ciently map
data variable onto visual dimensions in order to create graphic representations”. Since
visualization of abstract data is not a straightforward process, researchers face this
problem proposing reference models for visualization, such as in (Card et al.,￿￿￿￿).
￿e modern reference model for visualization is based on non-computational
visual mapping developed by Bertin (￿￿￿￿) in his already classical “Semiology of
Graphics” . On the other hand, the process of visual mapping must also take into
account the knowledge about how people perceive graphics (Cleveland and McGill,
￿￿￿￿). With that said, this section covers important aspects of graphical system
and its visual variables, as well as graphical perception in both static and dynamic
domains of representation.
￿.￿.￿. Semiology of Graphics
Jacques Bertin in a comprehensive and structured way theorized graphic represen-
tation (Bertin, ￿￿￿￿). He organized visual and perceptual elements according to the
features and relations in data and graphical system. Bertin’ s study on graphics as a
sign-system introduced new directions in their application. ￿e data visualization
process begun to have more control over information analysis and its represen-
tation. His studies still is considered as one of the most in￿uential in the data
visualization ￿eld, although the context su￿ered a lot of changes: the appearance
of computers that perform fast calculations, allowing interaction and animations;
high-resolution monitors that are capable to display accurate, vivid and colorful
images; and mobile devices that expand the world of information visualization with

￿.￿ Visual Mapping and Perception of Information ￿￿
many new possibilities of graphic representation. In order to correctly understand
Bertin’s theory we need to consider his assumptions: printable on white paper;
visible at a glance; reading distance of a book or atlas; normal and constant lighting;
and readily available graphic means.
In the world of information, its communication is possible due to combination
of two concepts:content and container. Content is the information to be transmitted
and container are properties of the graphic system. In visual mapping it is necessary
to separate the content from the container.
Regarding the content, ￿rst of all the information must be analyzed and cat-
egorized. Bertin de￿ned three stages of information analysis. ￿e ￿rst stage of
information analysis consists of identi￿cation ofinvariantsand variablesconsidered
as the components of information. ￿e second stage consists of the identi￿cation
of elements (or categories, or classes) and the length of given components. ￿e
notion of elements stands for identi￿able parts of a component. For example cate-
gories bus, car and motorbike are elements of component vehicle. ￿e length is the
number of elements that we are able to identify. ￿e component vehicle has the
length of three. ￿e third stage of information analysis is the identi￿cation of the
relationship between components or elements, which de￿nes organization levels.
Correct identi￿cation of the organization level of a given component has direct
impact on the e￿ectiveness of the visualization model.
￿ere are three levels of organization. ￿e ￿rst one is the qualitative level or
nominal – concepts of simple di￿erentiation, i.e., “this is similar to that” ( simi-
larity), which de￿nes associative level of organization, or “this is di￿erent from
that” (di￿erentiation) de￿nes selective level of organization. For the correct tran-
scription to graphical representation these two levels are considered as separated
notions. Second, the ordered level de￿nes another organizational level. Compo-
nents that belong to this level contain elements that have single and universal order
— a temporal order (e.g., age, generation, geologic era); and an order of sensory
discrimination (e.g., black-grey-white, small-medium-large). ￿e last level of orga-
nization is the quantitativeor interval-ratio level, which contains countable units
(“this is quarter or triple than that”). A series of numbers could be quantitative
when they specify the variation in distance among the categories.
Looking with more attention at these three levels of organization we can notice
their inclusiveness. ￿us, all quantitative series can be considered as ordered; all the
categories of an ordered series can be considered as di￿erentiated; all the categories
of qualitative series can be considered as similar. ￿is inclusiveness enables us to
identify the perceptual approaches and to choose appropriate representation for
components of equal level.
In information communication the second part is the container. In our verbal
language, in order to convey a message we use a universal system that comprises
phonemes, letters and combination rules. Note that the phonemes and the letters by
themselves are meaningless. Similarly, Bertin de￿nes a graphical system that can be
understood and used. ￿e visual units that designer has at his disposal are de￿ned
as marks. Formally, a mark is something that has to have the power to re￿ect light

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
(on paper) or emit light (on screen) in order to be visible. ￿e information is then
encoded by variation of marks, which can vary in position (planar variables), in how
we place them (implantation) and by their visual characteristic ( retinal variables).
V ariation of a mark in position can be expressed by two planar dimensions. When
￿xed at given point on the plane it can vary in size, texture, value, color, orientation
and shape. ￿us, the graphic system has eight components that are called visual
variables – two planar and six retinal variables. Finally, on the plane a mark can
be assigned as point, line or area. Bertin termed these types of signi￿cations as
implantation (classes of representation). In Bertin’s theory implantations de￿ne
classes of representation, as they have di￿erent visual characteristics. Bertin de￿nes
di￿erent features when distinguishing classes of representation: the length of retinal
variables varies with the selected class of representation; the representation of
quantities varies according to the selected implantation; di￿erence in classes of
representation are selective; and in a single image, the same concept cannot be
represented by di￿erent classes of representation.
In graphic design theory there are three primitive ￿gures of plane geometry –
the point, the line, and the area. ￿ese graphic elements are the “building blocks”
for creating images, diagrams, typography, patterns, among many others complex
designs. So, applied in visualization they are:
• Point is the ￿rst class of representation. Represents a position in space or on a
plane. Geometrically speaking, a point is a pair of x and y coordinates that has
no theoretical length or area. It has no mass at all. However, graphically it takes
a form of a dot to be visible. Marks that are assigned to a point can vary in all
visual variables.
• Line can be described by an in￿nite series of points. A line is a connection
between two points and theoretically it has length and position but no area. If a
line appears at the edges of two areas it creates boundary between them. Also,
lines can represent routes, connections, paths, etc. Marks that indicate lines can
vary in size (width), value, texture, color, orientation of its components and
shape of detail.
• Area in Bertin’s theory has slightly di￿erent meaning and application than in
graphic design. Areas are those graphical elements that have measurable size
on the plane. ￿ey can vary in position, but marks applied to areas cannot vary
in size, orientation or shape without changing their meaning, however they can
vary in value, texture and color.
￿e plane, according to Bertin, is continuous and is limited only by the limits
of perception. It o￿ers the longest visual variable that is capable to encode the
longest components of information. Since the plane has no breaks in continuity it
is very di￿cult to distinguish perceptible meaningful parts and those which do
not represent anything. When encoding information on the plane designers rely
solely on the conventions adapted within the signifying space, i.e., the part of the
image that convey meaningful information. ￿erefore, in signifying space, lack of
signs means absence of phenomena.

￿.￿ Visual Mapping and Perception of Information ￿￿
In many cases absence of phenomena can be confused with missing data, as in
a signifying space any sign means something. Consequently, information must be
applied to the entire area of signifying space. ￿e convention that we rely on is the
invariable of information and if changes it transforms the meaningful structure of
the distribution. Bertin also mentions the “inset” that is basically a supplementary
image that delimits a part of meaningful area simplifying and clarifying represen-
tation of that part. He also stated that the frame delimits signifying space, but not
necessary the phenomenon. It means that whenever we delimit a part of meaningful
area there is a presumption of an extension of the phenomenon.
Finally, the utilization of two planar dimensions is termed as “imposition” . ￿e
way how designers establish the correspondence on the plane de￿nes di￿erent
groups of graphic representation, which, according to Bertin, are diagrams, net-
works, maps and symbols. ￿e detailed description of each group is out of the
scope of this section, and can be found throughout this document.
￿e concept of visual variables has been studied and complimented by other
researchers in the ￿eld. Morrison (￿￿￿￿) extended Bertin’s classi￿cation and sug-
gested the addition of two more visual variables: color saturation and arrangement.
Color saturation (i.e., third dimension of the color) determines the spectral distri-
bution and de￿nes the amount of “gray” in a mark, which is referred to as “chroma”
or “intensity” in color theory. On the other hand, arrangement, de￿nes the layout
of graphic marks, which can vary from regular to irregular placements, i.e., from
perfect alignment (e.g., in a grid-like structure) to random placement of visual
marks.
With the advancement of technology the addition of more visual variables was
made possible. MacEachren (￿￿￿￿, ￿￿￿￿) identi￿ed three more visual variables:crisp-
ness, resolution and transparency. Crispness, also referred to as “fuzziness” in infor-
mation visualization, describes the variation in visual mark’ s contour (edge), which
is the most e￿ective visual variable for representing uncertainty (MacEachren,￿￿￿￿,
p. ￿￿). Resolution is tightly related to the cartographic generalization, making use
of di￿erent levels of abstraction to encode information, in which di￿erent graphic
elements are depicted with di￿erent resolutions, i.e., di￿erent meaningful levels of
detail. Finally, transparency, originally referred to as “fog” , determines the amount
of blending between visual mark and the background or other underlying visual
elements.
Last but not least, MacEachren ( ￿￿￿￿) describes the appropriateness of the
twelve visual variables, giving a rating of good, marginal, and poor for application
on numerical, ordinal, and categorical data, which is similar to Bertin’s two-step
rating of acceptable or unacceptable (Bertin,￿￿￿￿). ￿e suitability of visual variables
was demonstrated earlier by Mackinlay (￿￿￿￿) in his system for automating the
design of graphical representation (described later in this section).
Finally, although the use of motion as the information encoding is relatively
neglected in information visualization, there are attempts for ￿nding possible
features of animation that can be used as visual variables. In the domain of dynamic
cartography DiBiase et al. (￿￿￿￿) have extended Bertin’s visual variables with the
