Figure ￿.￿ ￿e dynamic variables and their perceptual e￿ectiveness in encoding di￿erent
types of data. ￿e perceptual properties of dynamic variables were evaluated by Köbben
and Y aman (￿￿￿￿). Figure derived from (DiBiase et al., ￿￿￿￿; Köbben and Y aman,￿￿￿￿;
MacEachren, ￿￿￿￿)
inclusion of so-called dynamic variables, such as duration, rate of change,a n dorder.
Duration is associated with the notion of “scene” in animation terms, which may
take the form of a static map, and translated to as the number of time units that
a scene is displayed. Ultimately, the duration of a scene can be used as a design
variable, and since the duration is a measure of time, it can be used to represent
ordinal or interval/ratio data. Next follows the rate of change , which becomes
available in animated representation, and can be expressed as the ratio m￿d, where
m is the magnitude of change in both planar and retinal variables and d is the
duration of a scene. Finally, the third dynamic variable is the order in which scenes
are displayed. Order can be associated to the chronological sequencing in time-
series data, or to a metric other than chronology, which may be bene￿cial in other
scenarios of geographic analysis.
Later, MacEachren,￿￿￿￿ added three more dynamic variable for animated maps
– display date, frequency,a n dsynchronization. Display date (a.k.a. moment) is the
time at which the change is initiated, and can be used for temporal (e.g., time
series) and non-temporal (e.g., appearance of some event on a map) animations.

￿.￿ Visual Mapping and Perception of Information ￿￿
Frequency as the name indicates is the rate of occurrence of graphical elements
per unit of time. Finally, synchronization is the temporal correspondence of two or
more related time series that are showed synchronously in an animated map.
Summing up, there are twelve visual variables: location, size, shape, orientation,
color hue, color value, color saturation, texture, arrangement, crispness, resolution
and opacity; and ￿ve dynamic variables: duration, rate of change, order, display
date, frequency, and synchronization. As a summary, Figure ￿.￿ and Figure ￿.￿ show
respectively existent visual and dynamic variables, their perceptual properties, as
well as their suitability for use with numerical, ordinal and categorical data. It is
important to note that other non-visual variables (e.g., sound and touch (Gri￿n,
￿￿￿￿; Krygier, ￿￿￿￿)) are out of the scope of this thesis.

￿.￿ Visual Mapping and Perception of Information ￿￿
￿.￿.￿. Graphical Perception
Visualization is tightly connected with the way the human perceive and process
visual information. Visual representation of information, in particular the choice
of graphic elements and visual variables must be based on such knowledge. In the
previous subsection we discussed information analysis and properties of graphical
system. Correct information encoding or visual representation is crucial in further
visual decoding or graphical perception. Cleveland (￿￿￿￿) claims that “ ...a graph is
a failure if the visual decoding fails. ”and “A g r a p h i c a l m e t h o d i s s u c c e s s f u l o n l y i f t h e
decoding process is e￿ective”. In the reminder of the section we describe a high-level
graphical perception, starting with the W are’ s three-stage model (W are,￿￿￿￿), then
cover Bertin’s (Bertin,￿￿￿￿) and Cleveland’s (Cleveland,￿￿￿￿) studies applied in
general graphical perception, and ending with Rensink’striadic architecture for
dynamic representation (Rensink, ￿￿￿￿, ￿￿￿￿).
Ware (￿￿￿￿) has proposed a three-stage model of perceptual processing. ￿e
￿rst stage consists of preattentive processing of visual information, which is based
on the studies of Treisman (￿￿￿￿). ￿e second stage consists in active (attentive)
perception of patterns, dividing the visual scene into regions and simple patterns.
￿e last stage in the three-stage model, the so-called “Sequential Goal-Directed
Processing”, consists in reduction of information to only few objects of attention,
which are held in visual working memory and serves as the basis of visual thinking.
￿e second and third stages can be thought of as active or attentive processing of
visual information.
￿e ￿rst stage of the W are’ s model is the preattentive processing of visual infor-
mation. In this stage the information is processed in parallel by billions of neurons
to extract low-level features, such as orientation, color, texture and movement
patterns, of the visual scene (W are, ￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿). ￿is visual query is similar to
Treisman’s set of feature maps (Treisman,￿￿￿￿). ￿e important characteristics of
this stage are rapid parallel processing of information; only basic visual features
are extracted; information is processed unconsciously; similar to other models of
information processing the parallel activation follows a bottom-up, data-driven
approach; and serves as the basis for understanding the visual salience of displayed
elements.
￿e second stage of the three-stage model is the Pattern Perception, which is a
rapid active process that divide visual ￿eld into regions and simple patterns. ￿ese
can be continuous contours, regions of the same color, and regions of the same
texture. Similar to Rensink (￿￿￿￿) this stage can be characterized as proto-object
￿ux that focuses on its dynamic nature. ￿is pattern-￿nding stage is ￿exible, and
its ￿exibility is in￿uenced by the information pulled from the feature maps (stage
￿) and the top-down activation of attention. Furthermore, this stage of pattern-
processing further splits into two branches, being one associated with object per-
ception and the other related to the tasks involved in the control of actions. ￿is
is tightly related to the “two-visual-system theory”found in (Milner and Goodale,
￿￿￿￿). One system, called the what system, account for object identi￿cation, and
another, called the action system, for locomotion and action. Finally, a series of

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
principles known as Gestalt Laws, pioneered by a group of German psychologists,
describe the way humans detect patterns in visual display, and provide a clear
description of basic perceptual phenomena (W ertheimer,￿￿￿￿). ￿ere multiple
ways the perceptual units are constructed, and they are described by the following
Gestalt principles: proximity, similarity, connectedness, continuity, symmetry, clo-
sure, relative size, relative perception, ￿gure-ground separation,a n dcommon fate
(Arnheim, ￿￿￿￿; W ertheimer,￿￿￿￿; W ertheimer and W ertheimer,￿￿￿￿). In the
end, W are characterizes the second stage including slower serial processing, a
￿exible interchange between bottom-up and top-down visual processing, and the
di￿erentiation between the perception and action channels.
￿e last stage of the W are’ s model is the“Sequential Goal-Directed Processing”,
and it is the highest level of perception that involves visual working memory and
active attention (W are,￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿). As we use external visualizations, we build a
sequence of visual queries, which are ful￿lled through visual search strategies. At
this stage only few objects can be gotten into attention, which are constructed from
the available patterns to ful￿ll given visual query task and the knowledge stored in
long-term memory associated to the given visual pattern.
Regarding visual information processing in dynamic representation, which is
tightly related to the W are’s movement pattern perception (W are,￿￿￿￿), Rensink
(￿￿￿￿)p r o p o s e saTriadic Architecture, which explains to some extent the way
how visual representation in dynamic terms is created and visually processed.
Based on the Coherence ￿eory he claims that “focused attention is needed to see
changes”, i.e., a change can be perceptible only if focused attention is given at
the time that change occurs. Moreover, only a small number of objects can be
in focused attention, leading to a coherent and stable representation. Ultimately,
“change blindness”will arise if attention cannot be naturally directed to the change,
leaving the changing objects unattended.
Based on that theory and discarding the assumption that all visual processing
take place in a single attentional locus(i.e., attento-centricmodel of vision), Rensink
(￿￿￿￿) proposes a triadic architecture with independent information processing
systems, which consist of the following:
• Early processing. ￿e ￿rst, low-level system, acts at the level of preattentive
features to prioritize attention, directing it to the areas of interest. ￿is system
characterized by the rapid creation of highly-detailed, volatile structures; and
the early parallel processing and creation of “proto-objects”, which can be com-
plex, can have limited spatial coherence, and can be replaced by new stimuli
(Rensink, ￿￿￿￿, ￿￿￿￿).
• Object system. ￿e second system, focused and limited-capacity attention system,
forms the structures created by the ￿rst system into stable object representa-
tion. In other words, the second system consists of the perception of spatial
arrangement (i.e., Layout) of the objects in the scene. When attention is di-
rected to particular objects in the scene, this system provides a non-volatile
representation of the locations of various structures (Rensink, ￿￿￿￿).

￿.￿ Visual Mapping and Perception of Information ￿￿
• Setting system. ￿e third, limited-capacity non-attentional system, provides a
“setting” to guide focused attention through the use of gist and layout informa-
tion (Rensink, ￿￿￿￿). According to Rensink (￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿), gist (i.e., meaning)
is the most abstract aspect of a scene, which provides connection to the kind
of expected object retrieved from the long-term memory. On the other hand,
layout is an important aspect of scene structure, or spatial arrangement of ob-
jects, and is used for directing attentional shi￿s. Finally, an associated collection
of representations comprise a scene schema, which are long-term structures,
whilst the gist and layout involve short-term (i.e., working) representations.
Finally, regarding the perceptual characteristics of visual variables, these are
processed preattentively, which suggests that visual variables are processed percep-
tually and not cognitively (Roth, ￿￿￿￿). Accordingly, Bertin (￿￿￿￿) proposes the
notion of “levels of organization”, which are tightly related to the way how human’ s
eye-brain system process each visual variable. ￿ese four levels of organization
(a.k.a. perceptual properties) are the following:
• Associative perceptionis activated when looking for equalization of variation and
grouping correspondences within the same group. From Bertin’s observation
he de￿nes shape, orientation, color and texture as associative, whether color
value and size as dissociative. Basically dissociative variables are those which
create di￿erentiation in uniformity of the area.
• Selective perception is activated when identifying elements of similar category
forming so-called “families” . For all three implantations the shape is not suited
for selective perception, nor is the orientation when represented by area.
• Ordered perception is utilized when comparing several orders. For example
gray perceived in-between the white and the black colors. ￿e shape, color
and orientation variables cannot be perceived as ordered. What is immediately
perceived as ordered are texture, color value and size.
• Quantitative perceptionis activated when one seeks to de￿ne numerically the
ratio between two signs. So, only variations in size can accurately represent
quantitative values.
Cleveland and McGill ( ￿￿￿￿) did a rigorous study about human’s cognitive
tasks in graphical perception and how people decode visual information. ￿ey
observed that people execute perceptual task associated to the interpretation of
graphical representation with di￿erent degrees of accuracy. ￿ey believe that using
graphical elements that involve elementary perceptual tasks that lead to more
accurate decoding increase the chances of a correct perception of patterns and
behavior in data. ￿eir study suggest following order of elementary perceptual
tasks (from most to least accurate):
￿. Position along a common scale;
￿. Position along nonaligned scales;
￿. Length, direction, angle;
￿. Area;

Mackinlay (￿￿￿￿) noticed that, although the ranking proposed by Cleveland
and McGill (￿￿￿￿) can be applied on quantitative information encoding, it fails
when addressing non-quantitative data. Moreover, the texture and color hue, were
intentionally excluded from their study, even though, the color hue, for example,
according to Bertin (￿￿￿￿), is an e￿ective visual variable for encoding nominal data.
With that in mind, Mackinlay (￿￿￿￿) extended the tasks of graphical perception
and their ranking, covering the entire range of data types and visual variables (see
Fig. ￿.￿).
￿.￿. Tasks in information visualization
￿e designers and researchers who develop visualizations frequently start with
identi￿cation of the tasks and goals to address. ￿ere are many design guidelines
that have been proposed so far, but in this document we will focus on the most
frequently used.
Shneiderman (￿￿￿￿) proposed a general taxonomy of tasks, so called T ask
by Data Type T axonomy, which consists of the seven tasks present in table ￿.￿.
￿ese tasks are related to the data type to be visualized. ￿e model describes
seven basic data types – one-, two-, three-dimensional data, temporal and multi-
dimensional data, and tree and network structures. Additionally, Shneiderman
suggests visualization techniques to support each data type. Despite the tight
relation of the proposed techniques with the data type, this approach cannot
describe the information revealed in the data.
Keller and Keller (￿￿￿￿) propose a user-centered approach for the classi￿cation
of tasks that a user might be interested to perform. ￿e authors identify nine tasks
and categorize them as presented in Table ￿.￿:

￿.￿ Tasks in information visualization ￿￿
Overview Gain an overview of the data
Zoom Zoom-in on areas of interest
Filter Filter out the items of no interest
Details-on-demand Select an object or group of objects and get details if
needed
Relate Get the view of the relationships among data
History Keep a history of actions to enable to undo, replay and
progressive re￿nement
Extract Enables the extraction of subsets of data
Table ￿.￿ Shneiderman’ s general taxonomy of tasks.
Identify Find characteristics by which an item is recognizable
Locate Identify the position of an item, either relative or absolute
Distinguish Determine of items are distinct/di￿erent
Categorize Divide items into di￿erent classes or types
Cluster Group similar items
Rank Order items according to their relative relevance
Compare Identify similarities and di￿erences among items
Associate Find a relationship among items of the same or di￿erent types
Correlate Establish a direct connection between items (causal or reciprocal)
Table ￿.￿ Tasks a￿er Keller and Keller (￿￿￿￿).
More recently Yi et al. (￿￿￿￿) provided a comprehensive classi￿cation of tasks,
which are derived from user’s intentions and the role of interaction techniques.
￿e authors classi￿ed the tasks in seven categories, which are listed in Table ￿.￿:
Task classi￿cations show that, in general, tasks can be subdivided in high- and
low-level tasks, which imply direct connection to the interaction techniques. For
instance, Visual Information Seeking Mantrais a top-down information seeking
approach, which can be abstracted to high-level tasks exploration and search that
eventually lead to retrieving important information. In fact Zhou and Feiner (￿￿￿￿)
classify the task explore as a high-level task for search and verify. A more detailed
task classi￿cation, proposed by Pike et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), extends the already mentioned
approach of Yi et al. (￿￿￿￿). ￿ey di￿erentiate between high- and low-level interac-
tions and relate them with high- and low-level user tasks and goals (see Fig. ￿.￿).
Also, they propose a mutual feedback between user goals and tasks and the a￿or-
dances of interactive visualization. ￿is relationship between user’ s goals and tasks
and low-level interaction techniques form the so called “analytical discourse” (Pike
et al., ￿￿￿￿). Although this approach considers interaction and user’s goals and

Figure ￿.￿ High- and low-level tasks and their relation to the interactions in visualization
and user goals (image adapted from (Pike et al., ￿￿￿￿)).
tasks from both perspectives of information visualization and visual analytics, it
lacks important high-level tasks, such as “decision-making” .
Earlier, Bertin (￿￿￿￿) proposed a general approach for tasks systematization
identifying two key concepts that de￿ne tasks: those byquestion type and by reading
level. He also argues that there are as many questions as there are components
of information (data types). For each question type there are three reading lev-
els: regarding one single element – Elementary; regarding a group of elements –
Intermediate; regarding all elements – Overall.
Similar to Bertin’ s question types and reading levels, Peuquet (￿￿￿￿) identi￿ed
three basic components that concern about spatio-temporal data: space (where),
time (when) and object (what). ￿e types of questions in the proposed topology
derive from the combination of these components as following:
• when +where → what : describes an object(s) that is present at a given location
at a given time

￿.￿ Interaction in Information Visualization ￿￿
• when + what → where : describes location(s) for a given object(s) at a given
time
• where + what → when : describes time for a given object(s) at a given loca-
tion(s)
Andrienko and Andrienko (￿￿￿￿b) extended this approach including the focus
and the target, which de￿ne the “where” question. ￿e focus is made on three
fundamental components, and they are de￿ned as: focus on objects, focus on space
and focus on time.￿ e target is a characteristic of addressed question. Additionally,
making the parallel with Bertin’s approach, the reading levels are reduced to two:
elementary and synoptic (combination of intermediate and overall reading levels).
￿.￿. Interaction in Information Visualization
In modern visualization, interaction techniques are one of the key features, allowing
the user to achieve his/her goals and perform the given tasks through graphical
interaction. ￿e challenges of interacting with visualization have already been
investigated by researchers for decades. ￿is section gives an overview of the
existing concepts, methods, and techniques, providing an idea of interaction in
information visualization.
￿e “Visual Information Seeking Mantra”proposed by Shneiderman (￿￿￿￿)h a s
been used for decades as a starting point for designing advanced graphical user
interfaces, as well as providing a foundation for “T ask by Data Type T axonomy”of
information visualization. As a visual design guidance in his mantra he proposes
the following interactions –“overview ￿rst, zoom and ￿lter, then details-on-demand”,
which are ordered sequentially. So, ￿rst the user gets an overview of the data, and
identi￿es areas of interest. ￿en, for further analysis, the user needs to either ￿lter
the data or zoom to get a closer view. Finally, the user gets access to details if
needed. Additionally, the mantra makes a correlation between seven data types
and seven tasks￿ at a high level of abstraction (Shneiderman, ￿￿￿￿).
Keim (￿￿￿￿) enhanced and later re￿ned (Keim,￿￿￿￿) Shneiderman’ s mantra. He
categorized interaction techniquesbased on the e￿ects they have on the visualization,
and puts them along withdata type to be visualizedand visualization techniqueson a
three-criteria-plot, which is shown in Figure ￿.￿￿. So, altogether (Keim, ￿￿￿￿, ￿￿￿￿;
Shneiderman, ￿￿￿￿), the interaction techniques encompass the following: Data-
to- Visualization mapping, Overview, Standard, Projection, Zoom, Filter, Distortion,
Relate, History, Extract,a n dLinking-and-brushing. Recently, Keim et al. (￿￿￿￿b)
reworked Shneiderman’s method for Visual Analytics. ￿eir “Visual Analytics
Mantra”consists of the following: “ Analyze ￿rst, show the important, zoom, ￿lter
and analyze further, details on demand”.
Modern literature expands interaction techniques even further, introducing
domain or task speci￿c ones. For example, the framework of interaction tech-
niques proposed by W ard and Y ang (￿￿￿￿), distinguishes between interaction
￿ ￿e tasks in information visualization are detailed in Section ￿.￿

Figure ￿.￿￿ Enhanced classi￿cation of interaction techniques as a function of data type
and visualization technique (image adapted from (Keim, ￿￿￿￿)).
operators and interaction operands. ￿eir framework consist of a series of spaces
as operands, such as screen-space (pixels), data value-space, data structure space,
attribute-space, object-space (￿D surfaces and visualization structure-space. ￿e in-
teraction operations act upon the operands, and they are navigation, selection and
distortion. Another example, by W ard et al. (￿￿￿￿), extends the classi￿cation with
the interaction operations, such as ￿ltering, recon￿guring, encoding, connection and
abstraction/elaboration.I ti st on o t et h a tt h edistortion operation is not considered
as interaction technique anymore. Although there are many more taxonomies of
interaction techniques (see for example Yi et al. (￿￿￿￿)), this section introduces just
a representative and most common ones, therefore, not claiming to be complete.
￿.￿.￿. Visual interaction techniques
￿is subsection details the most common interaction techniques that were used in
the context of the present thesis. ￿e techniques presented in this section are based
on the already mentioned classi￿cation of (Shneiderman, ￿￿￿￿) and (Keim, ￿￿￿￿),
as well as of (Hearst et al., ￿￿￿￿). Additionally, we describe direct manipulation
and semantic zooming, which are actively applied in our work. ￿e interaction
techniques are the following:
• Overview plus Detail – ￿e goal of this technique is to show information with
di￿erent levels of detail in multiple linked views (Card et al.,￿￿￿￿). In particular,
one view shows detailed information about the area of attention, while the other
view or views display an overview of the entire information space neglecting
detail. ￿ese views can be displayed simultaneously or separately (Zhu and
Chen, ￿￿￿￿). Additionally, Card et al. (￿￿￿￿) identi￿ed two types of zooming

￿.￿ Interaction in Information Visualization ￿￿
that are usually applied when detailed view is needed: geometric and semantic
zooming (these will be detailed further in this section).
• Focus-plus-Context – ￿e underlying idea of this technique is to enable the
user to see regions of visualization of primary interest in higher detail ( focus),
while retaining the context, i.e., overview of the surrounding information, in
lower detail on the same view (Card et al., ￿￿￿￿). One of the commonly used
techniques for focus plus context is the ￿sheye view proposed by Furnas (￿￿￿￿).
Initially proposed as the model of “Degree of Interest” ￿sheye view distorts
representation at the data-level amplifying a part of the visualization, while
keeping the context visible (Furnas, ￿￿￿￿). Another example of focus-plus-
context techniques isCone Treeby Robertson et al. (￿￿￿￿) applied on hierarchical
data, which consists of displaying visual elements at the front larger than the
ones at the back in ￿D environment. Finally, Card et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) enumerate
other techniques for keeping contextual information, among which is selective
aggregation, ￿ltering, micro-macro readingsand highlighting.
• Panning and Zooming – ￿ese techniques enable the user to change the view-
point of the visualization, i.e., panning allows the user to move the view on the
screen and zooming provides the ability to move the view closer or further away
from the user (Card et al., ￿￿￿￿; Hearst et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿is is probably one of the
basic interaction techniques of visual interaction which deals with the problem
of limited space of the displays.
• Semantic zooming – In contrast to a traditional zooming, which operates on
the visual level of representation, semantic zooming does not only change
geometric properties of objects, but also modi￿es selection and structure of
data to be displayed. In other words, semantic zooming uncovers more details
about zoomed target, revealing its meaning (De Chiara et al., ￿￿￿￿; Perlin and
Fox, ￿￿￿￿).
• Direct manipulation – As the name indicates, direct manipulationacts directly
upon the graphical user interface or visualization (Shneiderman, ￿￿￿￿). Direct
manipulation relies on intuitive graphical metaphors of interaction, which are
actively studied in the Human-Computer Interaction (￿￿￿) ￿eld, providing a
user with natural interaction mechanisms (Shneiderman,￿￿￿￿). An appropriate
metaphor (e.g., the desktop metaphor, or dragging-and-dropping an icon into
a trash can) can help accomplish tasks faster. Furthermore, direct manipulation
allows the user to observe results immediately, which is essential for a natural
and e￿cient data exploration (Shneiderman, ￿￿￿￿).
• Linking and Brushing – In cases when interactive visualization uses multiple
views “linking and brushing” connects the views of the same data, such that
highlighting or selecting graphical element in one view re￿ects the change in
other views as well (Keim, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e idea of interactive linking and brushing
technique is to provide visual connection and di￿erentiation of the displayed
elements in di￿erent views.
• Use of animation – Interaction in information visualization acts at di￿erent
stages of data visualization. In contrast to the interaction that involves ma-

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
nipulation animation does not provide direct manipulation functionalities.
Instead, animation improves the understanding of visual changes resulted from
interactions by manipulation (Robertson and Mackinlay, ￿￿￿￿). In other words,
animation attracts user’s attention to visual changes maintaining context by
using smooth transitions between the initial and ￿nal view (W ard et al., ￿￿￿￿),
such that the changes lead by manipulation or data transformation can be easily
followed and understood (Heer and Robertson, ￿￿￿￿; Robertson et al., ￿￿￿￿).
Additionally, animation is also used to make occluded information visible, as
can be seen in the works of Robertson et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) and Card et al. ( ￿￿￿￿).
￿.￿. Methods and T echniques for ￿ematic Cartography
￿is section describes methods and techniques for thematic mapping, providing
details on their historic context. In particular methods, such as: dot map; choropleth
map; graduated symbol map; isarithmic and isopleth map; ￿ow map; cartograms
and schematic map are covered in detail.
￿.￿.￿. Dot Maps
In the modern literature there is ambiguity between a dot distribution and dot
density map. Also, the term can be found in its reduced form, which is simply a
dot map. In practice these terms refer to the same mapping technique (Slocum,
￿￿￿￿). For the sake of simplicity in the context of this thesis we are going to use
the term Dot Map.
Dot distribution maps depict spatial distribution of geo-referenced discrete
data through a basic graphical element – point (Slocum, ￿￿￿￿). Each point on the
map represents either one datum with known geo-location (one-to-one mapping),
or the aggregation of values (many-to-one mapping). ￿e later is used to depict
densities in corresponding geographic areas, rather than speci￿c locations (e.g.,
one dot represents ￿￿￿￿ people).
Dot distribution maps were used as early as ￿￿￿￿ (Hargreaves, ￿￿￿￿), but the ear-
liest known use of irregularly spaced dots to depict data was the map produced by
Frère de Montizon in ￿￿￿￿ (Robinson,￿￿￿￿). ￿e map depicts population in France
by administrative units, where each dot represents ￿￿,￿￿￿ people (Figure ￿.￿￿). In
￿￿￿￿ de Geer published a scienti￿c paper on dot mapping, where he theorizes the
dot method, describing the rationale behind the choice of the size of dot units for
representing more than just one object, and exempli￿es his method with a map of
the distribution of population in Sweden (De Geer, ￿￿￿￿).
An iconic example of the use of dot maps is the Disease Map that portrays the
distribution of cholera in London, produced by Dr. John Snow (T u￿e and Graves-
Morris, ￿￿￿￿). Using the General Register O￿ce’ s weekly morality reports Dr. Snow
depicted individual deaths with bars in relation to the water pumps represented
with circles (Fig. ￿.￿￿). ￿is map was an attempt to aid proving Dr. Snow’ s theory
that cholera was a waterborne, and not an airborne disease, as was believed at the
time. In its second version Dr. Snow added a dotted line to represent the walking

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques for ￿ematic Cartography ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿￿ Dot map that depicts population in France by administrative unit produced by
Frère de Montizon in ￿￿￿￿
distances to the infected water pump at the Broad Street, which proved a stronger
evidence to the cholera disease being related to the water source. ￿e Dr. Snow’s
map did not bring an end to the cholera epidemic. However, it revealed the disease
pattern in spacial context, which helped the understanding of a public health issue.
Moreover, the real historical importance of the map is due to its originality and
in￿uence. Dr. Snow’s map innovation is not the ability to encode deaths, nor the
discussion around the epidemic, but the ability to reveal high-level patterns of the
disease outbreak using a true street-level data. A decade later, such approach was
considered as a tool for studying Schistosoma Mansoni infection in urban areas
(Barreto, ￿￿￿￿).
A more recent example of a dot distribution map is theRacial Dot Map by Cable
(Cable, ￿￿￿￿). ￿is visualization depicts geographical distribution, population
density and racial diversity of people living in the ￿￿￿. At smaller zoom levels, each
dot represents one person, and at national or regional levels each point represents
the aggregation of individual dots. ￿e map produced by Ben Fry in ￿￿￿￿ is another
example of the use of dot distribution maps to depict population densities around
the world (Fry, ￿￿￿￿). In this work, the size is used in an unusual way – the denser
the region, the smaller the circle size, and vice-versa.
￿e introduction of computer technology in cartography has provided the
possibility to generate dot maps automatically. An early developed method was
presented by Dutton in ￿￿￿￿ , which describes pseudo-random dot placement in
normalized raster cell in gridded data (Dutton, ￿￿￿￿). Lavin ( ￿￿￿￿) develops a
method of quasi-random dot placement, which limits dot overlapping. His method
of “Dot-Density Shading” can be seen as equivalent to the modern binning tech-
niques for visual clutter reduction (Chua and Moere, ￿￿￿￿). Given continuous
data, Alvin’s method applies a regular grid, and for each cell the number of dots

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
Figure ￿.￿￿ Original cholera map drawn by John Snow in ￿￿￿￿.
needed to represent the value is calculated. ￿en the grid is divided in smaller grid
cells to match prede￿ned number of dots, preserving, therefore, original spatial
distribution, similar to Quadtree rationale (Samet, ￿￿￿￿). When the number of
grid cells is de￿ned a dot is placed randomly in each cell. ￿erefore, the number of
dot overlaps is reduced and can only occur when the dots are placed near the cell
boundary.
Nowadays, approaches for dot placement can vary at diverse levels of ratio-
nale.De Berg et al. (￿￿￿￿) focus on density data and provide a method for reducing
the number of dots on the map, while preserving approximate distribution of
original dot set, taking into account map scale. In contrast, the work of Hey and
Bill (￿￿￿￿) investigates the aesthetic aspect of dot maps and is based on spiral
patterns that resembles the whirlpool of a galaxy. ￿e dots are placed in centered
manner and along the spiral arms arranged in a whirl style, generating aesthetically
pleasing dot agglomerations.

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques for ￿ematic Cartography ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿￿ Distribution and intensity of illiteracy in France by Charles Dupin, ￿￿￿￿.
￿.￿.￿. Choropleth Maps
￿is kind of maps are used to represent data using area symbols. In this technique,
each area, which is usually an administrative unit, is ￿lled with the corresponding
color value, saturation or texture, depending on the quantitative data it represents.
Hue is usually used to represent categorical data (Brewer and Pickle, ￿￿￿￿). One
of the major drawbacks of this technique is that the size of the geographic area
in￿uences the perception of quantities being represented. Hence, normalized values
are used instead of absolute data. One classic example of the choropleth map is the
map produced by André-Michel Guerry in ￿￿￿￿, which depicts crime in France
from ￿￿￿￿ to ￿￿￿￿ (Meirelles, ￿￿￿￿). In modern visualization this technique is used
in the same way with the di￿erence that the maps are drawn by computers.
A historical example is a map of illiteracy in France produced by Charles
Dupin in ￿￿￿￿, also known as the ￿rst modern statistical map. ￿is map depicts
distribution and intensity of illiteracy in France by means of shading from black
to white (see Fig. ￿.￿￿). In this visualization each unique value is represented by a
unique grayscale value.

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
Figure ￿.￿￿ Graduated symbol map by Charles Minard, ￿￿￿￿. ￿e map uses pie charts with
the circles proportional to the amount of butcher’ s meat supplied to the Paris market.
￿.￿.￿. Graduated Symbol Maps
Graduated Symbol Maps are a visualization model prepared to represent quantita-
tive data on maps, typically known as Point representation(Slocum, ￿￿￿￿). ￿is
kind of thematic maps is used to represent data employing the size of correspond-
ing graphical elements (e.g., circles, rectangular bars, etc.). According to Bertin
(￿￿￿￿) a point represents a location on the map and has no theoretical area, whereas
the graphical elements that render a point can vary in size, color, etc. Consequently,
visual elements vary proportionally to data value and unlike in choropleth map
their perception does not depend on the geographical area.
One of the ￿rst graduated symbol maps is the ￿￿￿￿ map produced by Charles
Minard (see Fig. ￿.￿￿). ￿is map depicts the amount of meat provided by each
region in France to the market in Paris in a time period between ￿￿￿￿ and ￿￿￿￿. ￿e
size of each circle is proportionally scaled to quantities of meat, and each wedge of
the pie charts is a relative proportion of beef, veal and mutton.
In a more recent work Y oung and Bilton (￿￿￿￿) used a graduated symbol map to
depict the New Y ork Times’ web and mobile site tra￿c nationwide and around the
world. In the produced visualization each circle represents the number of accesses
to the sites aggregated by one minute intervals. ￿e yellow circles represent tra￿c

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques for ￿ematic Cartography ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿￿ Map of compass variations in the W estern and Southern Oceans observable in
the Y ear ￿￿￿￿ using the isarithmic technique by Edmond Halley, ￿￿￿￿.
to the website, while the red circles represent tra￿c to the mobile site. In point
representation, the graphical elements can overlap. Consequently, when a large
number of points is considered the visualization may become illegible due to
visual clutter. One of the possible solution, suggested by Bertin (￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿￿), is the
superposition of the small elements over the larger ones.
Clutter reduction in point representation is an open problem, and numerous
techniques that tackle this problem have been developed. Much research was done
in multivariate data visualization, focusing primarily on visual clutter reduction
through the means of diverse techniques including ￿ltering (Shneiderman, ￿￿￿￿)
and clustering (Andrienko and Andrienko, ￿￿￿￿). In the context of urban mobility
clustering techniques vary from simple k-Means (Ferreira et al., ￿￿￿￿a) to sophisti-
cated interactive Kohonen Maps (a.k.a. Self-Organizing Map (￿￿￿)) (Schreck et al.,
￿￿￿￿). Ellis and Dix (￿￿￿￿) have introduced a taxonomy, with multiple examples, of
the methods for visual clutter reduction, which includes: point/line displacement,

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
topological distortion, dimensional reordering, space-￿lling, and pixel-plotting
for spatial representation; animation for temporal depiction; and sampling, change
in point size, and change in opacity with the addition of the already mentioned
techniques for the appearance of the data items. A method, developed within the
context of the present thesis, combines generalization and interaction techniques
to achieve variable point overlapping in a dynamic context, which is detailed in
Chapter ￿.
￿.￿.￿. Isarithmic and Isopleth Maps
￿is kind of maps (a.k.a. surface maps (Krygier and W ood, ￿￿￿￿)) is divided in
two classes: the isarithmic map (e.g., topographic and temperature map) and the
isopleth map (e.g., population density). In the ￿rst case each isoline depicts equal
data value on a continuous distribution, i.e., each isoline connects points at speci￿c
location provided with data. On the other hand, in isopleth map lines depict
data derived from the values at speci￿ed locations assuming given interpolation
function.
Numerous examples of isarithmic maps developed during the seventh century
can be found. A notable example is the work of Edmond Halley, particularly the
statistical maps depicting the trade winds and monsoons (￿￿￿￿), and, already clas-
sical, his ￿￿￿￿ map of compass variation in the Atlantic Ocean that uses isarithmic
lines Robinson, ￿￿￿￿ (Figure ￿.￿￿). His ￿￿￿￿ isarithmic map of magnetic ￿elds is
considered the ￿rst of this type. Halley’ s map of winds, also considered as the ￿rst
meteorological map, depicts trade winds and monsoons observable in the seas
between and near the tropics (Halley, ￿￿￿￿) (see Fig. ￿.￿￿). In this map data are
represented by dashed line, where the sharp edge of strokes are pointing out from
where the wind continually comes.
Figure ￿.￿￿ A map of historical account of the trade winds and monsoons produced by
Edmond Halley, ￿￿￿￿.
A more recent work by Lightfoot and Steinberg (￿￿￿￿) uses isopleth maps to
visualize travel-times from a given location. In this visualization travel-times are
represented as follows: the user ￿xes origin location and start time; then, the system
collects and calculates travel-times from de￿ned point to every point on the map

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques for ￿ematic Cartography ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿￿ ￿e map produced by Charles Minard in ￿￿￿￿, shows exports of French wine.
in selected region. ￿e visualization is drawn on a base map, where each pixel in
continuous distribution is colored according to journey time having warm colors
that indicate short journeys and cool ones for longer journeys.
￿.￿.￿. Flow Maps
In the context of thematic mapping, the representation of the data that involves
movement from origin to destination and their connections commonly refers to
the ￿ow visualization (also known as ￿ow maps), which is deeply rooted in the
history of information visualization (Dent, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e connections and origin and
destination points are o￿en referred to as edges and nodes, respectively. Flow maps
say little or nothing about the path, but include the information about what is
￿owing (moving, migrating, etc.), what direction the ￿ow is moving and how
much is being transferred. In most cases the data is represented using line width,
color of the line and spatial properties. ￿e advantage of ￿ow maps is that they
reduce visual clutter by merging edges. However, when representing large amounts
of data, this technique presents a series of problems, such as poor perception of
the ￿ow directionality, high degrees of visual clutter and overlapping of graphical
elements that represent locations.
A historical example is a map of wine exports from France, produced by Charles
Minard in ￿￿￿￿ (T u￿e and Graves-Morris, ￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿) (see Fig. ￿.￿￿). ￿is map
depicts the quantity, as well as the direction, of wine exports encoded by the
thickness of the corresponding edges, which split from the parent edge when
needed. Additionally, this map is accompanied by time-series representation, which
gives another point of view on data.

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
More recently Phan et al. (￿￿￿￿) proposed an automated approach to generate
￿ow maps. ￿eir method is based on hierarchical clustering, given a set of nodes,
geographic locations, and ￿ow data between the nodes. ￿e proposed approach
was inspired on graph layout algorithms that minimize edge crossing intelligently
distorting original spatial positions. Additionally, this method merges the edges
with similar destinations routing them through the approximately same path.
Cornel et al. (￿￿￿￿), propose a solution for representing multiple ￿ow compo-
nents of transportation data at once. In an interactive visualization, they combine
￿ows as individual ribbons in one composite ￿ow map, indicating the direction-
ality by arrows distributed along the ribbons. Another work that addresses the
challenges of ￿ow mapping by Guo (￿￿￿￿) presents an integrated interactive vi-
sualization, which combines three components: (i) a hierarchy of geographical
areas constructed by a graph partitioning method; (ii) multivariate clustering and
its visualization on the aggregated region-to-region ￿ows; and (iii) an interactive
￿ow mapping that combines ￿ow and multivariate patterns at di￿erent hierarchi-
cal levels. In similar way, but using square grid partitioning, Chua et al. ( ￿￿￿￿)
aggregated geotagged social media data and through a bidirectional arrow visual
representation, they depicted tourists ￿ow in Italy.
Regarding the trajectory clustering and extraction, the approach presented
by Lee et al. (￿￿￿￿) proposes partitioning trajectories into a set of line segments,
and then, grouping similar line segments together in a cluster, highlighting the
common sub-trajectories.
Although ￿ow maps generally use lines to represent ￿ows, there are other meth-
ods to depict Origin-Destination ( ￿￿) data. For instance, OD-Wheel, proposed by
Lu et al. (￿￿￿￿), is a circular-linear visual design, which depicts taxi trips starting or
ending in a speci￿ed region grouped into ￿￿ clusters, allowing the user to explore
dynamic ￿￿ patterns, variation of tra￿c ￿ow volume, and traveling times. In the
work of V an Den Elzen et al. (￿￿￿￿), the authors use arcs with a clockwise reading
in their geospatial view instead of straight lines to represent ￿￿ ￿ows retrieved
from mobile phone data, accompanied by a matrix view and a timeline. In similar
fashion, Chua et al. (￿￿￿￿) use arcs to show aggregated urban ￿ows, but instead of
indicating directionality through a clockwise arrangement, they use varied thick-
ness along an arc and color to indicate incoming and outgoing ￿ows. Regarding the
matrix visual design the work by Y ang et al. (￿￿￿￿) make use of a matrix to correlate
incoming and outgoing ￿ows, visually interconnecting the matrix to two separate
maps, which show additional statistics regarding the ￿ow. Another variation of
matrix design can be found in the work of W ood et al. (￿￿￿￿). In their work the
authors partition a map with a square grid, and on top of each cell, indicating
the location of origin, they plot a heatmap-alike of destination densities using
the same grid. Finally, Kim et al. (￿￿￿￿) presented a method for visualizing ￿ows
without trajectory information. ￿ey estimate a continuous distribution of events
occurred over time and space, and extract ￿ow ￿elds utilizing a gravity model,
further visualizing these through a heatmap representation and arrow lines.

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques for ￿ematic Cartography ￿￿

Figure ￿.￿￿ Cartogram depicts the mood of nation using the size of each county propor-
tional to the mood variation throughout the day.
￿.￿.￿. Cartograms
￿is relatively recent technique distorts geometry or space of the map in proportion
to the data being represented (e.g., time travels, population, etc.) to reveal high-level
information. ￿ere are two types of cartograms: area and distance cartograms. ￿e
￿rst one is used to reveal spatial-geographic patterns using algorithms to distort
shapes of geographic features retaining transformed map as close as possible to the
geographic space. On the other hand, the distance cartograms use the relationship
in land distance to represent given data.
“Pulse of the Nation” is an example of an area cartogram, which depicts the
mood of nation inferred from tweets (Mislove et al., ￿￿￿￿). In this map the size
of each county in the U.S. is altered according to the number of tweets that origi-
nated in that region, maintaining its shape as much as possible. Additionally, color
represents the mood, where red, yellow and green stands for unhappy, neutral and
happy, respectively (Figure ￿.￿￿).
Another example is an application of distance cartograms to depict taxi tra￿c
in Grand Lisbon (Cruz and Machado, ￿￿￿￿). In this visualization the roads of the
city are contracted and expanded according the speed of taxi cabs. Additionally, the
color and the thickness of each road encodes the number of vehicles at a particular
time of the day.
￿.￿.￿. Schematic Maps
Schematic maps, more known as Transit Schematic Maps, or simply transit maps,
are a representation of geographic systems, such as roads and subway networks,

￿￿ Information Visualization and ￿ematic Cartography
Figure ￿.￿￿ London Underground Map designed by Harry Beck. First printed version, ￿￿￿￿.
using abstract graphic elements with the goal of clear communication of the topol-
ogy of such systems. ￿e main characteristics of schematic maps are the omission
of all irrelevant details, the representation of relative position of geographical fea-
tures and relations between them using abstract graphical elements (Guo, ￿￿￿￿).
A historical example is the iconic Map of the London Underground rail system
designed by Electrical Draughtsman, Harry Beck in ￿￿￿￿ and ￿rst published by
￿e London Passenger Transport Board (now Transport for London (TfL)) in
￿￿￿￿ (Garland, ￿￿￿￿). Beck designed a new representation of the London’s under-
ground network completely ignoring its geographic accuracy and focusing on its
schematic representation of the lines, stations, and interchanges (see Fig. ￿.￿￿).
￿is map consists of the graphical elements that represent stations and straight
line segments connecting them. Lines are only vertical, horizontal or on ￿￿ degree
diagonal. Stations are di￿erentiated between ordinary stations, marked with tick
marks, and interchange stations, marked with diamonds sign. Its natural and clear
design makes tube map easy to read, even at great distances. ￿e map was tested
by the London Transport in ￿￿￿￿, and ￿rst printed in large masses in ￿￿￿￿ (Garland,
￿￿￿￿).
More recent works describe techniques for automatic construction of schematic
maps. For instance, the work of Cabello and Kreveld (￿￿￿￿) introduces an algorithm
for automatic construction of schematic maps given the shape of the schematic
connections and the distance between them. ￿e work by Jenny ( ￿￿￿￿) analyzes

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques for ￿ematic Cartography ￿￿
the current London Underground Metro map and presents visual results for the
geometric accuracy and distortion of maps, which can be used as a method for
“geometric truth” of schematic maps.

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿–￿￿
Adaptive (Geo)Visualization
Adaptive visualization is a relatively young research area that shows promising
ways to handle growing amounts, complexity, and heterogeneity of data (Ahn and
Brusilovsky, ￿￿￿￿). An adaptive visualization addresses issues of dynamic data
acquisition, autonomous adaptation of visual representation in interactive or real-
time environment supporting the exploration and analysis tasks. In this chapter
we provide an overview of the de￿nitions, adaptation processes, and the meth-
ods and techniques for adaptive information visualization and geovisualization.
Additionally, we make an overview of methods and techniques of closely related
￿elds such as: dynamic visualization, automatic visualization, and semantic-driven
visualization.
￿.￿. De￿nition
Adaptive Visualization takes roots in Adaptive Hypermedia (￿￿) systems, specif-
ically in the pioneering work of Brusilovsky ( ￿￿￿￿). Brusilovsky established the
basis for the classi￿cation of adaptive hypermedia methods and techniques, and
identi￿ed four dimensions and associated elementary questions, which can be
generalized to any computational adaptive system. ￿e ￿rst dimension: Where
adaptive systems can be helpful, which is related to the applicability of adaptation
and identi￿cation of problems that can be solved with adaptive techniques. ￿e
second dimension: to What features of the user the system can adapt, which in the
context of information visualization is tightly related to the in￿uencing factors,
such as users’ characteristics (e.g., knowledge, pro￿le) (T oker et al.,￿￿￿￿), users’
needs (e.g., goals and tasks), and data (Golemati et al., ￿￿￿￿; Nazemi et al., ￿￿￿￿b).
￿e third dimension: What can be adapted by a particular technique. Brusilovsky
(￿￿￿￿) identi￿ed two groups of adaptive techniques - content and link adaptation,
and called them technologies of adaptation.￿ e fourth dimension: Why particular
methods and techniques are used, and which problems they can solve. ￿is dimen-
sion is tightly connected with the adaptation goals achieved by di￿erent methods
and techniques.
Later, Kobsa et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) proposed a general model of adaptive system that
extends the ideas proposed by Brusilovsky ( ￿￿￿￿). Kobsa’s model involves data
acquisition, formal representation of these data, and the autonomous adaptation
of content, structure, and presentation. ￿e adaptation process described in the

￿￿ Adaptive (Geo)Visualization
model of Kobsa et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) provides a foundation for many works on adaptive
systems, including adaptive visualization.
Nazemi (￿￿￿￿), based on the works of Brusilovsky and Kobsa, as well as on
the theories of Bertin and Mackinlay, and common de￿nition of information
visualization, de￿nes adaptive visualization as “ ... interactive systems that adapt
autonomously the visual variables, visual structure, visualization method, or the
composition of them by involving some form of learning, inference, or decision making
based on one or many in￿uencing factors like users’ behavior or data characteristics
to amplify cognition and enable a more e￿cient information acquisition. ”.
According to Ahn (￿￿￿￿), in broad terms, adaptive visualization is “the combi-
nation of visualization and personalization”. In particular, adaptive visualization
incorporates methods and techniques from (￿) Visualization and ￿￿￿, (￿) Person-
alization, and (￿) Information Retrieval. Based on the work of Roussinov and
Ramsey (￿￿￿￿), Ahn argues that adaptive visualization enables the user to control
the way in which the system represents the data, with the goal to provide di￿er-
ent visualizations according to di￿erent user goals and tasks, as well as di￿erent
contexts.
￿e principles of adaptive visualization can be applied to geovisualization in
general. Similar to adaptive visualization the goal of adaptation in geovisualization
is to depict geo-referenced data more e￿ciently depending on the users needs,
enhancing overall relevance. So, the adaptation concepts can be introduced to the
cartographic domain. According to Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿), adaptive geovisualiza-
tion can be de￿ned as “a map or map-like visualization adapted to a signi￿cant
extent autonomously by a system or service”, and the result of adaptation in this
context is an adaptive map. Additionally, Reichenbacher lists basic principles of
adaptive map: the adaptation process should be transparent and should be in the
form of suggestions; the user should always have the overall control of the adap-
tation; the adaptation needs to be adaptable, i.e., the user can always change the
visualization and/or adaptive behavior of the system. Finally, Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿)
states that although the interactions are essential in geovisualization, they should
be minimized in adaptive geovisualization, retaining only essential and necessary
ones.
￿.￿. Adaptation Process
￿e process of adaptation, both in adaptive geovisualization and visualization in
general, takes several steps. ￿e adaptation process in generic computational sys-
tems, a￿er Kobsa et al. (￿￿￿￿), involves three main steps: data acquisition; formal
representation of these data; autonomous adaptation of content, structure, and
presentation. ￿ese steps address three major tasks that are performed by di￿erent
system components: Acquisition, Representation,a n dProduction. (i) Acquisition
is the task to identify information (e.g., users’ characteristics, computer usage
behavior, and the usage environment) that can be used for adaptation process, and
to construct initial models, the so-called user model, usage model, and environment
model. (ii) ￿e representationand secondary inference are the tasks to express the

￿.￿ Adaptation Process ￿￿
information gathered from task (i) in structured machine-readable manner. Addi-
tionally, secondary assumptions about this information are complemented with
information from various sources. (iii) Production is the last task of the adaptation
process responsible for generation of the adaptation of content, presentation and
modality, and structure based on the information gathered from previous steps.
Nazemi (￿￿￿￿) adjusted the model of Kobsa, so that it ￿ts better in adaptive
visualization. According to Nazemi ( ￿￿￿￿) the adaptive process in information
visualization passes through (i) the acquisition of data about in￿uencing factors
(e.g., user, task, and data), (ii) formal modeling and representation of gathered data,
and (iii) the autonomous adaptation of content, structure, and representation.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ In adaptive visualization one of the crucial goals is to
adapt the representation according to some in￿uencing factors (“to what”), which
are the following:users, data,a n dtasks. Adaptive visualization uses the information
about users for adaptation process, and that information can be gathered in two
ways: (i) explicitly ask the users for the required information, or (ii) implicitly
gather the users’ information through the interaction with the system. In the ￿rst
case, the users provide information about themselves (e.g., age, sex, profession,
etc.) through a graphical user interface (Ross, ￿￿￿￿). In the second case, machine
learning techniques are used to capture users’ interactions with the system, and
to model their behavior and further extract their characteristics (Ross, ￿￿￿￿). On
the other hand, the data itself can be used for the adaptation process. As discussed
in the previous chapter, the data (type and value) plays an essential role in visual
mapping, i.e., the choice of visual variables and visualization models to depict data
in meaningful way. Finally, the task, or users’ intention to achieve certain goal, can
also be used as in￿uencing factor in adaptation process, and this information can
be acquired in similar ways the information about users is gathered. Moreover, the
goal may be known by the user or can be prede￿ned according to the objectives of
the visualization.
￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e second step in adaptation process consists of the
formal modeling of information and its representation in computer-understandable
manner. On the one hand, the information about the user gathered in the ￿rst step
is processed and behavior patterns are extracted, leading to further understanding
of user’ s characteristics. On the other hand, the domain knowledge about the data is
modeled by identifying the types of data variables, data structure, data dimensions,
among others, to determine what visualization method or visual variable to choose.
￿is is achieved using machine-learning algorithms.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿, ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿, ￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e
￿nal step in the adaptation process is the adaptation of content, structure, and
representation. In general, this is done via visual mapping, which has already been
discussed in section ￿.￿.

￿￿ Adaptive (Geo)Visualization
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e process of adaptation
in geovisualization, described by Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿), is not linear, and passes
through several steps. ￿e ￿rst consists on sensing of the context ( adaptation tar-
get), which comprises the information about the user, the current activity, the
information existent in the usage situation, and the system factors. ￿e next step
consists on detecting the changes of the context, and triggering the adaptation in
response based on the empirically de￿ned threshold values. If the adaptation is
triggered, the decision engine checks if adaptation is needed and selects an appro-
priate adaptation strategy. Further, the adaptation engine selects an appropriate
method, applies rules of the adaptation model, and chooses the objects that will be
adapted. Finally, in the last step, the adaptor, which applies the chosen methods,
rules, and parameter values, is constructed.
So, according to Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿), an adaptive geovisualization system
requires two main and three secondary models: (i) task model – description of the
user tasks and activities, as well as the changing needs; (ii) user model – models the
user roles and the user pro￿les with di￿erent needs for various user groups and
roles; (iii) domain model – captures the knowledge about real world domain; (iv)
system model – keeps the knowledge about the system of use; and (v) adaptation
model – consists of adaptation rules specifying the adaptation mechanisms.
￿.￿. Classi￿cation of Adaptive (Geo)Visualization
Adaptive visualization is a relatively new area, which, in contrast to information
visualization that already has various taxonomic descriptions, still lacks such a sys-
tematic classi￿cation. As already mentioned, adaptive visualization mainly focuses
on the in￿uencing factors that guide adaptation processes and the adaptation of vi-
sual representation itself. Regarding the later, Ahn (￿￿￿￿) proposed a classi￿cation
for adaptive visualization based on the classi￿cation of Bunt et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), which
makes use of the already introduced model of Kobsa et al. ( ￿￿￿￿). Ahn subdivided
the classi￿cation into four elementary categories: Visualization Method Adaptation,
Visual Structure Adaptation, Adaptive Annotation,a n dUser Model Visualization.
￿e Visualization Method Adaptation, as the name indicates, suggests di￿erent
visualization methods (e.g., bar chart, pie chart, table), according to the user’ s char-
acteristics. ￿e Visual Structure Adaptationincludes two techniques: (i) variation
in the layout of the visualization, and (ii) providing methods to easily explore
visualization, i.e., show di￿erent visualization layouts depending on the varying
users’ context, and navigate to the di￿erent parts of the visualization based on the
user interactions. Adaptive Annotationsacts upon the visual elements (e.g., color
or icons), and change them to provide focus on certain data in the visualization.
￿e User Model Visualizationdoes not have direct connection with the previously
mentioned categories, but is an attempt to show the contents of the user model
to the users, enabling the possibility of complementing missing information and
reducing nose in the user model.
Later, Nazemi (￿￿￿￿) worked out the classi￿cation proposed by Ahn ( ￿￿￿￿), ad-
dressing additional aspects of information visualization, such as the Implantation

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques ￿￿
and Imposition by Bertin (￿￿￿￿), e￿ectiveness and expressiveness by Mackinlay
(￿￿￿￿), and the connection between content and interaction techniques (Shneider-
man, ￿￿￿￿). His classi￿cation for adaptive visualization consists of four categories:
Visual Interface Adaptation, Visual Content Adaptation, Visual Layout Adaptation,
and Visual Variable Adaptation(Nazemi, ￿￿￿￿). Visual Interface Adaptation,a st h e
name indicates, is the adaptation of the user interface by replacing di￿erent interac-
tive visualizations. Visualization Content Adaptationaims at the choice, reduction,
or expansion of the data (content) that is visualized. Visual Layout Adaptation
changes the placement of objects on the screen and their relation to each other
(layout). Finally,Visual Variables Adaptationaims at changing visual variables (e.g.,
color, size, shape, etc.) to direct the attention to certain information or patterns.
In what regards adaptive geovisualization, Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿) proposes four
major categories of the domains where the adaptation can take place: (i)Information
Domain – the adaptation of the information content, (ii) User Interface Domain –
the adaptation of the userinterface, (iii) Presentation Domain– the adaptation of the
visualization of the information, and (iv) T echnology Domain– the adaptation of
the information encoding depending on speci￿c device characteristics. Based on the
work of Brusilovsky (￿￿￿￿), Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿) establishes various dimensions
of adaptation in geovisualization: Why – the reasons for adapting geovisualization;
What – potential objects that can be adapted;to What – in￿uencing factors to which
the objects are adapted to; When – the timing and triggering of adaptation; How –
the adaptation process and methods; How well – the evaluation of the adaptation
solution. Finally, adaptive geovisualization can be adapted to the external context
factors (external adaptation), whereas the map itself can be internally adapted
(internal adaptation). According to Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿)t h i sa s p e c ti sw h a tm a k e s
the important di￿erentiation between adaptation in cartography and visualization
in general.
Drawing a parallel between the classi￿cations for adaptive visualization and
adaptive geovisualization we can perceive common lines in the categorization:
• Visual Interface Adaptation is similar to the User Interface Domain;
• Visual Content Adaptation is similar to the Information Domain;
• Visual Layout Adaptation is similar to the Presentation Domain, which is similar
to Visual Structure Adaptation;
• Visual V ariable Adaptation is similar to the T echnology Domain, which is
similar to Adaptive Annotation.
￿.￿. Methods and T echniques
￿is section describes methods and techniques applied in adaptive (geo)visualization
systems. W e mainly focus on the mechanisms for visual adaptation, including the
method for content, layout, and visual variables adaptation. In the end we will
overview the methods applied in adaptive geovisualization systems.

￿￿ Adaptive (Geo)Visualization
(a) (b)
Figure ￿.￿ ￿e ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ visualization. (a) the interactive user interface that allows to de￿ne
synthesized information using map and timeline; (b) visual exploration environment
enable progressive analysis. From (Gotz et al., ￿￿￿￿)
￿.￿.￿. Methods for Adaptive Visualization
Following the classi￿cation by Ahn (￿￿￿￿) and Nazemi (￿￿￿￿) this section provides
examples of visualizations that use visual content adaptation, visual layout adapta-
tion, visual variable adaptation, and visual interface adaptation, or combination
of these techniques. Since visual interface adaptation addresses techniques and
methods from ￿￿￿, detailed description of the methods under this category would
go beyond the scope of the present thesis. ￿erea￿er, a short description of notable
techniques and methods for visual content, layout, and variables adaptation will
be given.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Visual Content Adaptation
￿e methods of visual content adaptation address the choice, reduction, or expan-
sion of the data being visualized. In other words, while information representation
and the way the user interface is displayed are performed by the graphical means,
content adaptation focuses on the information itself. Recall Bertin’s di￿erentia-
tion of the content and container, the methods under this category focus on the
adaptation of the former (Bertin, ￿￿￿￿).
An example of a visualization with adaptive content mechanisms is ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿,a
mixed-initiative visual analytic system (Gotz et al., ￿￿￿￿). Although not explicitly
stated as adaptive, this visualization uses mechanisms to synthesize information
in response to the user actions and analytic context. ￿e visualization aims at
supporting criminal analysts in cases under investigation. It provides a map and
a timeline, enabling interactive collection of knowledge and evidences about the
investigated case (Fig ￿.￿). ￿e architecture of ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ consists of two main
components, and several supplementary components. ￿e two main components
are the synthesis manager and the visual composition engine. ￿e system receives
user actions, and, using the action recognizer, identi￿es its type and parameters.
￿is information is sent to the visual dialog manager, which dynamically further
decides the course of actions. ￿en, the content manager dynamically retrieves the
data relevant to the user’s analytic context, and sends it to thesynthesis manager,
which automatically collects newly derived knowledge and evidence. Finally, based

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques ￿￿
(a) (b) (c) (d)
Figure ￿.￿￿e HiMap visualization. (a) initial view with the blue zoom frame; (b) geometric
zoom-in; (c) semantic zoom-in, more elements are shown; (d) hierarchical zoom, the
drilling in the cluster. From (Shi et al., ￿￿￿￿)
on the output from underlying modules the visual composition manager constructs
an interactive visual representation.
Another example by Shi et al. (￿￿￿￿) is an adaptive visualization approach called
HiMap, which visualizes large-scale online social network as clustered graphs via
hierarchical grouping and summarization. ￿e goals of their visualization are to
adaptively visualize each graph view depending on the scale, topology, and the
screen size, to provide interactive methods, to apply animation for smooth transi-
tion between zoom levels (geometric, hierarchical, and semantic zoom), and to
enable the visualization to run fast enough to provide a smooth user experience.
￿e pipeline of the visualization follows three main steps: (i) O￿ine Data Ma-
nipulation, (ii) Adaptive Data Loading/Summarization, and (iii) Clustered Graph
Visualization.￿ e O￿ine Data Manipulation consists of data collecting, cleaning,
and hierarchical clustering stages. In other words, this step does the necessary
processing of the graph data for further visualization. ￿e second step involves the
application of ranking algorithm to build a hierarchical tree with the leaf-nodes
placed within a certain depth from the root node in the current view. Further, based
on a quanti￿cation criteria of the screen size more nodes are added to the tree. In
the third step, Clustered Graph Visualization, the graph is laid out using a variant of
force-directed Kamada-Kawai algorithm (Kamada, Kawai, et al., ￿￿￿￿). In addition
to the traditional geometric zoom, the visualization uses hierarchical and semantic
zoom. Hierarchical zoom (termed drill-in/roll-up in the article) enables the navi-
gation through di￿erent graph hierarchies. ￿e semantic zoom enables adaptive
reload of the graph, providing more detailed information and focus view on the
desired portion of the graph. ￿is behavior is illustrated in the Figure ￿.￿.L a s tb u t
not least, the zooming transitions are supported with the smooth animation to
keep user’ s attention on the objects of interest.
A notable example of a visualization that focuses on the content adaptation is
Adaptive ￿￿￿￿ by Ahn and Brusilovsky (￿￿￿￿). ￿is work is based on their earlier
personalized search study – the TaskSieve (Ahn et al.,￿￿￿￿)a n dt h eVisual Informa-
tion Browsing Environment(￿￿￿￿) by Olsen et al. ( ￿￿￿￿). ￿eir work incorporates
adaptation characteristics to enable a more e￿cient search, based on the retrieved

user’ s models. ￿e idea of Adaptive￿￿￿￿ consists on the following: add two types
of Point of Interest (￿￿￿), one type for user and task model, and another for query

￿e prototype shares the search engine of the TaskSieve, and utilizes ￿￿￿￿ visual-

two types of ￿￿￿s are represented by blue and yellow circles placed on the le￿ of the

￿.￿.￿.￿. Visual Layout Adaptation
As already mentioned the visual Layout or Imposition a￿er Bertin ( ￿￿￿￿)i st h e
positioning of the graphical elements on the screen and their correlation to each
another. Visual layout adaptation or visual structure adaptation displays di￿erent
visualization layouts according to the varying context, i.e., the composition of

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿ ￿e ￿gure illustrates the SemaVis visualization. User-centered adaptation of the
visualization for the searched term Obama, which results in multiple view visualization
of relations, dependencies, temporal and geographical information. From (Kohlhammer
et al., ￿￿￿￿).
graphical marks and the correlations among them alters in response to the changes
of the impact factors (Ahn and Brusilovsky, ￿￿￿￿; Nazemi et al., ￿￿￿￿a).
A prominent example for an adaptive visualization that incorporates not only
visual layout adaptation, but also the content adaptation and the visual variable
adaptation is SemaVissystem introduced by Nazemi et al. (￿￿￿￿a). ￿eir system
consists of three components: (i) Semantics Adaptation, (ii) Layout Adaptation,
and (iii) Presentation Adaptation. ￿e pipeline for data transformation is the fol-
lowing: the ￿rst layer analyzes the semantic structure, properties, and data; then
the second component decides which is the appropriate layout algorithm for the
data; ￿nally, the chosen layout is presented to the user by the presentation layer.
￿e semantic layer formally models the data to be visualized, integrating the in-
formation about the data, its structure, and implicit and explicit properties. ￿e
layout layer structures and transforms the semantic information, and de￿nes where
and how this information will be visualized. With the formal models of various
graphical metaphors (e.g., graph layout, or space-￿lling visualizations) de￿ned,
this layer weights and classi￿es each chosen layout algorithm. Furthermore, the
impact factors, such as user’ s preferences and current task, are taken into account
for choosing an appropriate geometric data model. Finally, the presentation layer,
de￿nes the visual appearance of geometric data model, attributing the most appro-
priate visual features (e.g., size, color, texture, etc) to render data visually based on
the previously mentioned graphical system of Bertin (￿￿￿￿). Figure ￿.￿ illustrates an
example of the SemaVis usage. ￿e user searches for the term Turing, and through

Another example of layout adaptation can be found in the work of Mackinlay
et al. (￿￿￿￿). ￿e visualization system called Show Me provides a set of di￿erent
visualization layouts integrated in the commercial visualization system Tableau
(Fig. ￿.￿, (a)). ￿e system is based on the early work of Mackinlay ( ￿￿￿￿)o nt h e
automatic design of graphical presentations of relational information. As men-
tioned earlier (Sec. ￿.￿.￿) the foundation for his work was Bertin’s semiology of
graphics (Bertin, ￿￿￿￿), which was codi￿ed as algebraic operators that suggest the
most e￿ective presentations of information. ￿e automatic presentation of Show
Me comprises four main components: (i) Automatic Marks, (ii) Add to Sheet, (iii)
Show Me, and (iv) Show Me Alternatives. Provided with the speci￿ed rows and
columns Automatic Marksautomatically selects, based on the prede￿ned rules and
properties of the data, an appropriate layout for the given data (e.g., bar graph for
categorical data with quantitative values). With the contextual menu the user can
Add to Sheet a single ￿eld to the view (Fig. ￿.￿, (b)). ￿is command is based on
various heuristics, e.g., the a￿nity heuristic supports the generation of e￿ective
small multiple displays by adding related ￿elds to the visualization. ￿e Show
Me component/command, based on the data values and a set of prede￿ned rules,
decides the most appropriate layout. In the form of ranking these rules specify the
relationship between data, speci￿cally data ￿elds and attributes, and the prede￿ned
visualization models (e.g., text tables, bar graphs, line graphs, scatter plot, Gantt
diagram, heat map) built in Tableau. Finally, Show Me Alternatives allows the user
to choose an alternative visualization among the possible alternatives, without
considering the ranking (Fig. ￿.￿, (c)).

business analysts, in particular to analyze text documents from a corporate wiki

￿.￿.￿.￿. Visual Variable Adaptation
In Section ￿.￿ we have covered visual variables in information visualization and
their perception characteristics. ￿e methods for visual variable adaptation ad-
dresses the issues associated to visual mapping and directing users’ attention to
the areas of interest in the visualization.
In what concerns directing users’ attention we found several works that adapt
graphical elements, such as color and shape, to emphasize a speci￿c content in
visualization. For example the work of Brusilovsky et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), called NavEx
￿￿￿￿￿￿, is a visualization that provides personalized access to a repository of
educational examples. ￿is visualization integrates several views, such as spatial
and similarity-based representations, which use adaptive annotations of elements
to guide users’ attention. ￿ese annotations are generated based on a knowledge-
based indexing of examples with domain concepts. ￿e similarity-based view shows
educational examples laying out the icons according to the similarity among these
examples (such as show in Figure ￿.￿). ￿e layout is generated based on the Force-

￿￿ Adaptive (Geo)Visualization

A more recent work of De Jongh et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), also explores adaptable graph-
ics. In general, the visualization depicts network data of research communities,

depict degree of correlation; then user selects all the nodes of the highlighted

Another work that we found of particular interest, due to its analogy with
a map-like representations, is the one of Gansner et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), a visualization
called TVLand. In this work the authors propose grouping and depicting recom-
mendations of TV shows in map like fashion (as shown in Figure ￿.￿), instead of
traditional cluster-based representation. T o de￿ne the overall layout and separate
the regions of the TVLand the Force-Directed Placement algorithm is applied. ￿e
color-based adaptation determines the background color of the regions, where
the range between yellow and dark blue colors is mapped to the number of recom-
mended shows in the corresponding region. Additionally, the framed labels are
used to explain recommendations, i.e., the font size, background color, and frames
of the labels change according to the amount of watched time, recommended
shows, and the show already watched by the user (as shown in Figure ￿.￿￿).
