￿.￿.￿. Methods for Adaptive Geovisualization
￿is section covers methods used in adaptive geovisualization systems, in particular
the methods proposed by Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿) in his model, which is similar to
the models used in adaptive visualization. W e start with the notion of adaptable
objects, which provide the context for describing the adaptation methods.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ In the Reichenbacher’ s model for adaptive geovisualiza-
tion any properties and functions are potential adaptable objects (Reichenbacher,
￿￿￿￿). ￿ese, for instance, can be the geo-information (e.g., encoding, amount,
classi￿cation, grouping, level of detail), or the user interface (e.g., functions, inter-
action). However, the adaptation of the visualization is the most important one.
Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿) lists map layout, map section, map scale, map generaliza-
tion, method, dimension, graphical elements (e.g., color, values, size, among other
retinal variables), and typographical elements (e.g., font, style, etc) as the adaptable
objects of visualization. Furthermore, adaptable objects can be attributed to dif-
ferent levels of adaptation: high level (e.g., encoding, scale, visualization method),
and low level (e.g., color, font). Finally, depending on the object type the adaptation
can be morphological or structural, i.e., the adaptation can a￿ect visual appearance
or the functionality and the internal structure of the map, respectively.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ According to Reichenbacher (￿￿￿￿), adaptation meth-
ods, which make part of the adaptation engine, are responsible for o￿ering proce-
dures to adapt the adaptable objects to the adaptation target. As discussed earlier,
adaptation methods can a￿ect the information content and encoding,t h einforma-

￿.￿ Methods and T echniques ￿￿
tion structure,t h e user interface,a n dt h einformation presentation. In the other
words, the content adaptation methods adapt map features; structural adaptation
methods adapt map features, interaction and functions; and morphological adap-
tation methods adapt map style, graphics and text. Reichenbacher ( ￿￿￿￿)a l s o
proposes a list of adaptation methods for mobile maps:
• select map feature (the map can be ￿ltered, for instance, based on the user
pro￿le);
• reduce the map content to only few object classes;
• reduce the information density by limiting the map to selected, important
objects and information;
• remove, omit or eliminate map objects based on map saturation or capacity;
• prioritize information;
• substitute or exchange equivalent representations;
• switch between prede￿ned design alternatives or encodings;
• change representation or (re)change symbolization (e.g., di￿erent opacities for
relevance, color to grayscale, area to line/point, etc.);
• (re)con￿gure map components (e.g., di￿erent base maps for di￿erent purposes)
or dynamic composition of layers for the base map (e.g., with or without public
transport network);
• adapt the user interface, changing the degrees of freedom for interactivity;
• change encoding (e.g., vector to raster).
In the context of geovisualization, V atin and Napoli (￿￿￿￿) proposes a knowledge-
based system, which uses an ontology and rules, to characterize visualization meth-
ods and to support users in usage of geovisualization, more precisely visualization
of movement data – trajectories. ￿is work questions the requirement of new tech-
niques of visualization. ￿ere are already many solutions for visualization, ranging
from general to speci￿c domains of application. With that in mind, the researchers
propose a knowledge-based system that integrates existing techniques with ￿￿￿￿
ontology and rules that allow reasoning on data. ￿e proposed framework consists
of ￿ components:
￿. ￿e model of current situation, i.e., the context of use (e.g., a given user, the
task or ordered tasks, the time period and the data).
￿. ￿e model of graphical methods described with ￿￿￿.
￿. ￿e rules that match situations to the graphical methods (de￿ned using ￿￿￿￿￿
rules).
￿. ￿e reasoner that applies the rules.
￿ Web Ontology Language (￿￿￿) is a semantic web standard of knowledge representation including
description of concepts and the relations among them. ￿is standard expands the capabilities of
⁄￿￿￿ vocabulary (Allemang and Hendler, ￿￿￿￿).
￿ Semantic Web Rule Language(￿￿￿￿) is used to express rules and logic in ￿￿￿ ontologies.

￿￿ Adaptive (Geo)Visualization
￿is work is an example of usage of semantic web technologies in the context
of geovisualization, which depicts movement data using existing visualization
methods.
A recent work of Zhang et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) proposes a general strategy to handle
semantic level of detail in visualization of geo-information. Basically, this method
consists of mapping between urban level of detail and semantic levels of detail,
improving rendering and processing performance. ￿is strategy allows the visual-
ization to stay consistent with information density at any scale. Although the idea
is not new (see for instance semantic zoom￿, originally proposed by Perlin and Fox
(￿￿￿￿)), this method is simple, ￿exible and e￿cient in dynamic map zooming.
￿.￿. Dynamic Visualization
In the context of this thesis dynamic visualization refers to representations that
go beyond traditional static forms. A visualization that is presented in animated,
interactive or real-time forms (Heer and Robertson, ￿￿￿￿; Robertson et al., ￿￿￿￿;
Robertson and Mackinlay, ￿￿￿￿). Moreover, one of the key characteristics is that
these visualizations are fed with temporal or streamed data, dynamically updating
its representation and creating di￿erent views on the same data. When dealing
with large volumes of data, dynamic visualization facilitate visual exploration of
datasets providing a ￿rst glance about considered data.
A recent work of Cottam et al. (￿￿￿￿) presents a taxonomy of dynamic visualiza-
tions, focusing on the techniques that deal with streamed data. ￿e authors based
their classi￿cation on Bertin’ s graphic system (Bertin,￿￿￿￿) by adapting planar and
retinal variables to the context of dynamic visualization, where planar variables
are the coordinates in space and retinal variables are the size, value, orientation,
texture, hue and shape of visual elements. Each type of variables is categorized as
follows:
• ￿e changes in spatial variables were categorized in ￿ classes: Fixed –t h es p a t i a l
dimensions don’t change;Mutable – the number of visual elements remains
￿xed, but may change in position;Create – new elements may be created/added;
Create and Delete – elements may be created or deleted.
• For the retinal variables the authors identi￿ed ￿ categories: Immutable– retinal
variables don’t change;Known Scale – the scale or order are known in advance;
Extreme Bins – the scale is known as a sentinel categories (e.g., ￿￿￿+, less than
￿, etc.); Mutable Scale – new data may change the representation of elements
and even of the mapping function itself.
￿ese categories are arranged in a ￿x￿ matrix and meta categories are de￿ned:
Identity processing, Transitionaland Immediate. Finally, the decision tree guides
the choice of appropriate technique in the matrix. Likewise, our work is intended
to follow principles described in this taxonomy.
￿ Semantic zoom does not only change geometric properties of objects, but also modi￿es selection
and structure of data to be displayed

￿.￿ Dynamic Visualization ￿￿
An example of dynamic visualization is the Gapminder World tool used for
extracting trends over time (Rosling, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e visualization presents values in
a scatter plot as they progress over time. ￿e values are mapped to the size and
position of ￿xed number of graphical elements. As data updates the visualization
modi￿es the size and position on a known scale enabling comparison between
time steps. Additionally, this visualization provides an option to enable traces
that are le￿ by each graphical element as the time passes. Related to our work,
this visualization presents a set of key interactive elements to navigate in time (a
timeline), to ￿lter data, and the main visualization that dynamically updates in
animated fashion.
Another example of dynamic visualization, which additionally uses nature
inspired approach with a strong focus on aesthetics, is the code_swarm (Ogawa
and Ma, ￿￿￿￿). ￿is visualization shows the relationship between documents
and authors during the course of so￿ware development. Here, the documents
are represented with the position of points and authors that modi￿ed documents
are represented with text labels around these points. Color is used to represent
￿le type and recent activity. ￿e position and the quantity of elements appear
dynamically as emergent property of developer activity. ￿e result is a video that
shows development activity in a low-precise manner giving a general impression
of what is happening in the project. Likewise, in our work we intend to follow the
principle of building visualizations that give an overview of data neglecting the
precision of representation at initial views of visualization.
￿e work of Ferreira et al. (￿￿￿￿b) is an example of dynamic visualization of taxi
activities in New Y ork. ￿is interactive tool enables the user to specify the query
by visually selecting and connecting regions on the map. ￿en the query is sent
to database and the result is rendered on the map. ￿e visualization depicts taxi
activity by plotting pickups and drop-o￿s encoded by blue and orange dots on the
map and by providing supplementary graphics, which show additional statistical
information. For each time interval, speci￿ed by the user, the dot map reveals
the patterns of urban mobility in New Y ork city. Additionally, this exploratory
visualization implements the tasks introduced by Peuquet (￿￿￿￿) that was described
yearlier in this chapter. Similarly, in our work we intend to use dynamic queries
that are generated while interacting with visualization.
Finally, the work of W ang et al. (￿￿￿￿) is an example of interactive visualization
that uses ￿ow map in combination with propagation graph techniques to depict
tra￿c jams in a city. ￿e visualization analyzes trajectory data of cars in the city
and detects the tra￿c jam events – their occurrences in space and time. ￿en these
are depicted by coloring a￿ected segments of the road system, and by visualizing
propagation graphs, which form a high-level description of a tra￿c condition in a
city. ￿is visualization provides multiple views for exploring the map: (i) a city as a
whole, (ii) on the level of propagation graph, and (iii) on road segment level.

￿￿ Adaptive (Geo)Visualization
￿.￿. Automatic Visualization
In the ￿￿’s and beginning of ￿￿￿￿’s there was increasing interest in intelligent
or “smart” graphics, usually referred to as automatic visualization – a method to
automatically visualize data without presupposing a graphical model (Casner, ￿￿￿￿;
Iizuka et al., ￿￿￿￿; Petajan et al., ￿￿￿￿; Senay and Ignatius, ￿￿￿￿). In general, the
approaches of automatic visualization consist of a rule-based mapping between
graphical elements and data type. Additionally, underlaying mathematical statistics
are used to summarize the data. Automatic visualization is e￿cient to get a ￿rst
glance over the data. However, the disadvantage of this approach is the fact that
automatic visualization is just statistical projections on visualization artifacts. It
is extremely di￿cult to extract any insight or high-level information from such
projections.
In our literature review we were unable to ￿nd automatic visualization methods
for geovisualization, as such we will focus on two recent examples applied in general
information visualization. One such example is the AutoVis tool, developed by
Wills and Wilkinson ( ￿￿￿￿). ￿is visualization tool focuses at providing a ￿rst
glance at data before analysis is made. Given data, the system decides how to
appropriately visualize it without presupposing a visualization model and based
solely on visualization theory. ￿e overall strategy of selecting proper graphical
elements is based on so called Grammar of Graphics, introduced by Wilkinson
(￿￿￿￿). ￿en, similarly to Google’s Page Rank, a prioritizing mechanism selects
most “interesting” views to display. Finally, the visualization uses statistical methods
to ￿nd relationships in the given data, and visualize them using a graph model,
accompanied by simple graphics (e.g., area chats, bar charts, box-plots, etc.) that
summarize computed statistics.
￿.￿. Semantic-Driven Visualization
Our review of literature shows that principles of semantic mapping addresses four
goals in information visualization: to suggest visualization methods; to interact
with visualization; to ￿lter data; and to adapt graphical representation to data using
existing visualization methods (Nazemi et al., ￿￿￿￿). In the next paragraphs we
present several examples of works and methods in this area.
A visualization tool called SemaVis, which was previously detailed in Section￿.￿,
is an example of semantic-driven visualization that integrates methods of visual
adaptation of layout and semantic interaction (Nazemi et al., ￿￿￿￿a). ￿is visual-
ization implements methods for visual recommendations, i.e., the system auto-
matically chooses appropriate visual layouts and suggests them to the user. In this
interactive visualization, ￿rst, the user searches for a term and gets an adapted
interface and recommended visual layouts. ￿en, the user drags and drops the
chosen layouts to the screen and these are automatically rearranged and adapted
to the searched result. ￿e visualization implements numerous existing visualiza-
tion techniques (e.g., graph representation, time representation, thematic maps,

￿.￿ Semantic-Driven Visualization ￿￿
pie charts, etc.) and chooses them in accordance with the data and application
scenarios.
V eas et al. (￿￿￿￿) proposed a prototype of information visualization that con-
siders both visual perception guidelines and personalized recommendations to
suggest the proper visualization. ￿is visualization system consists of a set of data
types and graphical models. ￿e components of data types are de￿ned as primitive
elements of ￿￿￿￿ so that the complex structures can be constructed. Similarly,
structural elements of graphical model are described in visual vocabulary speci-
fying “visual channels” (e.g., position on x-axis and y-axis, color, size, etc.) and
the data type they support. ￿e suggestion of visualization then proceeds in two
stages: ￿rst, the attributes of the given data are extracted; then these attributes are
mapped to the visual channels of a visualization. ￿e result is a list of visualizations
including all possible mapping combinations.
￿e work of Endert (￿￿￿￿) presents a method to adapt visualization in accor-
dance with user’ s intent throughout the interaction with the visualization –semantic
interaction. ￿e author developed a method that enables “co-reasoning” between
the user and the analytical model, without its direct control. ￿is is, users directly
manipulate the visualization; then the semantic interaction engine captures the
“knowledge” of the users; and guides the underlying analytical model through its
parametrization. In other words, the parameter controls of the Graphical User
Interface (￿￿￿) bound directly to the visual metaphor. ￿e system then can infer
the user’ s analytic reasoning from the underlying mathematical model. However,
it is still unclear how this technique can guide the underlying mechanisms to
retrieve and visualize only the most relevant information with respect to the user’ s
manipulations or high-level analytical process.
Finally, a work of Lin et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) introduced an algorithm for automatic selec-
tion of semantically-resonant colorsto represent data. ￿is method tries to identify
the colors that best match the data, analyzing representative images to determine
value-color correspondence (e.g., green for data about “trees”). ￿en, given categor-
ical values and a target color palette, the algorithm chooses a unique color for each
data value. Although this method uses a statistical approach to select appropriate
colors, we included it in this category, because it is important to consider relevant
works that match the goals of semantic representation of data.
￿ Resource Description Framework (￿￿￿) is a semantic web standard to specify any type of knowledge
with small pieces including rules to specify meaning of those pieces in computer understandable
manner (Allemang and Hendler, ￿￿￿￿).

Part II
DYNAMIC CARTOGRAPHY

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿–￿￿
Research Methodology
￿e work presented in this dissertation follows a general methodology based on
the ￿ree Cycle View of Design Science Research (Hevner, ￿￿￿￿). ￿is methodology
consists of three components: (i) relevance cycle, which de￿nes the context of the
research; (ii) rigor cycle, which provides grounding theories and methods; and (iii)
design cycle, which is a loop of research activity for the construction and evaluation
of design artifacts and processes.
Each project starts with the de￿nition of the context of the work, which com-
prises the application domain, the goals and the data. In the present dissertation
this is expressed in the introductory part of each chapter. ￿e rigor cycle ensures
that each work takes into consideration state of the art techniques and methods, as
well as it updates the knowledge base by publishing scienti￿c papers in relevant
conferences, journals and books. In the context of this document the general state
of the art is discussed in the corresponding chapter, and each project’ s related work
is analyzed within each corresponding section.
￿.￿. Design cycle
In what regards the design cycle, this was mainly based on two models of visu-
alization creation: (i) the seven stage visualization process model proposed by
Fry (￿￿￿￿); (ii) nested model for visualization design and validation proposed by
Munzner (￿￿￿￿).
In regard to the model by Fry ( ￿￿￿￿), it consists of the following stages:
• Acquire – the very ￿rst stage is the data acquisition. ￿is involves collection of
data from any available source (e.g., hard disc, internet, large data bases, etc.).
• Filter – second stage is parsing of the data. ￿is consists in changing data’ s initial
structure/format to the one that is intended for the project.
• Mine – this step involves diverse scienti￿c areas, including data mining and
statistics. In this stage raw data is transformed in something meaningful that
can be further analyzed.
• Represent – ￿￿h step concerns with data representation. In this step we choose
a basic visual model, and here is where the information takes its ￿rst visual
form.
• Re￿ne – next step is the re￿nement step. In this step the basic representation is
improved by applying graphic design methods.

￿￿ Research Methodology
• Interact – in the last stage the interaction is added, enabling the user to interact
with the visualization.
￿is model is not static, nor linear allowing us to return to previous stages at any
point of the process. ￿e interaction with other stages starts in the representation.
For example, in the ￿rst representation we attempt to conclude that the data must
be ￿ltered di￿erently in order to answer the question at hand, or that there is not
enough data to answer this question, which implies acquiring more data. In the
interaction stage we can improve the visualization by returning to the re￿nement
stage, or if a￿er exploring the data we say that the information still needs additional
improvements we should go back to the mining stage.
Concerning the model by Munzner (￿￿￿￿), it consists of four nested levels for
visualization design:
• Domain problem and data characterization – this very ￿rst level concerns with
characterization of tasks and data of target user in some particular target domain.
￿is involves understanding domain’s vocabulary, work￿ow of how the data
is used, challenges to be solved, producing a set of questions asked or actions
carried out by the target users;
• Data type and operation abstraction design – this layer involves translating a
domain vocabulary to the vocabulary of information visualization. I.e., descrip-
tion of operations (i.e., tasks of the visualization domain) and data types, which
will be used in visual encoding.
• Visual encoding and interaction design– the third level is designing the visual
encoding and interaction.
• Algorithm design– the last stage consists of developing algorithms to implement
the visual encodings and interactions in a computer program.
As can be noticed the two model are partially overlapped complementing each
other. ￿e ￿rst two layers of Munzner’ s model in some extent are equivalent to the
acquire, ￿lter and mine stages in Fry’ s model. ￿e visual encoding and interaction
design corresponds to represent, re￿ne, and interact stages. Furthermore, Mun-
zner’s model adds an important step in modern visualization creation, which is
the development of adequate algorithms. Also, it is to note that the ￿rst layer of
Munzner’ s model in part overlaps with thecontext of the three cycle view of design
science research.
Figure ￿.￿ illustrates the methodology we followed in the research presented in
this thesis.
￿.￿. Evaluation
When validating contributions in the area of information visualization researches
have at their disposal a wide range of evaluation approaches (e.g., algorithm com-
plexity analysis, qualitative discussion of result images, user study). However, it
is not an easy task to choose appropriate validation approach, specially when
it comes to validate adaptive methods (see e.g., (Höök, ￿￿￿￿; Paramythis et al.,

Figure ￿.￿ Schematic representation of the instantiation of the ￿ree Cycle View on Design
Science Research with the integrated design models a￿er Fry ( ￿￿￿￿) and Munzner (￿￿￿￿).
￿￿￿￿)). Since adaptive system responds to user interaction, and at the same time,
the user is a￿ected by the adaptation processes, introducing subjective judgements,
it is hard to answer the question if the adaptation is relevant. As for evaluating a
general visualization contribution, Munzner (￿￿￿￿) proposes choosing validation
approach according to type of contribution, and presents a respective taxonomy.
￿e taxonomy consists of ￿ve types of contributions, which includes:
￿. Algorithm/technique– the works under this category focus on novel algorithms.
Typically, a contribution of this type should result in an algorithm/technique
that is faster, more scalable, or provides better visual quality than the state-of-the-
art ones. T o validate such contribution, usually, one would perform complexity
analysis, quantitative timing measurements, and a qualitative discussion of
the resultant images. Also, quantitative metrics of image quality can be also
appropriate (e.g., edge crossings in graph representations).
￿. Design study – the works under this category claim that a new visualization is a
suitable solution for a particular domain problem, providing enough informa-
tion about target domain and the methods used. Also, the design requirements
and visual encodings and interaction techniques that ful￿ll these requirements
should be stated. T o validate such contributions, typically, one would provide
arguments referring to perceptual principles and information visualization
theory. Also, a description of case studies or scenarios of use can also be appro-
priate. Finally, the process of a design study, in most cases, is iterative and uses
formative evaluation for re￿nement.
￿. Systems/T oolkits– the contributions under this category focus on the archi-
tectural design choices and abstractions in the creation of an infrastructure,
framework, or a toolkit. A discussion of lessons learned, along with the perfor-

￿￿ Research Methodology
mance, ￿exibility, and extensibility implications, would be a typical evaluation
of works under this category.
￿. Evaluation – the works under this category focus on how a information visual-
ization system or technique is used. An example of this kind of contribution
would be a most common approach in information visualization, which is know
as formal user study, which is conducted in laboratory settings, using sets of
tasks and measuring quantitative performance in terms of time and accuracy.
A typical validation would be proving that the tested tasks are valid, i.e., they
correspond to some extent to the tasks assumed by the target users in a target
domain.
￿. Model – the contributions under this category present formalisms of abstrac-
tions for designing or evaluating particular technique or system. ￿is category
is further subdivided into three subcategories. (i) T axonomycontributions pro-
pose categories that help researchers understand structure and organization
of the design space in some topic. (ii) Formalism contributions present new
models, de￿nitions, or terminology to describe techniques or phenomena. (iii)
Commentary contributions that presents an arguable opinion/position about
an issue.
It is natural that contributions can be of mixed type of work. For example, a
design study might include a novel algorithm as a secondary contribution. Due
to experimental nature of our work, the contributions presented in this thesis fall
in mixed type. ￿us, di￿erent evaluation approaches for each speci￿c work were
chosen. ￿is includes, quantitative metrics, qualitative discussion of result pictures
(we call it critical discussion), laboratory user study, design justi￿cation from
task analysis and case studies, and visual encoding justi￿cation from theoretical
principles.

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿–￿￿
Hexagonal Gridded Map
In retail business intelligence, and more speci￿cally in an analysis of customer-
supermarket relationship, factors, such as geographic location of customers, de-
mographic distribution, customers’ preferences, accessibility to the store are cru-
cial in decision-making tasks. Visualization is an important tool for analysis and
decision-making, which should provide means to make informed business deci-
sions. ￿e work presented in this chapter describes an approach to the hexagonal
gridded maps, which integrates diverse information layers along with adaptive
zooming. ￿ese layers are complementary, providing di￿erent points of view over
the same dataset and various levels of abstraction: starting with a dot map, which
portrays the impact of supermarket localization on customers’ choices, up to a
choropleth map, which depicts population density in an adaptive form depending
on the di￿erent granularity of administrative units. Ultimately, the visualization
model presented herein provides means to: (i) explore and analyze data regarding
customer-supermarket relations; (ii) reveal the impact of supermarkets localization
on customer preferences; (iii) suggest areas of low coverage by supermarkets.
￿.￿. Introduction
Visual exploration and graphical analysis tools are essential to reveal bene￿ts from
large, complex and diverse data volumes. Nowadays, in the age of Big data, the ￿eld
of information visualization provides valuable techniques and methods that enable
the users to increase their perception of the information contained in the data.
￿rough visualization, they can discover hidden relationships and patterns within
datasets, and therefore improve the practices of analysis and decision-making.
For retail companies – such as ￿￿￿￿￿, one of the biggest retail companies
in Portugal – the identi￿cation of potential sites to open new supermarkets is an
important decision. Factors, such as geographic location, demographic distribution,
customers’ habits, and accessibility, can a￿ect the choice for the potential site.
Experts in charge of these decisions need to enhance their capabilities with visual
tools to interact, explore and analyze the data, in order to ￿nd signi￿cant patterns,
trends and anomalies. In this case, one can rely on visualization techniques to
explore and analyze large and complex datasets, and therefore to make properly
informed decisions.
￿is work focuses on the dot distribution map technique that inherits some of
the properties of graduated symbol maps to display geo-referenced points in the

￿￿ Hexagonal Gridded Map
form of combined circles depicting di￿erent data values. In particular, our model
integrates the following:
• A hierarchical hexagonal grid to aggregate the geo-referenced points reducing
visual clutter and increasing readability of the map;
• A Choropleth map, to depict demographic distribution of the customers;
• Our ￿rst approach to distorted V oronoi diagrams, to de￿ne geographically
weighted boundaries of supermarkets’ accessibility.
￿ese techniques are presented in the form of layers of information, and their com-
bination provides diverse points of view over the dataset. Hence, the visualization
provides means to:
￿. Explore and analyze data regarding customer–supermarket relations in terms
of frequency of visits and consumption;
￿. Reveal the impact of supermarket locations on customer preferences;
￿. Identify areas of low supermarket coverage.
Other techniques, such as heatmaps and isopleth maps were previously explored,
however not included in this dissertation.
￿is section is followed by the related work section, where we give an overview
of existing works and visualization techniques that are close to the ones developed
and applied in presented work. ￿en, we detail our approach. In the last section
we present our ￿ndings using our visualization model.
￿.￿. Related Work and Background
As mentioned in Section ￿.￿.￿ there are di￿erent approaches to dot maps, which
already have been discussed. In this subsection, we consider mainly the techniques
to reduce visual clutter in dot maps.
￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿ere are di￿erent approaches to tackle the
visual clutter, mainly caused by point overlapping, commonly generated in this type
of visualization. A common approach in popular Geographic Information System
(￿￿￿) so￿ware is to randomly displace points around their original position or by
following a prede￿ned pattern – jittering (Hey and Bill, ￿￿￿￿). Other techniques
tackle overlapping issue by altering spatial arrangement of dots through the means
of distortion, and consequently providing additional space for the dots in dense
regions (Bak et al., ￿￿￿￿; Keim et al., ￿￿￿￿), or by reducing the number of dots
preserving speci￿c characteristics of data –re￿nement (Peters, ￿￿￿￿; Y an and W eibel,
￿￿￿￿).
￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ Modern visualization techniques ad-
dress the visual clutter by combining adjacent dots in a single graphical element
according to the spatial density distribution, referred to as aggregation (Birch et al.,
￿￿￿￿; Chua and Moere, ￿￿￿￿). ￿ere are various approaches to aggregate points in

￿.￿ ￿e Model of Hexagonal Map ￿￿
geographical space. ￿e work of Slingsby and Loon ( ￿￿￿￿) uses tiles to aggregate
data points, which also enable the representation to show the ratio of di￿erent
types of points. Additionally, the authors make direct connection of zoom levels to
density estimation scale, which provides a coherent transition between zoom layers.
Similarly, the work of Zhang et al. (￿￿￿￿) considers various scales of aggregation,
providing consistent thematic context while transitioning from di￿erent zoom
levels. Nowadays, it is common to use adaptive zooming techniques (Cecconi and
Galanda, ￿￿￿￿), this is, the granularity of the representation or the implantation
itself changes according to zoom level.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿ Whilst the majority of the works focus on the use of rectangular
grids to subdivide the space and to aggregate points (a.k.a. binning technique),
there are other less popular methods that rely on hexagonal binning, i.e., the subdi-
vision of space is done using a hexagonal grid and the data points are aggregated by
each hexagon. Hexagonal grid seems to have advantage in what regards spatial cov-
erage e￿ciency, visual clarity when used in visualization, match between distances
measured in grid and Euclidean units, and error in estimation of points distribu-
tion (Birch et al., ￿￿￿￿; Yfantis et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿e shape of hexagons resembles a
circle more than a square, thus having shorter perimeter, and consequently having
greater area coverage. Also, the average distance from the center of a hexagon is
shorter than of a square. In spite of the previously pointed advantages, it seems that
only a small percentage of works in the ￿eld of geovisualization use hexagonal grids
(￿ out of ￿￿ articles (Birch et al., ￿￿￿￿)). Examples are the work by Roth et al. (￿￿￿￿)
that uses hexagon binning in their Crime Analysis work, or the “hexagon mosaic”
maps used this technique to display the uni-variate and bi-variate geographical data
(Carr et al., ￿￿￿￿). Finally, another common characteristic of binning techniques
is that they are usually based on hierarchical space subdivision by recurring to
Quadtrees (Finkel and Bentley, ￿￿￿￿) or similar data structures, starting with a
square and recursively subdividing it until a certain condition is met.
￿.￿. ￿e Model of Hexagonal Map
￿is section starts by describing the dataset. ￿en, we proceed with the detailed
description of our approach for hexagonal binning, which is based on dot distribu-
tion, to depict the geographic distribution of the customers and their consumption
behavior. Along with the adaptive zooming, the map provides di￿erent levels of
abstraction. A series of supplementary graphical layers are then presented, allow-
ing the user to explore and analyze the data from di￿erent perspectives, such as
spatial coverage by supermarkets, population densities with di￿erent degrees of
granularity, and the potential customers routes to the nearest supermarket.
￿.￿.￿. Data
￿e datasets of the supermarket visits and consumption are retrieved from ￿￿￿
Portuguese supermarkets and hypermarkets of the ￿￿￿￿￿ chain, which covers the

￿￿ Hexagonal Gridded Map
entire country. ￿e consumptions are linked to the client through a client card
issued by house hold, which can be shared by the entire family. For this project
we used all the transactions made on those supermarkets and hypermarkets from
May ￿￿￿￿ to April ￿￿￿￿. Each transaction corresponds to one product bought and
it has several properties, such as, amount spent (in euros), date, time, and place of
purchase. ￿e data for this project were aggregated by month and were divided in
three datasets.
• ￿e ￿rst dataset contains the total amounts spent, and the number of visits to
the supermarkets for each customer.
• ￿e second dataset contains customer–supermarket pairs with the amounts
spent and visits to a particular supermarket.
• ￿e third one is composed by customer–supermarket pairs with the supermar-
ket preference score.
￿e preference score is calculated as follows:
score ij = Fij + Cij
D￿ , (￿.￿)
where Fij and Cij are the frequency of visits and the spent amount, respectively, by
customer i in the supermarket j. D is the number of days in the corresponding
period, which, in our case, is one month. In other words, we say that a customer
prefers to go to a supermarket if: (i) he/she spends little, but visits it frequently;
or (ii) he/she visits it once in a while, but spends a lot. W e normalize the score by
dividing the frequency and consumption by the squared number of days in the
corresponding period.
￿e second and third datasets contain information regarding the nearest su-
permarket in relation to the customers’ residence. ￿e computation of the nearest
supermarket was done in twofold: (i) through geometric distances – the nearest
supermarket in straight line; and (ii) computing distances by traversing the road
network – the nearest supermarket taking into account the road network topology.
A more detailed description is given in Section ￿.￿.￿.￿.
Finally, the demographic data that consists of population densities bymunicipal-
ity and civil parish was retrieved from pordata.pt (a Portuguese statistical database).
￿.￿.￿. Dot Distribution Map of Customers
In our visualizations, we used combined circles to represent the residence’ s location
of each customer residence. It is important to note that multiple customers can live
in the same location, which is more noticeable in urban areas. T o avoid overlapping,
the points su￿er a small random perturbation in space. Our goal was to understand:
(i) the relationship between consumption and frequency of visits to supermarkets;
(ii) if the customers consume in the nearest supermarkets and how frequently;
(iii) if the customers prefer to visit the nearest supermarkets or not. For that, we
converted continuous values into discrete ones by dividing the data into logical

￿.￿ ￿e Model of Hexagonal Map ￿￿
groups. ￿is conversion reduces the number of colors for encoding, simplifying
overall visualization, however, adding some degree of uncertainty.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Bivariate Color Space
￿e ￿rst dataset contains two variables, the total amount spent in all supermarkets,
and the total number of visits to all supermarkets per client during a given period.
T o encode these values in categories we project them onto a triangular bivariate
color space, in which the colors for the vertices were manually prede￿ned, while
the other ones are interpolated in runtime, as illustrated in Figure ￿.￿.F r o mt h e
geometric point of view this mapping is done as follows: ￿rst, the vector from top
vertex to consumption vertex is divided by the normalized consumption value;
then, the same is done for the vector towards the frequency vertex; ￿nally, by using
the ratio between the two values we can determine where the point falls between
the two vectors. I.e., let us consider the following example: the consumption and
frequency values are ￿￿￿ and ￿￿￿, which are marked with points A and B on the
Figure ￿.￿,r e s p e c t i v e l y .I nt h i sc a s e ,C will fall at the point ￿.￿￿ on the normalized
segment AB, since the ratio of ￿￿⁄￿￿is equal to ￿.￿ , the point will be dislocated ￿￿￿
from the center of the segment AB towards point B.
Figure ￿.￿ Discrete triangular color space. Red and cyan colors correspond to the upper
bound for consumption and frequency of visits, respectively. Y ellow color corresponds to
zero consumption and frequency of visits. Other colors are linearly interpolated. Points A,
B and C exemplify the projection of values to this color space.
Regarding the color scheme for the second and third datasets, these were de￿ned
as static. ￿e second dataset is encoded as follows: red represents customers that do
not purchase in the nearest supermarket; cyan represents customers that visit the
nearest supermarket one or less times per week; and yellow represents customers
that visit the nearest supermarket more than once per week. For the third dataset
we used cyan and red to depict customers that do and do not prefer consuming in
the nearest supermarkets, respectively.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Hexagonal Binning
Hexagonal binning is a process that produces hexagonal grids of variable resolution
to subdivide space according to the density of points in geographical space. Similar
to Quadtrees the hexagonal tree uses a hierarchical structure with hexagons on the
leaves and a composition of seven copies at higher levels of the tree. ￿e construc-

￿￿ Hexagonal Gridded Map
tion of the hexagonal tree and the insertion of points are more sophisticated when
compared to the Quadtrees. Since it is impossible to evenly subdivide a hexagon,
we employed an algorithm based on the simpli￿ed version of Snow￿ake fractal
(Barcellos, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e process proceeds in four successive steps:
￿. Start with the hexagon and recursively subdivide it until a certain level of depth;
￿. Compute the geometry of polygons at each level using bottom-up approach;
￿. Insert the data points into the tree;
￿. Merge branches that contains no points or the branches that contains the
number of points below a certain threshold.
With that said, the algorithm to construct the hexagonal tree proceeds as follows:
￿. start with a hexagon;
￿. make seven copies;
￿. scale each one by s = ￿√￿ ;
￿. rotate each one by α = arcsin(
√￿
￿×√￿ );
￿. translate six hexagons so that they surround exactly the central one.
￿e translation vectors for each newly created hexagon, except for the central
one, are computed by subtracting the locations of each vertex from the correspond-
ing vertex in the original hexagon (as shown in Figure ￿.￿). ￿e process repeats for
each newly created hexagon until a certain depth level is achieved (see Figure ￿.￿,
image on the right). Having generated the tree, the algorithm proceeds with the
calculation of the boundaries for each level of depth. W e recursively merge poly-
gons coming from currentlevel − ￿ using a simple union method – the edges that
form each polygon are removed if more than one edge coincide. As a consequence,
only the edges that form the boundary of the set of polygons remain (as shown in
Figure ￿.￿).
Figure ￿.￿ Illustration of the hexagonal spatial subdivision. Dashed lines show the initial
hexagon with seven hexagons that subdivide it (black lines). ￿e blue arrows represent
translation vectors. ￿e variation in the line thickness represents di￿erent levels of depth.
￿e insertion of points into the tree proceeds in the same way as in Quadtrees –
starting from the root and in each level testing a data point against its inclusion
in the polygons at that level up to the leaves. When all the points are inserted we
proceed with the merging stage. ￿e branches that contain no points or those that

￿.￿ ￿e Model of Hexagonal Map ￿￿
have the number of points below a certain threshold, are merged. In the end, the
space is subdivided as show in Figure ￿.￿.
Figure ￿.￿ Spatial subdivision using hexagonal binning. Red dots are geo-referenced points.
Line thickness of the boundaries corresponds to the level of depth in the tree.
￿e depth of the tree depends on the minimal size allowed for circles. T o be
able to distinguish color and size variation of the composite circles a minimal size
is prede￿ned. W e subdivide the tree until the size of the hexagons at the leaves is
close to, but not smaller than the minimal prede￿ned size. ￿en, the size of the
composite circles is readjusted according to the inner circle radius of the hexagons
in the leaves.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Composite Circles
￿e aggregation of points is performed in each cell of the grid and is represented by
composite circles. ￿e composite circles, located at the center of each cell, depict the
category of data points found in the corresponding cell. ￿e number of points per
category is encoded by the area of the corresponding circle, where the maximum
allowed size is calculated in the way mentioned earlier. ￿e order in which the
circles are drawn depends on the frequency of points of each category – starting
from the inner circle and ending in the outer circle, depicting high to low numbers,
respectively. Finally, we apply a color scheme depending on the dataset being
visualized (as shown in Figure ￿.￿).
￿.￿.￿.￿. Adaptive Zooming
Adaptive zooming is one of the key features of this work, due to the large amount
of data represented. ￿e adaptive zooming enables users to identify areas of interest
in higher levels of zoom, and to analyze these areas in more detail at low levels of
zoom. Also, due to the restriction for the minimal size of cells, the density estima-
tion can be inaccurate, i.e., the number of points can vary among cells. ￿is leads
to some degree of uncertainty in representation. ￿us, the map provides di￿erent
levels of abstraction and granularity depending on the zoom level. In particular,
the cells are scaled proportionally with the zoom level, providing di￿erent granu-

￿￿ Hexagonal Gridded Map
Figure ￿.￿ Comparison between the dot map (le￿) and our model (right).
larity of aggregation. Consequently, the variation in the number of points per cell
diminishes as the user zooms in, providing a more accurate representation at the
lower levels of zoom (see Figure ￿.￿).
With that said, adaptive zooming can be seen as a continuous transition from
an exploratory visualization, where the user searches for areas to analyze, to analyt-
ical visualization, where the user analyzes the data in an accurate representation.
Regarding the graphical error we considered the following metrics: maximum
number of points per cell; maximum and mean displacement error, which is the
distance in pixels from original location to the center of the corresponding cell.
For reference, the canvas size de￿ned in our visualization is equal to ￿￿￿￿x￿￿￿￿
pixels. Table ￿.￿ summarizes the e￿ect of the zoom over the graphical error.

￿.￿.￿. Graphical Layers
In this work, each layer enriches the visualization by providing additional graphical
tools. ￿e di￿erent layers depict di￿erent types of information, and have di￿erent
goals: (i) the identi￿cation of territorial domain boundaries and areas with low
supermarket’s coverage; (ii) the highlight of the customers that reside beyond a
certain distance from the nearest supermarket, allowing the identi￿cation of areas
that are poorly served; and (iii) the representation of the population densities
through a Choropleth map, providing the user with additional information about
the demographics.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Geometric and Geographic Boundaries
￿e aforementioned second and third datasets are used to reveal how the customers
behave in relation to the nearest supermarket. ￿e nearest supermarkets were
calculated in two ways: (i) using simple euclidean distances, and (ii) using the
topology of the road network (for the sake of simplicity further referred to as
geographic distance). Our study shows that using only the geometric distance is
not an optimal choice, due to an associated error. In real life, people are faced with
geographical obstacles, such as rivers, mountains, forests, etc. Hence, the nearest
supermarket is de￿ned as the one closest to the client, using the road network.
In the case of the euclidean distance, we computed territorial boundaries using
the V oronoi diagram technique (Aurenhammer,￿￿￿￿). By applying this technique
on the supermarket sites, we obtained closed areas bounded by the perpendicular
bisector of pairs of nearby supermarkets. ￿is bisector separates all the points
(customers) closer to one supermarket from the points closer to another. ￿ese
separators are used to mark territorial boundaries, delimiting the covered areas of
each supermarket (Figure ￿.￿).
In the case of the geographic distance, we calculated the nearest supermar-
kets for each client using the road network of Portugal. ￿e road network was
retrieved from OpenStreetMap, imported to a PostGIS database, and the topology

￿.￿ ￿e Model of Hexagonal Map ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿ Second dataset visualization along with the population density by civil parish,
using choropleth map technique.
was calculated using the pgRouting extension (Obe, ￿￿￿￿). W e found the near-
est supermarket for each customer using the Dijkstra algorithm provided by the
pgRouting extension. ￿e output of the previous step is a set of customers linked
to the corresponding closest supermarket.
T o determine the territorial boundaries for each supermarket, we propose
a technique for a weighted distortion of the V oronoi boundaries according to
geographical properties. Our ￿rst approach consists of three successive steps:
￿. Subdivide edges of the boundaries by smaller segments, with the size that was
empirically de￿ned, which depends on the resolution of the image;
￿. Each vertex in the diagram is attracted by the points that do not belong to the
area being computed, taking into account the geographic proximity between
costumer residence and supermarket. ￿e attraction force f is computed as:
f = a × log ￿ d+￿
b ￿, where a and b are the parameters that control the degree
and rate of distortion and d is the distance between vertex and the point. ￿e
parameters were empirically de￿ned and are equal to ￿.￿ and ￿.￿, respectively;
￿. ￿e vertices are pushed back to their original position using attached springs.
￿e push force s is computed as: s = k ∗ d, where k is a parameter that controls
the original shape, which is equals ￿.￿ in our case, and d is the distance between
the current and original location of the vertex. ￿e force is then applied over a
unitary vector pointing towards the original location.
W e consider the resultant forms as the supermarket territorial boundaries that
take into account the geographical proximity to the customers’ residence (see
Figure ￿.￿).
￿.￿.￿.￿. Population Density
￿e layer “below” the Dot map is a Choropleth map that depicts demographic
data. In particular, we considered population densities by municipalities and civil
parishes. Using gray scales, we mapped population densities as follows: the values
that are above a certain threshold are represented with black; and the values that

￿￿ Hexagonal Gridded Map
Figure ￿.￿ Close view of Grand Lisbon area. Visualization of the ￿rst dataset in a time
window of May ￿￿￿￿.
are below that threshold are linearly mapped to the shades of gray in the range [￿,
￿￿] percent. ￿is mapping ensures that even low values are visible on the map (as
shown in Figure ￿.￿). ￿e limitation of this approach is that when the Choropleth
map is positioned beneath the Dot map, some of the regions are hidden in the
visualization. So, users have to zoom-in to be able to read the map (see Figure ￿.￿).
￿.￿. Findings
In this section we describe four case studies. In particular, the analysis of super-
markets performance in terms of the relationship of customers’ consumption and
frequency of visits. ￿en we present one case of site suggestion to open a new super-
market, and also the impact of opening a new supermarket at the suggested area.
Additionally, we describe two cases of the impact of the distance to supermarkets
on the customers’ behavior.
￿.￿.￿. Case study ￿
For this case study we focused on the ￿rst dataset. ￿e crosses represent super-
markets, and color of each dot represents the relationship between frequency of
visits and consumption, in accordance with the triangular color scheme described
earlier in this chapter. Figure ￿.￿ illustrates a close view of the Lisbon area. As
can be observed, in the selected area there are almost all possible combinations of
customer behaviors. ￿ere are customers that spend a lot, but visit supermarkets
less frequently (indicated by the red color). In contrary, there are customers that
frequently visit supermarkets, but spent less (indicated by the cyan color). ￿is
pattern is clearly perceptible. ￿e customers that reside closer to the supermarkets
– clusters of cyan points – share this kind of behavior. Also, there is a strong correla-

￿.￿ Findings ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿ Coimbra district area. Illustration of the case of low customer visits to the
nearest supermarket, or no visits at all during the May ￿￿￿￿.
tion with rich neighborhoods, such as Alcochete, Oeiras, among others. Strangely,
Cascais, which is one of the most richest neighborhoods, presents randomness in
the customers behavior. On the contrary, there are customers, mostly in downtown
area of Lisbon, that consume and visit supermarkets at lower rates (indicated by
yellowish colors).
￿.￿.￿. Case study ￿
￿e second case attempts demonstrating the adequacy of our approach in iden-
tifying potential sites to open new stores, which is an important task in decision-
making chain. Its is also important to note that an onsite analysis of potential
locations has huge associated costs. With that said, let us consider the example of
Figure ￿.￿. For this case study we used the second dataset, which contains informa-
tion about the frequency of visits. Intuitively users can identify a big cluster of red
circles located in Figueira da Foz. Recall that the red color indicates that customers
do not consume in the nearest supermarket. From the V oronoi diagram one can
see that the nearest supermarket is located in Cantanhede. However, it turned out
that customers consume mostly in Coimbra city, which is one of the biggest cities
in that region. Nevertheless, Figueira da Foz seems to be an optimal location to
open new supermarket.
As can be observed, this kind of information can also be inferred from the
visualization. ￿e intersection of the regions generated by the distorted V oronoi
diagram suggests that this is a potential area to open a new supermarket. At least,
this is the farthest point from all the nearest supermarkets. Later, we discovered
that a new supermarket had been opened in October ￿￿￿￿ in Figueira da Foz. It
is important to note that the available data do not cover this period. As such, the
ability to identify this promising location can be seen as a demonstration of the
adequacy of the visualization approach. Unfortunately, due to lack of data, we
were unable to visualize the impact of the opening of this store and analyze its

￿￿ Hexagonal Gridded Map
performance, which would further demonstrate the validity of our approach. In
the following subsection we consider one of such cases.
￿.￿.￿. Case study ￿
For this case study we used the second dataset, since it better re￿ects on the
customers’ reaction to the opening of new supermarkets. Figure￿.￿￿ illustrates the
impact on the opening of a new supermarket in the city of Porto. As can be observed
in Figure ￿.￿￿, the image on the top, there is a cluster of red points that stands out.
￿is means that there are customers that for some reason have not consumed in
the nearest supermarkets during May ￿￿￿￿. Later, in the end of June ￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿
opened a new supermarket precisely in the area indicated by the diagram. ￿e
resulting e￿ect of the customers’ behavior can be perceived in Figure￿.￿￿, the image
on the bottom. As can be observed the pattern represents a more homogeneous
distribution of customers, without obvious separation between the areas of low
frequency of visits. ￿e remaining red circles may have to do with the accessibility
to the supermarket. Nevertheless, there is a large number of points that changed
to cyan, which is the indicator that these customers visit the supermarket one or
less times per week.
Figure ￿.￿￿ Visualization of the impact of opening of new supermarket. Second dataset
visualization of the time periods May ￿￿￿￿ (image on the top) and June ￿￿￿￿ (image on the
bottom).

￿.￿ Findings ￿￿
Figure ￿.￿￿ Visualization of the negative impact of distance.
One can notice the following typical pattern – the customers that reside close to
supermarkets are colored with yellow, indicating that these are frequent customers;
on the contrary, the customers residing further from supermarket visit them less
frequently, which is indicated by the cyan color. However, this pattern was not
perceptible with the newly opened supermarket. Mainly, this is due to the fact that
the supermarket opened at the end of the month, and it is natural that during that
time period, on average, people have not been able to visit the supermarket more
than one time per week. Also, customers are usually accustomed to their typical
places of purchase, and the transition to the newly opened supermarket takes time.
￿.￿.￿. Case study ￿
For this case study we used the third dataset, which allowed us to understand the
customers’ preferences. One of such cases is presented in Figure￿.￿￿. ￿is image
captures the customers that reside near the intersection of three districts – Coimbra,
Viseu and Guarda. ￿ese areas are mostly covered with mountains. Nevertheless, a
signi￿cant number of customers appears to live in this area. As it can be observed,
these clients are colored in red, which means that they do not prefer to shop in
the nearest supermarket. Ultimately, this can be veri￿ed by looking at the V oronoi
diagram. As turned out, ￿￿￿￿￿ has recently opened a new supermarket in that
area. Again, we were unable to visualize the impact of the new store opening, since
our data do not cover this period.
In some cases, big distances do not have impact over the customers preferences.
For instance, consider the example of Figure ￿.￿￿. ￿is is the south part of Portugal,
which is deserti￿ed and ￿at. As can be seen, a considerable number of customers
live there, and they are spread around a small city, where a supermarket is located.
Also, it can be seen that the majority of these people reside far away from that
supermarket, which is the nearest one. As we saw previously, customers that live
that far way, in general prefer not to shop in the nearest supermarkets, and logically
they probably shop in the supermarkets of concurrent companies. However, the

￿￿ Hexagonal Gridded Map
Figure ￿.￿￿ Visualization of the impact of distance.
cyan color indicates that the customers from this area prefer consuming in that
speci￿c supermarket, which can also indicate the lack of competition.
￿.￿. Chapter Summary
In this chapter we described a uni￿ed visualization approach, which provides di￿er-
ent perspectives over the same dataset. ￿e goal of this visualization is to provide
the user with su￿cient visual tools to learn from data and to make a more informed
decisions. ￿is work started by retrieving data regarding the consumptions in the
supermarkets of the biggest retailing company in Portugal. In order to get valuable
information the data were aggregated by months, and the amounts spent and the
frequency of visits of each pair customer–supermarket was computed. ￿is data
were separated in three datasets: (i) total consumptions and number of visits; (ii)
frequency of visits to the nearest supermarket; (iii) customers’ preference in regard
to the nearest supermarket. Additionally, the nearest supermarkets were calculated
using Euclidean distance and the distance traversing a road network.
All the three datasets are visualized using our approach, where each composite
circle represents data using hexagonal binning. ￿e color is used to encode di￿erent
categories, through the use of the same primary colors. Moreover, the methods to
encode each dataset are distinct. In the case of the ￿rst dataset values are projected
onto the bivariate triangular color scheme, converting continuous values into
discrete ones. ￿e second dataset is encoded using three colors, and the third one
uses only two colors. Apart from that, we described di￿erent graphical layers that
enable users to get additional insights, such as the types of consumption behaviors,
identi￿cation of poorly served areas, impact of distance, among others.
In regard to the visual clarity and e￿ciency of the visualization we presented an
overlap free method that reduces visual clutter and enables the user to easily identify
areas of interest. ￿is method relies on adaptive zooming, which consists of di￿erent
levels of abstraction that facilitate the reading of the map, despite adding some

￿.￿ Chapter Summary ￿￿
degree of graphical error. Additionally, the method presented provides continuous
zooming with smooth transitions among di￿erent levels of abstraction.
￿e case studies exemplify the use of the visualization in a real situation and the
types of ￿ndings that can be discovered and analyzed. As was seen, this visualization
helps to understand the relationship between consumption and frequency of visits,
and reveals the patterns of consumption behaviors that are closely related with
demography. Also, this visualization helps to analyze if the customers consume in
the nearest supermarkets and how frequently, or if the customers prefer consuming
in the nearest supermarkets or not. Additionally, this visualization enables the user
to analyze the impact of opening a new supermarket and the distance from nearby
stores. However, the most important point is that this visualization helps users to
take an informed decision of where it is advisable to open new supermarkets.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e visualization model presented in this chapter includes
several innovative contributions, namely:
• A novel approach to hexagonal mapping/binning (see algorithm presented in
Section ￿.￿.￿.￿). Our algorithm can be used as described in this chapter: (i) to
provide hierarchical spatial structure; and (ii) to provide geometric description
of bins at their corresponding level. Additionally, our algorithm can be applied
in (iii) spacial subpartition, and/or (iv) as an alternative to Quadtrees or similar
spatial structures.
• A novel method for adapting V oronoi diagrams to geographic features (see
algorithm presented in Section￿.￿.￿.￿), which in this work was used to determine
territorial boundaries of supermarkets.
• An approach for adaptive zooming and the study of the e￿ect of the resulting
graphical error.
• An approach for dynamic adjustment of granularity in Choropleth maps, which
is controlled by adaptive zooming.

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿–￿￿￿
Smart City Portrayal
In the modern world big cities face the demand for high urban mobility, which
has implications at diverse levels of ecology and economy. T o address these is-
sues urban and transportation planners apply an integrated approach, involving
diverse areas of computer science. ￿e use of data analytic tools is a common
and regular practice among specialists in the area. ￿e methods and techniques
provided by machine learning and information visualization take a predominant
role in helping to understand how cities are used by their inhabitants. Scientists
and practitioners created diverse solutions to represent information in dynamic
and interactive forms, allowing users to explore and analyze urban data faster and
more e￿ciently in comparison to traditional approaches. However, in dynamic
environments the changes in data (e.g., null values, new ￿elds in streamed data,
etc.) and user’ s manipulations (e.g., map zoom, ￿lter toggling, etc.) can a￿ect the
context of the visualization, therefore leading to ine￿cient graphical represen-
tations. In these cases, the visualization should adapt automatically in order to
provide coherent and e￿cient visual representations to the user. ￿e work de-
scribed in this chapter studies adaptive graphical representation in the context of
the visualization of urban mobility, exploring di￿erent situations of contextual
changes. More speci￿cally, adaptive visualization techniques are applied to depict
the use of public transportation, which expresses the movement of inhabitants in
the city throughout the day.
￿.￿. Introduction
T oday, ￿￿￿ of the world’ s population lives in urban areas and by ￿￿￿￿ this number
is expected to reach ￿￿￿ (Habitat, ￿￿￿￿). ￿ese areas are currently faced with
the challenges of growing car ownership, vehicle travel, and energy consumption.
￿ese issues are usually addressed by an integrated approach, involving energy,
transportation and Information and Communications T echnology (￿￿￿).
Transportation systems are not only a key factor for economic sustainability
and social welfare, but also a key dimension in the smart city agenda. A smart
city is a place where the traditional networks and services are made more e￿cient
with the use of digital and telecommunication technologies, for the bene￿t of its
inhabitants and businesses. ￿e wide deployment of pervasive computing devices
(smartphones, Global Positioning System (￿￿￿) devices, digital cameras, etc.), in-
creasing storage and processing capacity of computing, improvements in sensing

￿￿ Smart City Portrayal
and modeling capabilities, Internet of ￿ings (IoT), near ￿eld communications
or social media, just to name a few, o￿er opportunities for digital transformation
in cities. ￿ere is an opportunity to use ubiquitous urban sensing, big data, and
analytics to better understand the real-time functioning of the cities, as well as to
collect data necessary to estimate and calibrate the mobility and energy consump-
tion models, providing powerful tools, which can be useful in several contexts like
economical, social, and public policy. Smart transportation management systems
should use technology and collect information about mobility patterns. Under-
standing personal travel patterns and modeling travel demand is essential to plan
sustainable urban transportation systems to ful￿ll citizens’ mobility needs. T o do
this e￿ectively and timely, urban and transportation planners need a dynamic way
to pro￿le the movement of people and vehicles.
Pro￿ling of urban movements has traditionally relied on the knowledge of land
use patterns, but, while land use and transportation infrastructures tend to remain
in the same form for a long time once they are put in place, urban movements
o￿en change. Transport planning input information mostly comes from traditional
survey methods that are expensive and time consuming, giving planners only a
picture of what has happened and needing an active involvement. In contrast, the
wide deployment of pervasive computing devices and transport system records (e.g.,
ticket validation counts; tra￿c counts) provides unprecedented digital footprints,
telling where and when people are, possibly in real time, and enabling dynamic
mobility pro￿ling. For instance, Trépanier et al. (￿￿￿￿) present a model to estimate
the destination location for each individual boarding using the smart card data.
More recently, Ma et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) developed an e￿cient data mining method to
demonstrate the temporal travel patterns for public transport riders in Beijing. With
millions of users around the world, transportation researchers have also realized the
potential of Social Network Analysis (￿￿￿) for travel demand modeling and analysis
(Carrasco et al., ￿￿￿￿). All these mobility data, together with modern techniques
for geo-processing, ￿￿￿, data fusion and visualization o￿er new possibilities for
deriving activity destinations and allowing us to link cyber and physical activities
through user interactions, in ways that can be usefully incorporated into models
of land use and transportation interactions. Mining individuals’ mobility patterns
is an emergent research area (Lin and Hsu, ￿￿￿￿) for predicting future movements
or destinations (Calabrese et al., ￿￿￿￿), tra￿c forecast (Krings et al., ￿￿￿￿) or city
planning (Makse et al., ￿￿￿￿), for instance.
￿e ￿eld of information visualization provides methods and techniques that
enable the exploration and analysis of geo-referenced data. For instance, graduated
symbol maps, or transit maps, just to name a few, are modern visualization tech-
niques that can e￿ciently be used for visual analysis of spatial data (Slocum, ￿￿￿￿).
Most of the existing visualization methods are well-studied and proven approaches
to speci￿c data analysis tasks. However, in many situations – e.g., when multiple
visualization techniques are applied simultaneously – con￿icts may arise: visual
clutter resulting from overlapping of elements; inconsistency of representation
in di￿erent map layouts; etc. Moreover, this problem increases when individual

￿.￿ Related Work and Background ￿￿
techniques are applied in the form of static layers, creating additional ine￿ciency,
which in dynamic environments can vary from simple point superposition to
incompatibility of the map layouts (e.g., schematic versus exact map). Further-
more, the general visual depiction of spatiotemporal data is still a challenging
problem, which has been addressed by many researchers of the ￿eld (Andrienko
and Andrienko, ￿￿￿￿a).
￿erefore, the work presented herein focuses on multiple aspects of integrating
di￿erent visual techniques within a dynamic environment, attempting to achieve
following goals:
• Visualization layers should complement each other graphically and semanti-
cally, i.e., no redundant information should be presented by di￿erent layers of
graphics (semantic), and no con￿icting graphical elements should appear on
di￿erent layers (e.g., using circles on multiple layers to encode di￿erent data).
• ￿e visualization should provide multiple perspectives over the same data and
di￿erent ways of reading it (mainly trough the means of generalization).
• ￿e visualization should comprise in overall a reduced redundant information
and the number of graphical elements.
W e implemented an interactive application, which has been developed taking
into account general perceptual and cognitive properties of the graphic system.
￿e proposed approach is based on the principles described by Munzner ( ￿￿￿￿)–
identify and analyze the real world problem; design a visualization system, validate
the results, and re￿ect upon lessons learned. Our interactive application runs in real-
time, providing means to observe changes in daily usage of the metro network from
di￿erent perspectives (e.g., detection of overloaded areas of the network, analysis
of the usage of individual metro stations, observation of the daily patterns of entire
network, etc.). W e showcase the application with the quantitative spatiotemporal
data related to the metro stations of London – statistical information regarding the
counts of passengers (e.g., entries and exits per station), aggregated by ￿￿ minutes
intervals.
In the following section we overview background work. ￿en we detail our
model for point representation, starting with the concept, then color coding, and
then proceed with implementation. Next, the map transformations, such as map
morphing and linking line displacement, are detailed. ￿is is followed by a descrip-
tion of the graphical user interface. Finally, we provide a critical discussion about
the visualization and draw some conclusions.
￿.￿. Related Work and Background
Taking into account the nature of the data at hand, and the modern techniques
to visualize it, we considered the following topics related to our work – dynamic
visualization, graduated symbol and transit maps, which have already been dis-
cussed in Chapters ￿ and ￿, as well as circular time series visualization, heatmaps,
and geographic generalization, which follow in this section.

￿￿ Smart City Portrayal
￿.￿.￿. Geographic Generalization
In geographic visualization, one of the used techniques to represent high density
data is data abstraction, which is in some sense similar to geographic generalization
(Bertin, ￿￿￿￿, p. ￿￿￿). Due to limitations of human perception certain graphical
elements are not distinguishable at higher levels of geographic zoom ￿.I nt h i sc a s e ,
instead of direct representation, the concepts are generalized and then these are
rendered with appropriate graphical elements. For instance, the work of Kim et al.
(￿￿￿￿) focuses on a multivariate abstraction for representing spatiotemporal data.
￿e data is estimated from a set of spatiotemporal events and visualized by means
of bristled lines of the underlying vector map.
In some cases abstraction is achieved through distortion of the representation.
In the work of Sips et al. ( ￿￿￿￿), abstraction results in the distortion of underlying
map for the purpose of decreasing point overlapping. ￿e higher the abstraction,
the lower the level of overlapping. In contrast, distortion can be used as a mean
to represent the data. For instance in the SubMap project the vector maps of
Budapest and Finland are distorted in response to high biking and twitter activity,
respectively (Feles et al., ￿￿￿￿). In the context of our work, we prefer the idea of
exaggeration to distortion. As will be described later in this chapter, our approach
does not distort the map, but exaggerates the representation highlighting the areas
of attention, which are derived from the data.
￿.￿.￿. Heatmap
Similar to the visualization technique known as Heatmap (Slocum, ￿￿￿￿) our
approach is based on the idea of pseudo-elevation, this is, using color to represent
a third dimension. In the case of heatmap, the elevation, which encodes the density
of points as a rule, is represented by the gradient from green to red meaning
low and high elevation, respectively. ￿is technique is an e￿cient approach to
visualize spatial data density, thanks to the exaggeration that creates clear and
distinguishable color ramps. ￿e heatmap technique was successfully applied in
the work of Fisher (￿￿￿￿). In his article the author presents Hotmap, which uses a
heatmap to represent aggregate activity of the use of Microso￿’ s Live Search Map.
￿e use of elevation to represent spatial data for the purpose of urban mobility
studies can be encountered in diverse applications. For instance, in the project
Real Time Rome (Calabrese and Ratti, ￿￿￿￿) the cellphone activity was visualized
by dividing the Rome urban area by a modular grid and by assigning a telecommu-
nication tra￿c intensity to the elevation of each cell. ￿e visual output is a ￿D layer
over the urban space that emphasizes the areas of attention similarly to heatmaps.
￿ In the context of this work three levels of zoom are de￿ned as follows: the general view – in this view
all the considered geographic area is visible on the screen; the elementary views – in this views all
the details are visible and distinguishable (e.g., streets, houses, etc.); and the intermediate views – as
the name suggests, are the views that lay between the general and the elementary views.

￿.￿ Point Representation: Design Study ￿￿
￿.￿.￿. Circular Plot
￿ere are di￿erent visual methods and techniques to analyze and characterize
time-oriented data (see e.g., Aigner (￿￿￿￿), Aigner et al. (￿￿￿￿), Chick et al. (￿￿￿￿),
and Silva and Catarci ( ￿￿￿￿)). ￿e main purpose of these techniques is to de-
tect periodic behavior, compare changes in continuous data to identify patterns,
similarities and exceptions. Due to the nature of our dataset, which contains rep-
resentative days of metro usage separately for weekdays and weekends, we focus
on the methods to depict cyclic time. ￿ese methods can be divided in two main
groups – representation in circle and representation using spirally shaped axis. ￿e
￿rst method is usually applied when there is only one full cycle or when there are
multiple attributes in the time series. In the second case the data attributes are
mapped along the circumference and the time evolves from the center to the edge
of the circle (Keim et al., ￿￿￿￿).
Regarding the representation of time-oriented data on spirals, the work of
W eber et al. (￿￿￿￿), called Spiral Graph, depicts cyclic features of data by using
a spirally shaped time axis, which clearly shows cyclical patterns when the cycle
length is correctly aligned. ￿is technique was further improved by applying two-
tone pseudo coloring, which yields to a more accurate reading of the data (T ominski
and Schumann, ￿￿￿￿). Also, time-oriented data can be visualized through the use
of a swarm system applied on a spiral axis and with an expressive visual language to
better convey information, invoke memorability of the graph, and visually engage
the end user (Maçãs et al., ￿￿￿￿b). Finally, interactive techniques can be used along
with the spiral graphs, allowing the user to analyze a speci￿c period of the time in
more detail (Carlis and Konstan, ￿￿￿￿; T ominski and Schumann,￿￿￿￿).
￿.￿. Point Representation: Design Study
In our work a simple visualization technique as point representation is revisited
and expanded, taking into consideration current hardware, algorithms and data
availability, and applied in the context of urban mobility. More precisely, we recon-
sidered a point representation of spatial data, where the quantities are encoded
with the size of circles. Due to spatial data heterogeneity the point representation
generates a large amount of overlapping points. ￿us, the problem increases with
amount of data, resulting in high degrees of visual clutter, particularly in the gen-
eral views of the map. T o tackle this problem we created an algorithm based on the
Metaball algorithm, and implemented it using OpenGL Shading Language ( ￿￿￿￿),
to take full advantage of hardware acceleration. ￿e model reacts to changes in
zoom and transforms its representation accordingly, providing di￿erent degrees of
graphical accuracy and modes of interpreting the visualization.
In the reminder we proceed with the concept de￿nition, describing the under-
lying model, and then proceed with the formulation. ￿e following sub-section
describes the color coding for our model, with further formulation of the algorithm
and implementation of the model based on hardware acceleration.

￿￿ Smart City Portrayal
￿.￿.￿. Concept and Formalization
In order to compare the proposed technique with a canonical point representation
we started with the direct representation of data. ￿e points were rendered by
circular graphical elements with variable area. Each circle encodes the di￿erence
between entries and exists in the London underground metro at a given time
interval. Positive and negative values are encoded with the red and green colors,
respectively. As expected the dense areas contain numerous overlapping points that
generate unwanted visual clutter. In the context of data exploration and analysis
interaction techniques such as zooming and panning, are used to amplify and
navigate in the geographical space. ￿ese techniques allow the user to amplify
highly dense areas and to analyze them in detail using closer views. However, the
amount of visual clutter may be inappropriate for analytic and communication
purposes.
As previously mentioned, a heatmap applied in geographical context enables
the representation of high density data revealing the areas of interest. Moreover,
this technique is e￿cient in conveying spatial information, although exaggerating
its visual representation. ￿e underlying idea for our approach was based on
the visual exaggeration of the data in the general views and the rigorous point
representation in zoomed views. ￿e combination of both features enables an
e￿cient representation of high density data in all views, whilst keeping the same
graphical elements and the same visual language throughout the views.
In order to combine the two graphical representations with the interactive
aspects, the variable and constant parts of the representation were established. On
the one hand, the general view must present strong detection and assembly char-
acteristics, i.e., graphical elements should be displayed in a way that allows them
to be easily detected and visually combined in meaningful groups. Exaggeration
causes important elements to be distinct from other ones, revealing patterns and
emphasizing important parts of the data. On the other hand, the zoomed views
must have e￿cient visual estimation characteristics, i.e., the graphical elements
must rigorously correspond to the data they encode. ￿is allows the user to analyze
the data in a precise manner. Following the principles identi￿ed by Hall et al. (￿￿￿￿)
exaggeration, zooming and map transformations, which will be covered later, are
used to create emphasis in visualization, in order to draw user’s attention to the
areas of interest.
With this mindset, we employed an algorithm that generates visual artifacts
with pseudo-elevation characteristics, similarly to heatmaps. More precisely, a
variation of Metaball algorithm, also known as blob algorithm, was applied (Blinn,
￿￿￿￿). Simply put, this consists of a mountain-like surface where only the parts that
are above a given threshold are rendered. In other words, the metaball approach
de￿nes a function, in our case in two-dimensional space, which takes as input x
and y coordinates of a point, and outputs a value. ￿en, the value becomes ￿ if
above a de￿ned threshold or ￿ if below. One of the common functions to de￿ne
an underlying surface is a magnetic ￿eld equation ￿￿d￿, where d is the distance
between the given point and the point of the surface. One of the properties of this

￿.￿ Point Representation: Design Study ￿￿
function is that the output never equals ￿, meaning that every surface point is
in￿uenced by every given point, and the points of the surface closer to input points
have bigger elevation (as illustrated in Figure ￿.￿).
