By comparing the Metaball output with the point representation (see Figure
￿.￿(b)) the following can be observed: the areas with high point density generate
an elevated degree of positive exaggeration, while the areas with a small number
of points generate negative exaggeration. By positive and negative exaggeration we

￿￿￿ Smart City Portrayal
mean the following: the areas that encode a given value, sometimes referred to as
a meaningful area, are bigger or smaller than expected; while positive exaggera-
tion overestimates the data highlighting the areas with high deviations, negative
exaggeration leads to underestimation of data, thus, hiding information. In order
to generate solely positive exaggeration, or no exaggeration at all, the points that
are located inside the circles should have a value of pseudo-elevation greater than
the de￿ned threshold. In this case the total meaningful area created by Metaball
algorithm would be equal or bigger than the one of the point representation. With
that said, the elevation valueV of each point of the surface p is computed as follows:

where r is the expected radius of the blob, c is a given point and b is a constant that
controls the slope of the fall-o￿ function.
By controlling the slope of the fall-o￿ function the amount of exaggeration
can be changed, this is, how much the meaningful area is bigger than expected.
￿is parameter also establishes a relation between precision/detail and expressive-
ness/abstraction of the representation. For instance, supposing that the users want
to visualize changes in the overall pattern without much detail, they would choose a
lower value for b. Figure ￿.￿ exempli￿es the visual outcome resulted form the equa-
tion ￿.￿ for di￿erent values of b. As de￿ned in Section ￿.￿.￿, b ∈ [￿.￿, ￿.￿ ] presents
a good trade-of between exaggeration and accurate organic-looking visualization.
￿.￿.￿. Color-coding
￿is model comprises two types of colors – primary and secondary. ￿e primary
colors are red and green, and they encode the sign of the given data values. Positive
and negative values are represented by red and green colors, respectively. ￿e
secondary color is the yellow, and it emerges from the blend of elements with
primary colors (see Figure ￿.￿). ￿e underlying idea for using secondary color
comes from the problem of circle overlapping. ￿at is, when two circles of the
same size painted with di￿erent colors are superposed, we rather paint with the
secondary color, which clearly indicates that elements that represent di￿erent
categorical values exist. ￿e core idea is to make a direct blend of primary colors,
in particular their red, green and blue components. Note that the blue color is
not used in our model, and since color is composed by all three components, we
simply set the blue to zero. A formal description of how this is accomplished is
presented in the next subsection.
￿.￿.￿. Implementation
￿e complexity of Algorithm ￿.￿ is O(nm), where n is the number of given points
and m is the number of points on the surface, which may lead to performance
issues. For that reason, we resorted to hardware acceleration, enabling the visu-
alization to run in real-time. In order to reduce the number of communications

￿.￿.￿. Graphical Error
Graphical error is expressed as the di￿erence between the area occupied by the ren-
dered circles of the actual data and the area rendered by the Metaball algorithm. In
other words, it is the additional pixels that create the trade-o￿ between the exagger-
ation described above and the accurate representation. In order to understand how
the fall-o￿ function and dynamic zoom actually a￿ect exaggeration, we measured
the graphical error as a function of both components. ￿e graphical error itself
was computed as Ep￿Cp − ￿, where Ep and Cp are the number of pixels painted by
Metaball algorithm and covered by the circles, respectively. Since we consider the
total area covered by circles as ground-truth (i.e., meaningful area), we take the
proportion of metaball pixels over the area covered by circles. Also, we subtract
one to get the error and not the actual proportion. ￿e plot of graphical error as
a function of zoom scale and fall-o￿ value is shown in Figure ￿.￿. Additionally,
we de￿ned a safe margin for the allowed graphical error, which does not a￿ect
the perception of the data, neither the accuracy of the representation. Empirically
de￿ned, a safe margin in the range of [￿.￿, ￿.￿￿ ] yields the fall-o￿ values between
￿.￿ and ￿.￿ . As can be observed from the ￿gure, the higher graphical errors occur
at lower levels of zoom, hence the de￿nition of the interval for the fall-o￿ function.
Also, one can notice that the error fall below zero, which means that at higher levels
of zoom and/or when applying high values for fall-o￿ function the area painted by

￿.￿. Map Transformations
￿e second core feature of this work is the transformations applied to the metro
map in real time and dynamic environment. ￿ese transformations are:
￿. transitions between two map layouts – schematic and georeferenced metro
maps;
￿. changes in line displacement in proportion of zoom scale;
￿. changes in data scaling as a function of map zooming and panning.
In the context of dynamic visualization animated transitions are a common
practice nowadays (Heer and Robertson, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e changes in the visualization

presented in an animated form facilitate their perception, therefore increasing the
e￿ectiveness of the representation (Robertson et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿e need for smooth
transformation increases when multiple layouts of representation exist. In our case,
the user should clearly perceive and correlate the location of stations in both map
layouts, in order to be able to keep track of changes in data regardless of the zoom
level. ￿e reminder of this section describes map morphing, edge displacement
and the reactive data scaling.
￿.￿.￿. Map Morphing
In the case of the metro map, morphing consists of transiting from one map style
(schematic map) to another (georeferenced map) and vice-versa. Similar to the
conventional morphing applied in computer graphics, where each vertex of object
A is mapped to the corresponding vertex of object B, we mapped stations and
linking connections of both maps. In this section we begin by describing the map
layout, and proceed with the description of the mapping techniques.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Metro Map Layout
Due to their natures the schematic and real metro maps are stored in di￿erent
formats. Additionally, the common components, such as stops, lines and connec-
tions, are stored in separate ￿les. ￿e schematic map is a direct transcription of the
existing underground map. W e developed an algorithm that parses the Scalable
V ector Graphics (￿￿￿) image of the map, and stores it in a unique ￿le with the
following structure:

• Stops – contain ID and coordinates in screen space;
• Curves – describe curved connections of the map in the form of ID of the stops
they connect and the coordinates of the vertices of the polygonal lien;
• Lines – contain ID and a set of coordinates that describe each metro line in
screen space.
Finally, the ￿les stations and connections are structured according to a tradi-
tional directional graph representation. Stations contain information regarding
the ID, coordinates, in our case in geographic space, and additional information.
Connections contain information about origin, target stop and the metro line
number. ￿e renderings of both maps are presented in Figure ￿.￿.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Station-to-Station Transformation
In order to make the seamless transition between two maps perceptible, we applied
a linear interpolation strategy. Since both maps lay in di￿erent coordinate spaces,
the very ￿rst step is to align bounding boxes, origin axis, and the scale. Both
bounding boxes were aligned empirically in the center of the town, as this is the
most dense area. Once the bounding were boxes aligned, the transition between
stations of both maps is performed using a linear interpolation of each of the
components of the coordinates. ￿e parameter, alpha value, that controls the
transformation lies in the range [￿, ￿], and is bound to a certain zoom range. In
other words, consider the range [zi , z f ], which corresponds to the zooming scale,
where zi is totally zoomed-out map, and z f is totally zoomed-in map. ￿e alpha
value is mapped to the interval [zi + s, z f − e], where s and e correspond to the
o￿sets at which the transition begins and ends, respectively, andzi + s < z f − e.￿ e
presentation of actual values is unreasonable, since we used an arbitrary relative
scale for the zoom levels.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Connection-to-Connection Transformation
As can be observed in Figure ￿.￿(a), in schematic representation in some cases the
inter-station segments are drawn in the form of a polygonal line. It is inevitable to
end-up in such drawing style, because of the rule of ￿°, ￿￿°and ￿￿°angles, and the
equidistant stations placement. ￿us, the transition between the straight lines that
depict inter-station segments of the real metro map and the polygonal lines of the
schematic map becomes challenging. In order to solve this problem, we proceed
with the following strategy for each inter-station connectionRM = {c￿ , c￿ ,..., cn}

the polygonal line pli = {v￿ , v￿ ,..., vn}, which result in second set of vertices
ci = {v￿ , v￿ ,..., vn}. Consider the example of Figure ￿.￿.I nt h i sc a s ew eh a v e

in schematic map and real map, respectively.

￿.￿.￿. Splitting Overlapped Segments
￿e georeferenced network presents a particular issue, which comes from the
nature of data. ￿is issue is the overlapping of segments that connect pairs of
stations that share multiple metro lines. Moreover, the dataset does not describe
the exact location of connecting lines, which is dictated solely by the geographical
locations of stations, causing them to overlap when representing them graphically.
T o overcome this issue the following approach was developed. W e compute
corresponding render locations for each station in several steps:

￿. Find and group adjacent segments, identi￿ed by the stations they connect;
￿. Distribute them evenly along the perpendicular to a common axis;
￿. Center them on that axis, which connects both stations location;
￿e order by which the segments are distributed is dictated by the number of
the metro line they represent. It is to note that the order direction is not impor-
tant, although the same ordering within adjacent segments should be retained
to preserve continuous representation of the segments that belong to the same
line. Finally, the displacement o￿set corresponds to a ￿xed thickness of rendered
line ( fi x e d W i d t h), such that each segment is rendered right next to each other
without any gaps. ￿e result of the application of this approach is illustrated in
Figure ￿.￿.

shown by the pseudo-code in Algorithm￿.￿. ￿e function getA djacentConnections ()
proceeds with sorting the array of connections, comparing the pairs of IDs of sta-

that connect the same pair of stations. ￿e #            —of fset is used to compute the displace-

￿￿￿ Smart City Portrayal
ment of each segment. Finally, the ￿p, with a magnitude equal to half of the sum
of the widths of the segments within each group, de￿nes starting locations from
which each segment is displaced (as shown in Figure ￿.￿).
￿.￿.￿. Reactive Circle Scaling
In this project we opt for relative graphical scaling, i.e., the graphical components,
in our case the size of the circles, are scaled in proportion to the data being displayed
in the canvas at a given time interval. ￿is means that only the displayed objects
can be correctly compared with each other. ￿is approach aliviates the problem of
representing values with big di￿erence in magnitude, i.e., when the representation
of high values hinders the perception of the smaller ones, due to the e￿ect of scale.
W e start by identifying the stations being displayed at each interaction instance
– zooming or panning. First, the bounding box, which is located at the origin of
axis and equal to the canvas size, is projected onto the transformed map space. W e
take the inverse of the transformation matrix and multiply it by the bounding box
location, and scale the size according to the matrix scale. ￿en, we check for the
inclusion of each station within the bounding box, taking the maximum value in
that region. Finally, the local maxima found is used to scale all the other values of
the displayed stations. Figure ￿.￿ illustrates the reactive circle scaling.
Figure ￿.￿ Comparison between di￿erent view instances and the corresponding response
from visualization. ￿e image on the right represents the map pushed down along the Y
axis compared to the image on the le￿. All the relative proportions are retained, except the
local maxima.
￿.￿. Interactive Application
￿e previously described point representation and the map transformations are the
core of our visualization method. T o help users interactively explore and analyze
movement of inhabitants through the metro network we designed and developed

￿.￿ Interactive Application ￿￿￿
Figure ￿.￿￿ Interactive application for the analysis of ￿ows of passengers in metro. ￿e
image on the top shows the fully zoomed out map, and the image at the bottom shows
end of the transformation of the map. In both representations the same station and time
interval is selected.
an interactive graphical user interface. As shown in Figure￿.￿￿, the interface consist
of multiple components, which are described in this section.

￿￿￿ Smart City Portrayal
￿.￿.￿. General Overview
Exploring and analyzing the use of a complex metro network o￿en consist of
multiple tasks, such as: per station daily usage analysis; comparison of the users’
￿ows among metro stations; detection of overused regions or stations; etc. ￿e
interactive application depicted in Figure ￿.￿￿ provides means to ful￿ll these tasks.
At the upper le￿ corner the user can ￿nd a circular diagram. ￿is provides means
to navigate in time and depicts data regarding a daily usage of the selected metro
station. ￿e main window depicts the core visualization. ￿e user can zoom and
pan the map, as well as click on the stations to get additional information. Zooming
in on the map behaves as described earlier – the schematic map transforms into
an accurate georeferenced representation. At the end of the transformation a base
map is added, allowing the user to better orient in geographic space. ￿e interactive
application can be seen in action in the following link https://cdv.dei.uc.pt/
2017/metro-analyzer.mov.
￿.￿.￿. Circular Diagram
￿e circular diagram, found in the upper le￿ corner of the window (see Fig. ￿.￿￿),
serves two main functions – allow user to navigate in time and show data for the
selected station. ￿e diagram is presented in the circular form and is subdivided
by ￿￿ equal parts, each one corresponding to one hour of the day. By clicking on
the diagram the application jumps to the corresponding time interval, updating
the visualization accordingly. ￿e pointer is positioned according to the selected
time interval and the label is updated as well. Finally, when user selects a station
on the map its name appears centered on the top of the diagram, and its data is
depicted by a circular plot.
Regarding the representation of data in the circular plot, we considered both
entries and exists per metro station. Similar to reactive data scaling described earlier,
the circular plot shows relative data, i.e., the representation is only comparable
within the selected station and time interval. ￿is is done because we want to
separate di￿erent instances of the analysis, as well as diminish redundant infor-
mation, making each part of the visualization complement each other. Ultimately,
the circular plot should provide a broad picture of the daily usage of the station,
rather than allowing the comparison of the values among stations. Nevertheless,
the ranges for the values are shown in the visualization.
Entries and exits of a metro station are depicted using bars. ￿e height of each
one represents the counts of metro users at the selected station, and the red and
green colors represent entries and exist, respectively. ￿e grid with four divisions
provides visual cues that help analyze the data. ￿e labels are updated according
to the maximum value found throughout the day at the selected station. Finally,
for each time interval the color of the predominant value corresponds to the color
on the map.

￿.￿ Critical Discussion ￿￿￿
￿.￿. Critical Discussion
￿is section provides a qualitative evaluation based on T u￿e’ s principles on graphi-
cal excellence and integrity (T u￿e and Graves-Morris,￿￿￿￿), as well as a theoretical
evaluation taking into account the properties of graphic system proposed by Bertin
(￿￿￿￿). In general, the presented visualization follows the main principle of graphi-
cal excellence, which gives to the user a large number of ideas in a short time with
the least “ink” in the smallest space. First, in regard to the circular diagram, this
is based on the traditional method of visualizing time-oriented data. As for the
color hue, which is also used in the main visualization, Bertin claims to be the
most e￿cient visual variable for representing categorical data. In our case this is
an appropriate usage as the user wants to make a clear distinction between entries
and exits. ￿e use of a circular grid and the labels for temporal and data divisions
encourage graphical clarity and the precision of reading the plot.
￿e main map provides multiple readings, such as pattern detection in temporal
and spatial domains, low-level station analysis, and comparison of entries and
exits, and corresponding di￿erentiation among the stations. ￿e size is also used
appropriately, since according to Bertin this visual variable combined with the
plane mark are the only variables that can be used quantitatively. On the other hand,
the aggregation technique introduces graphical error in the rendering of blobs,
which can lead to incorrect estimation of the values, if not used as the model was
designed – observe and identify areas of interest in the general views, and analyze in
closed views. Also, the rendering of the blobs results in additional “ink” , which may
con￿ict with the principle of data-ink minimization – present the largest amount of
data with the least amount of “ink” . In contrast, the aggregation technique reduces
overall visual clutter, which perfectly ￿ts into T u￿e’ s principle of data density. Last
but not least, in the close views, a reference base map is added, such that the user
can accurately and rapidly locate each station in the city. Finally, the visualization
as a whole reveals multiple details and provides multiple perspectives over the
data, which ensures T u￿e’ s graphical excellence. Additionally, the visualization is
accompanied by the dynamically updated legend that assists with the reading of
the graphic and decoding the data.
￿.￿. Chapter Summary
In this chapter we described a visualization application that combines charac-
teristics such as pattern detection and low-level analysis of urban mobility, and
integrates multiple visualization techniques in a dynamic environment. While
zooming, the model adjusts, such that the representation is e￿cient at any zoom
scale. Our approach tackles the overlap problem by occluding unnecessary visual
information in general views, while mixing the primary colors, which allows detec-
tion of occluded elements of di￿erent categories. ￿e extended Metaball algorithm,
which was implemented using hardware acceleration, is an e￿cient and e￿ective
solution to represent high-density point-based geographic data in real-time. W e
presented the concept behind our model and the algorithm that computes the blobs

￿￿￿ Smart City Portrayal
from a set of points on geographic space. W e improved aesthetic aspects of point
representation by the means of exaggeration, which results in a organic-looking
visual outcome. By changing the slope parameter of the fall-o￿ function, the model
can be adjusted to ￿nd a good trade-o￿ between expressive visual artifacts and
precise representation. Finally, we demonstrated how graphical error behaves in
relation to zoom scale and fall-o￿ function.
￿e second core feature of this work is the transformations applied to the
representation of the metro map, which are essential for the e￿ectiveness of the
perception of changes in visualization. In our case, the user should clearly perceive
and correlate the location of stations in both map formats while zooming. W e pre-
sented methods for station to station and connection to connection transitions, link
displacement and reactive data scaling. ￿ese provide the user with the di￿erent
perspectives for reading the same data, namely: detection of overloaded areas of the
network at lower zoom scales in a visual clutter free display; and detailed analysis
of station usage at higher zoom levels though an accurate representation. Finally,
reactive data scaling partially solves the problem of representing values with big
di￿erence in magnitude, i.e., when the representation of high values hinders the
perception of the smaller ones, due to the e￿ect scale.
￿e interactive application presented provides the necessary tools to explore
geo-referenced time-series. ￿e circular diagram allows the users to navigate in
time, giving them a ￿rst general temporal overview of the data, and then allowing
them to move directly to the interesting time interval. Also, the patterns of metro
network usage throughout the day can be observed using the same graphical
interface. Additionally, the circular diagram depicts the daily usage of the selected
station. ￿e map implements zooming and panning, translating these actions into
a meaningful transformation of the visualization. Finally, each station is selectable,
providing the user with additional information on demand.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e visualization model presented in this chapter makes sev-
eral contributions, namely methods for:
• Point representation that can deal with high amounts of point-based geograph-
ical data in real-time. ￿e model is based on metaball technique and takes full
advantage of hardware acceleration.
• Computing graphical error in point representations, and the study of its be-
havior as a function of zoom and fall-o￿ function in our visualization. ￿is
method can be applied to other similar approaches to determine its accuracy
(e.g., graduated symbol maps).
• Metro map transformation through morphing between geographical and schematic
metro maps.
• Splitting overlapped segments, which accurately places multiple parallel seg-
ments along a common axis.
• Showing point-based data with varying scalings of graphical elements depend-
ing on the context (e.g., zoom, selected geographical area).

￿.￿ Chapter Summary ￿￿￿
￿ese developments allowed us to build an interactive application that integrates
map representation along with circular diagram, suited for the analysis of daily
￿ows of passengers in metro.

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿￿–￿￿￿
Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
Applying ￿ow maps in large datasets involves dealing with the reduction of visual
clutter. Nowadays, a technique known as edge bundling, which is geometric in
nature, is o￿en applied to reduce visual clutter and create meaningful traces that
highlight the main streams of ￿ow. ￿is chapter discusses an alternative approach
of edge bundling for generating ￿ow maps. Our approach uses a swarm-based
system to reduce visual clutter, bundling edges in an organic fashion and improving
clarity. ￿e method takes into account the properties of data, edges and nodes, to
bundle edges in a meaningful way while tracing lines that do not interfere visually
with the nodes. Additionally, the Dorling cartograms technique is applied to reduce
overlapping of graphical elements that render locations in geographic space. ￿e
method is demonstrated with application in the analysis of the U.S. migration
￿ow, and the transportation of products among warehouses and supermarkets of a
major retail company in Portugal.
￿.￿. Introduction
Flow mapping is a technique used to show the movement of objects from one loca-
tion to another, such as the migration of people, the amount of goods being traded,
the amounts of products being transported from warehouses to supermarkets, etc.
Flow maps say little or nothing about the pathway, but include the information
about what is ￿owing (moving, migrating, etc.), the direction of the ￿ow, and how
much is being transferred. In most cases, the data is represented using line width,
color and spatial properties. Flow maps are advantageous in what regards to the
visual clarity and ease of visual communication, enabling a quick understanding
of the main paths of ￿ow. ￿is is achieved by merging edges that share similar
destinations, or in some cases by tracing them through a similar path. However,
this technique o￿en fails when applied on large amounts of ￿ow data. ￿e vast
amounts of data to be displayed in the map, which can easily reach the range of
thousands or even millions of edges, can hide relevant paths. Due to multiple
crossings result in confusing and cluttered representation, making the map di￿cult
to read, and di￿cult to distinguish the grouped individual streams.
T o that end, visual clutter reduction strategy known as edge bundling can be
applied (Zhou et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿e goal of edge bundling is to merge sets of edges
that share close destinations, i.e., the original edges su￿er a geometrical distortion
so that they are routed along the same path. ￿e techniques for edge bundling

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
are characterized by graph simpli￿cation, reduction of clutter, and display of the
principal streams of ￿ow. Generally speaking, edge bundling consists of drawing
similar edges on the same path, i.e., edges that are related in geometry are accurately
(re)routed along the same path using rigorous prede￿ned rules and procedures. A
wide range of bundling approaches have been developed, the majority of which
are based on control structures, force-directed models, computational geometry,
image based methods, or graph simpli￿cation techniques. Notwithstanding, we
found lack of bundling techniques which present properties of contextual sensing
that go beyond geometric and topological properties of graphs and data. I.e., tech-
niques that, even for the same input, can produce di￿erent visual results depending
on other factors (e.g., level of zoom, canvas size, user preference) in addition to
geometrical and topological properties of graphs and data attributes. It is to note
that only recent approaches consider data attributes in bundling algorithms. Finally,
the majority of existing techniques tend to ignore nodes of the graph, or explicitly
do not take them into account, which can result in a visual interference of traces
with nodes.
In this work we describe a novel nondeterministic method for edge bundling –
Swarm-Based Edge Bundling (￿w￿￿￿￿), which produces ￿ow maps with varying
visual output and “style” , is prepared for contextual changes in dynamic visualiza-
tion, and is able to depict large amounts of data. ￿is method uses a customized
swarming system to trace edges in an organic fashion, reducing visual clutter and
overall graph complexity. In particular, our system is based on:
￿. ￿e steering algorithm of autonomous agents proposed by Reynolds (￿￿￿￿);
￿. ￿e mechanism of indirect communication among agents in system known
as stigmergy, i.e., the agents communicate with each other through the envi-
ronment. ￿e traces le￿ by agents are used to stimulate the behavior of other
agents.
￿e method described in this chapter takes into account edge properties (e.g.,
directionality, length, angle) and the data attributes. Also, our method accounts for
the presence of nodes and their properties. By routing the traces that avoid visual
interference with nodes, the visualization provides a clear distinction between
traces and nodes. Additionally, our method employs graphic design decisions to
promote clarity of visual communication in a high density environment. In order
to improve clarity of representation of geographic locations, a technique, known as
Dorling cartograms was applied (Dorling, ￿￿￿￿). With this technique, overlapped
nodes are separated retaining some degree of spatial relationship. Finally, our
approach supports mixed types of points – geo-referenced data points and those
that have no ￿xed position in space. An example of the former are supermarkets
￿ It is important to mention that in the original work the term Swarm-Based Edge Bundling was
acronymed as ￿￿￿￿. However, an earlier work known as Skeleton-Based Edge Bundling by Ersoy
et al. (￿￿￿￿) uses the same acronym. In order to clarify the distinction we changed the Swarm-Based
Edge Bundling acronym to ￿w￿￿￿.

￿ .￿ Related Work and Background ￿￿￿
with known location and of the later are warehouses with unknown geographic
positions.
With that said, our work addresses the issue of depicting large amounts of
transported products among warehouses and hyper and supermarkets of a major
retail company in Portugal. Our dataset consists of warehouse-to-supermarket
transitions, over a time span of ￿ months. ￿e locations consist of approximately
￿￿ warehouses, the majority of which located outside of Portugal, and ￿￿￿￿ super-
markets in Portugal, ￿￿￿ of which are geotagged, and ￿￿￿ supermarkets located
outside Portugal. ￿e ￿nal graph used in this work, nonetheless comprises only the
warehouses and supermarkets that are located on the continental part of Portugal.
In the following sections our approach is described in detail. Section￿ .￿presents
the underlying idea and the method. Section ￿ .￿describes an application of this
technique on the available dataset. Finally, Section ￿ .￿presents a comparison of
the results obtained by our approach and the existing one.
￿.￿. Related Work and Background
￿is section provides an overview of techniques and methods applied in our work.
Speci￿cally, we cover techniques for edge bundling applied in ￿ow mapping and
in graph drawing, and self-organizing systems observed in nature, and the cases of
their application in graphical representation. Additionally, this section provides
theoretical background on the concepts of self-organization and Multi-Agent
System (￿￿￿).
￿.￿.￿. Edge Bundling Methods
In the context of thematic mapping, an ￿￿ representation commonly refers to
the ￿ow visualization (also known as ￿ow maps), which is deeply rooted in the
history of information visualization (Dent, ￿￿￿￿). Generally speaking, a ￿ow map
depicts quantities of any type of objects that move from one location to another
– e.g., migrations, or transportation of goods (for more detailed description see
Section ￿.￿.￿. ￿e advantage of ￿ow maps is that they reduce visual clutter by
merging edges. However, when representing large amounts of data, this technique
presents a series of problems, such as poor perception of the ￿ow directionality,
high degrees of visual clutter, and overlapping of graphical elements that represent
locations.
Direct visualization of large amounts of￿￿ transitions can generate high degrees
of visual clutter. T o face this problem a visual clutter reduction strategy known as
edge bundling can be applied. ￿e goal of edge bundling is to merge sets of edges
that share close destinations, i.e., the original edges su￿er a geometrical distortion
so that they are routed along the same path. ￿e reduction of the visual clutter
enabled by edge bundling techniques reveal and present the main streams of ￿ow
(Zhou et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿e techniques of edge bundling are characterized by graph
simpli￿cation, reduction of clutter in graphs, and the revelation of the principal
streams of ￿ow. ￿e techniques for edge bundling have appeared in the beginning

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
of ￿￿’s (Newbery,￿￿￿￿), and termed by Dickerson et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) in early ￿￿￿￿’s.
Holten (￿￿￿￿) extended edge bundling method for compound graphs, which
roughly consists in routing edges through a hierarchical layout using B-Splines.
Nowadays, there are several variations of edge bundling, such as: Force-Directed
Edge Bundling (￿￿￿￿) (Holten and Wijk, ￿￿￿￿) and (Dwyer et al., ￿￿￿￿) applied
in general graphs; (Cui et al., ￿￿￿￿; Hurter et al., ￿￿￿￿; Lambert et al., ￿￿￿￿)u s e d
in spatial trail sets; (Hurter et al., ￿￿￿￿) used in dynamic graphs; and more recent
approaches that account for edge properties have changed edge bundling into a
meaningful, driven by data, edge tracing (Peysakhovich et al., ￿￿￿￿).
￿ese techniques cover a wide range of approaches to edge bundling, and the
way they trace edges is based on diverse techniques, such as: control structures
(Holten, ￿￿￿￿); force-directed models (Dwyer et al., ￿￿￿￿; Holten and Wijk,￿￿￿￿);
computational geometry techniques (Cui et al., ￿￿￿￿; Lambert et al., ￿￿￿￿); image
based techniques (Ersoy et al., ￿￿￿￿; Hurter et al., ￿￿￿￿; T elea and Ersoy, ￿￿￿￿);
and graph simpli￿cation techniques (T elea and Ersoy,￿￿￿￿). ￿is section does not
claim to be complete, and a more extensive overview of the existing techniques
can be found in (Lhuillier et al., ￿￿￿￿). Nevertheless, in essence, edge bundling
techniques consist of drawing similar edges on the approximately same path, i.e.,
edges that are related in geometry are accurately (re)routed along the same path
using rigorous prede￿ned rules and procedures. ￿e reduction of the visual clutter
enabled by edge bundling techniques reveals and presents the main streams of
￿ow.
￿.￿.￿. Self-organization
Another important characteristic of this work is the focus on nature-inspired
approaches. ￿e underlying idea is based on self-organizing systems, more specif-
ically on the phenomenon of emergence in such systems. As the term indicates,
self-organization is a property of some complex systems, in which the structure
or organization appears without interference from external sources, typically re-
sulting form a decentralized approach (Di Marzo Serugendo et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿us,
self-organizing processes o￿en result in the occurrence of emergent phenomena,
when the complex structure or behavior appears due to the simple low level inter-
actions of a collection of individuals. ￿ere are several mechanisms of interaction
among individuals, but the one that is used in this work stigmergy, which consists
of indirect communication through the environment (Di Marzo Serugendo et al.,
￿￿￿￿).
Self-organizing processes o￿en result in the occurrence of emergent phenomena.
Namely, when the complex behavior appears due to the interaction of a collective
of individuals, which was not programmed for that. For instance, the patterns of a
sand dune are an emergent property of the system, rather than a property applied
by external forces. Self-organization is widely used in multi-agent systems, which
provide solution to diverse problems.
Self-organizing and emergent phenomena can be observed in many natural sys-
tems. According to Di Marzo Serugendo et al. (￿￿￿￿) natural self-organizing systems

￿ .￿ Related Work and Background ￿￿￿
can be classi￿ed as physical, biological and social systems. Self-organizing systems
can be built from the beginning with all the characteristics of self-organization.
￿ese systems are referred to as arti￿cial self-organizing systems, which tend to
be based on existing natural systems, or implement mechanisms explicitly for
the purpose of self-organization. Arti￿cial systems have been applied to a wide
range of ￿elds, e.g., problem solving, learning and optimization problems, pattern
recognition, decision-making, simulation (Kennedy and Eberhart, ￿￿￿￿).
Self-organization is widely used in ￿￿￿ applications, which provide solution
to diverse problems. An agent is a physical or virtual entity situated within an
environment, i.e., a physical world or operating system respectively. ￿ese agents
act autonomously and realize a set of objectives that a￿ect environment over time.
Intelligent agents can be roughly classi￿ed as reactive, pro-active, socially able
and adaptive (Franklin and Graesser, ￿￿￿￿). Single agents have limitations (e.g.,
physical, cognitive, temporal). In other words, a single agent might be rationally
bounded, controlling only a small amount of information, or might have physical
limitations, or an agent can simply exceed its lifetime.
Intuitively, a￿￿￿ is populated with intelligent agents to overcome their individ-
ual limitations, which o￿en leads to emergent phenomena in di￿erent contexts
of application (Serugendo et al., ￿￿￿￿). In the same work Serugendo et al. ( ￿￿￿￿)
highlights ￿ve di￿erent approaches of implementation in ￿￿￿:
• Direct interaction between agents using basic principles such as broadcast-
ing and localization, where agents interact locally with each other and the
environment;
• Indirect interactions between agents and stigmergy, where agent interact with
each other through the environment;
• Reinforcement of agents’ behavior, where agents dynamically modify their
behavior according to some reinforcement;
• Cooperation behavior of individual agent, where the system cooperates with
its environment in order to improve the response time to the environment;
• Choice of generic architecture, where a meta-model agents’ organization is
instantiated and dynamically modi￿ed as needed.
￿.￿.￿. Self-organization and visualization
In the ￿eld of data visualization there are techniques of graphical representation
that are based on multi-agent systems. ￿e emergent behavior and visual patterns
of these systems can be seen as a portrayal of the dynamic data. W e found that
the use of these systems can range from drawing simple graphs (Nascimento
et al., ￿￿￿￿) to more sophisticated agent based visualization architectures that
implement complex rendering and abstraction strategies (Roard and Jones, ￿￿￿￿).
Mechanisms of self-organized systems can vary from well-known particle systems,
for instance, to visualize fuzzy object (Reeves, ￿￿￿￿) to customized systems with
its rules and agents, for instance, visualization agents, which allow e￿ective access

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
to information in a large website through an iterative process representing the
structure of W orld Wide W eb (￿￿￿) in a ￿D hyperbolic tree (Hiraishi et al., ￿￿￿￿).
“Geo￿ocking” is an example of a￿￿￿ applied in geovisualization (Macgill and
Openshaw, ￿￿￿￿). In their work Macgill and Openshaw ( ￿￿￿￿) propose a method
to reveal patterns in multivariable spatial data through the use of customized
￿ocking system, originally proposed by Reynolds ( ￿￿￿￿). ￿e model is able to
adapt to various characteristics of spatial data using so called geoboids. A geoboid
explores geographical space and tests it for “interesting” patterns sampling each
new location. In traditional ￿ocking systems each boid has equal behavior, since
they all share the same cohesion, separation and alignment rules. In this system
each geoboid changes its behavior according to its performance, which is based on
the data found in corresponding location.
A more recent work by V ande Moere et al. (￿￿￿￿) is based on particle systems.
￿e system consists of a collection of particles, authors designate them as infoticles,
and a set of static forces, which in￿uences the speed and direction of particles. Each
particle in the system encodes a unique data object in the dataset using traditional
graphical variables (e.g., color, position, size). When the time-window shi￿s the
system sends a query to the database and updates all the data in the next time-
window. Consequently, as the data values update, the particles are attracted by a
di￿erent set of forces. Due to the diverse interactions between speed and direction,
emergent visual patterns appear, each one conveying meaningful changes. ￿is
example demonstrates how the visual variables can be e￿ciently combined with
self-organizing systems, resulting in emergent visual artifacts that can convey
meaningful information and allow reasoning about the time-varying data.
Another work that uses a nature-inspired approach is the information ￿ock-
ing visualization by Moere ( ￿￿￿￿). As the name indicates, this visualization is
based on an arti￿cial ￿ocking system, which follows simple behavior rules origi-
nally introduced by Reynolds (￿￿￿￿). ￿e forces of attraction and separation are
modi￿ed proportionally to the similarity of data values each boid encodes. As
the time-window shi￿s, new data are collected and the data objects are updated
making each boid to behave accordingly to the new data value conditions. During
the simulation each boid leaves a trace with a gradually decreasing transparency.
Additionally, all the boids are enclosed within semi-transparent shapes, which
facilitate the perception of three-dimensional clusters. Although the information
￿ocking system allows the user to e￿ciently analyze the data, the exploratory
capabilities of visualization are limited to observe the changes in time-varying data.
Ultimately, the problem of visual clutter and computational cost might arise when
large data sets are considered.
In comparison to the traditional visualization approaches the self-organized
systems have their advantages and disadvantages. In traditional visualization the
data values are directly encoded with graphical elements. ￿us, the similarity
in data corresponds to visual similarity of the representing elements, i.e., data
items that are similar tend to be highlighted with identical color. In contrast, in

￿ .￿ Our ￿ocking model ￿￿￿
Figure ￿ .￿Detail of the visual
output from the system using
Swarm-Based Edge Bundling.
Closeup of Figure ￿ .￿
self-organized systems the similarity is achieved through a decentralized process,
where similar data items should ￿rst ￿nd each other and then apply similar color.
Self-organizing systems may be inadequate, for instance, in cases when each
data item is allocated to a single data visualization agent. ￿e processing of data and
rendering might become computationally costly. Also, ine￿ciency of controlling
multi-agent systems might lead to unexpected emergent patterns, consequently
making visualization less precise.
Following these ideas we intend to use nature-inspired algorithms that will
allow the creation of visualizations, which derive from emergent properties of data.
In this way, emergent phenomena will be explored to reveal high-level information,
and represent it, in non-conventional ways. Also, we intend to contribute to the
development of the state of the art, by applying other nature-inspired algorithms
in this context and by improving the e￿ciency of existing ones.
￿.￿. Our ￿ocking model
In order to re￿ect the ￿owing nature of the data we resort to a customized ￿ocking
system. ￿e underlying model to construct ￿ow maps shares common characteris-
tics with our initial work on edge-bundling (Polisciuc et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿e underlying
idea is to get an arti￿cial agent from point A to point B avoiding obstacles and,
when possible, following existing trails that potentially lead to the destination.
However, our new approach changes the way agents of the system interact with
each other. Basically, the coordination is done through the environment, using stig-
mergy. Also, action selection rules were changed, in order to achieve meaningful
behavior, prioritizing obstacle avoidance and trail following.
￿e visualization can be seen as a directed graph composed by nodes and edges.
￿e system consists of arti￿cial agents (further referred to as vehicles), each one
tracing an individual ￿￿ edge. A vehicle is characterized mainly by its position in
space, direction and speed. During the simulation each vehicle leaves persistent
traces (further referenced to as markers), which inherit the location and direction,
at each simulation instant. Since the process is asynchronous, i.e., each trace is
computed separately, the vehicles in the system interact with each other through
the environment. While interacting with the markers each vehicle follows simple
rules: attract to the compatible markers; avoid obstacles, which are the nodes of
the graph; arrive at the destination. In order to determine the relationship between
the vehicle and the marker we used a pairwise compatibility measures, including
geometric properties and the weight of each one. ￿e reminder of this section
describes our system in detail, including the strategy for steering behavior selection,
compatibility metrics, and the modi￿cation of the environment. An example of
the output of the system is depicted in Figures ￿ .￿and ￿ .￿.
￿.￿.￿. ￿e Model
As previously mentioned, our system consists of a set of autonomous agents –
vehicles. Following Reynolds’ ideas the motion behavior of an autonomous agent

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
Figure ￿ .￿Visual output from the system a￿er ￿￿ full cycles. Circles with black stroke
represent nodes. Red points with an arrow are markers.
is de￿ned by three layers: action selection, steering and locomotion (Reynolds,
￿￿￿￿). Regarding the locomotion we use a simple vehicle model described in his
work. ￿e simple vehicle is characterized by a position in space, mass, velocity,
maximum speed and maximum force. Using Euler integration the position is
altered by adding the velocity, which is modi￿ed by applying steering forces that

￿ .￿ Our ￿ocking model ￿￿￿
are truncated by the maximum force. Additionally, the velocity is truncated by
the maximum speed. Regarding steering, we used several behaviors, such as seek,
arrive, obstacle avoidance, separation and cohesion. Finally, action selection uses
the sensors of the vehicle to de￿ne the strategy, stetting the goal of the vehicle in
response to the environment. For detailed information see the original Reynolds’
work (Reynolds, ￿￿￿￿).
In our systems each edge is traced by only one vehicle, starting at the origin
node and ￿nishing at the destination node, constantly leaving markers along its
trail. Each trace is computed individually, and the computation of each one only
starts when the previous one has ￿nished, and so forth for all the traces. In each
cycle the traces are updated according to the current state of the environment.
More precisely, during the execution cycle each vehicle considers the markers
le￿ by other vehicles, and updates them if the speci￿c conditions are met. ￿is
interaction determines the behavior of the vehicles. ￿e process repeats until the
visual result is acceptable and the user decides to stop it. ￿e procedure of single
cycle is expressed in the pseudo-code found in Algorithm ￿ .￿.
￿e behavior of our vehicles can be better understood by describing each step
individually and their integration. ￿ere are four essential steps in our system:
action selection, steering, locomotion and environment update.
￿ .￿.￿.￿. Action selection
￿e ￿rst step that de￿nes the overall strategy of the vehicle’s behavior is action
selection. Using the attached sensors, vehicles perceive the environment and decide
what action to take. In our model there are three types of sensors: one that detects
the presence of obstacles, another that detects the presence of markers, and the
last one that measures the similarity between an agent and a marker.
• ￿e ￿rst sensor has a circular form, with a certain radius and distance of ac-
tivation. ￿e distance of activation, as the name indicates, dictates how soon
the obstacles will be detected and avoided. In order to create smooth traces
the distance should use reasonably high values, which depend on the scale of
the image. ￿e radius de￿nes minimal collision distance between vehicle and
obstacle, which in turn has its own radius.
• ￿e second sensor, which detects the presence of markers, has the form of a
cone, and is characterized by the ￿eld of sight, more preciselyangle and distance
of vision. ￿e direction of the ￿eld of sight is also aligned with the velocity
vector. If there are markers found in the ￿eld of sight they are considered for
similarity comparison.
• ￿e third sensor measures the similarity between the current vehicle and the
markers in the ￿eld of vision. ￿e similarity metric is described later in this
section.
￿e strategy of applying steering forces is based on the prioritization of behav-
iors and consists of simple rules (as depicted in Figure ￿ .￿). ￿e very ￿rst step is
to check if the vehicle is within its arrival distance. In the positive case the only

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
Algorithm ￿ .￿Pseudo-code for a single cycle of edge bundling.
1: procedure ￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿(edges, nodes, environment)
2: for each e in edges do
3: v ← createV ehicle(e)
4: clearTrace(e.trace )
5: while alive(v) do
6: fo r c e← (0,0,0)
7: if withinArrivalDistance(v, e.destination ) then
8: fo r c e← fo r c e+arrival(v, e.destinationNode ) ▷ Direct
implementation of arrival behavior (Reynolds, 1999).
9: else
10: if obstaclesFound(v, nodes ) then
11: fo r c e← fo r c e+obstacleAvoidance(v, nodes ) ▷ Direct
implementation of “obstacle avoidance” behavior (Reynolds,1999).
12: else
13: if markersFound(v, environment ) then ▷ Searches for
markers in the environment using a set of rules
14: fo r c e← fo r c e+attract(v, environment ) ▷
Uses a combined behavior of cohesion and seek (Reynolds, 1999). Also, this
function increases markers weight at a prede￿ned forti￿cation rate.
15: else
16: fo r c e← fo r c e+arrival(v, e.destinationNode )
17: end if
18: end if
19: end if
20: applyForce(fo r c e)
21: if ￿v.lastLocation − v.location ￿ > traceGranularity then
22: m ← crateMarker(v)
23: if foundMarkers(m, environment ) then
24: updateMarkers(m, environment ) ▷ Updates each found
marker with the information of the marker, following a set of rules.
25: else
26: addT oEnvironment(m, environment )
27: end if
28: addT oTrace(e.trace , v.location )
29: end if
30: end while
31: end for
32: for each marker in environment do
33: evaporate(marker , evaporationRate ) ▷ Decreases the weight of
markers at a prede￿ned evaporation rate.
34: end for
35: end procedure

￿ .￿ Our ￿ocking model ￿￿￿
behavior that is applied is arrive to the destination, which follows arrival model
proposed by Reynolds (￿￿￿￿). Otherwise, we apply obstacle avoidance behavior,
which implements obstacle avoidance behavior described by Reynolds (￿￿￿￿). If
the computed steering force is null, then we proceed to the second behavior, which
is attraction. ￿e attraction force consists of an average weighted vector pointing
towards the “center of gravity” of the markers in the ￿eld of view, using the com-
patibility measure as a weighting factor described in the following paragraph. ￿e
attraction is based on combined behavior of cohesion and seek (Reynolds, ￿￿￿￿).
Finally, if the attraction force is null, we proceed with the calculation of the arrival
behavior. In other words, the idea behind the vehicle’ s behavior is to avoid obstacles
if there is the possibility of collision, otherwise it tries to follow existing potential
trails that lead to the destination. If there are no trails in the ￿eld of vision, it just
proceeds in the direction of the destination.

￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e similarity measure is comprised by a set of rules,
which are based on the comparison of angles. In our approach we only consider
the angle in the compatibility measure. While other solutions use a geometric
approach, and, therefore, there is the need to use some edge compatibility measure
to compute bundles, in our case bundles are the emergent output from the simple
interaction among vehicles and their collective behavior. So, given a maximum
allowed angle, say θ, for each marker we proceed with the following steps:
￿. Calculate the angle α between the vector pointing towards the marker location
and the vector pointing towards the destination node;
￿. Compute the angle β between the vector pointing from the marker location
towards the vehicle’ s destination and the marker’ s direction;
￿. If both of these angles are bellow θ, i.e., α < θ ∧ β < θ, then the attraction force
is calculated and weighted by the marker’ s weight (as show in Figure￿ .￿).
In order to get smooth approximation to the trail, the attraction vector points
towards a location a few steps ahead of the marker. Figure ￿ .￿exempli￿es di￿erent

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
α1
α2 α3
β1

Black and red circles represent markers accepted and rejected for calculation, respectively.
￿e white circle with a dot is a vehicle and the gray area is the ￿eld of sight. ￿e white

scenarios of vehicle–mark compatibility. In this example, red dots are not consid-
ered for calculation, either because they are outside of the sight area, or because
do not satisfy the similarity rules. Assuming that α￿ > θ and β￿ > θ,t h er e s p e c t i v e
markers are rejected. In the end the vehicle is attracted to the marker that points
in the direction of vehicle’s destination ensuring that it will reach its destination.
Other similarity metrics were studied and presented in Section ￿.￿.￿.
￿ .￿.￿.￿. Steering and Locomotion
￿e steering behaviors used in our system are: arrive, avoid obstacles, and attract.
￿ese models and their implementation, as well as locomotion, strictly follow
Reynolds models. However, there are some additional aspects that were introduced
in order to achieve an intelligent edge bundling. In the beginning, the maximum
speed limit is zero and increases with time. ￿is is done to achieve less erratic
behaviors near the origin location. ￿e mass and the distance of vision are modi￿ed
according to the edge’ s weight, such that the vehicle’ s that trace edges with higher
weight are heavier and less responsive than the ones that encode edges with low
weights. In this case, the former traces become the central trails that su￿er less
modi￿cation. Finally, the attraction force is weighted according to the weight of
the markers being considered in each step. ￿erefore, vehicles prioritize the trails
with higher weights.
￿ .￿.￿.￿. Environment update
￿e environment of our system shares similar ideas of stigmergy, where vehicles
communicate with each other through the environment. In contrast to other

￿ .￿ Application ￿￿￿
algorithms, such as ant colony, where there are one nest and various sources of
food, in graphs there are various origins and destinations. In this case, in order to
get to a speci￿c destination there is the need to know an approximate indication
about the direction of the trail. ￿erefore, we use the concept of marker, which
is characterized not only by the location in space and its evaporation rate, but
also by the direction. Each marker inherits some characteristics of the vehicle
that deposited it – the location where the marker was le￿ and the direction which
corresponds to the velocity vector at that instance. With that said, the trails are
updated as follows:
• Each vehicle leaves markers at a certain rate, which de￿nes granularity of the
trace;
• ￿en search for other markers that are already in the environment within a
certain range;
• If no markers are found, create one new marker and add it to the environment;
• Otherwise, for each found marker proceed with compatibility veri￿cation by
comparing their directions against a prede￿ned threshold. ￿en for each com-
patible marker update their location and direction, averaging with the location
and direction of the vehicle.
An important aspect of environment modi￿cation is the rate of marker’ s forti￿-
cation and the evaporation. At each frame (time unit) the weight of each marker
in the environment is decreased by a certain degree. When the weight reaches zero,
the marker is removed from the environment. Each time an existing marker is
updated, the weight is increased by a certain degree. W e used small values for forti￿-
cation and lager ones for evaporation. In other words, the markers that are updated
with frequency become more stable composing central trails of the environment.
Also, there are upper limits of forti￿cation that restrict continuous increase in the
weight. ￿e forti￿cation and evaporation can be seen as the stabilization rate of
the system and the tested number of bundling solutions, respectively.
￿.￿. Application
In this section we describe a visualization application of our method for the ￿ow
map. W e apply this technique to visualize the ￿ow of products among warehouses
and supermarkets, depicting movements of stocks of products of a major retail
company in Portugal.
￿.￿.￿. Data Description
Our dataset consists of warehouse-to-supermarket transitions of products over a
time span of ￿ months. Each transition has the following attributes: product id,
quantity of products in transit (further referred to as stock in transit (￿￿￿)), quantity
of products delivered (further referred to as stock on hand (￿￿￿)), warehouse id,
supermarket id, and the date of transition. ￿e locations consist of approximately ￿￿
warehouses, some of which are located outside the Portugal and ￿￿￿￿ supermarkets

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
in Portugal and ￿￿￿ supermarkets outside the Portugal, from which only ￿￿￿ are
geolocated (see also Maçãs et al., ￿￿￿￿a,b; Polisciuc et al., ￿￿￿￿).
In order to get a graph representation of the data we proceeded with the cal-
culation phase. First, the data were aggregated by days. Each day sums-up ￿￿￿
quantities by aggregating pairs of warehouse-supermarket locations, which con-
stitute the edges and the nodes of the graph. Finally, the total of ￿￿￿ quantities
per supermarket is calculated. ￿erefore, we get a weighted directed graph whose
edges are directed from warehouse to supermarket nodes and weighted by ￿￿￿
quantities. ￿e nodes that represent supermarkets have an assigned ￿￿￿ quantity,
while the nodes that represent warehouse have none.
Finally, we ￿ltered the nodes that only belong to the continental part of Portugal.
W e also excluded the supermarkets that have no geographic reference. For the sake
of demonstration we focused only on the date of April ￿, ￿￿￿￿. So, the ￿nal graph
consists of ￿￿￿ nodes and ￿￿￿￿ edges.
￿.￿.￿. Visualization of the Flow of Products
￿is visualization depicts amounts of products that have been transported and the
amounts of products that have been delivered during one day. It is important to
describe the process to get a readable layout of a mixed graph, i.e., graph that is
comprised by di￿erent types of nodes (e.g., georeferenced vs non-georeferenced
nodes). ￿ere are two challenges to visualize this particular graph: (i) not all
the locations have a geographical reference and (ii) the ones that do have may
overlap. ￿e ￿rst issue was solved by anchoring the position of the nodes that
have a geographical location, and then applying a force-directed graph layout
algorithm (Fruchterman and Reingold, ￿￿￿￿) to compute the location of other ones.
￿is algorithm is e￿cient in what concerns the graph topology, since it considers
clusters of nodes and not individual nodes. ￿e second issue was solved by applying
the Dorling cartogram technique over the pre-computed layout. ￿e beauty of this
algorithm is that it preserves the original relative positioning of geo-referenced
elements, enabling the map of Portugal to be recognizable, while making the
locations distinguishable, increasing visual clarity (as shown in Figure ￿ .￿).
In the visualization each trace represents the quantity of products in transit
from warehouse to supermarket. ￿ese quantities are encoded by two means –color
and line thickness. ￿e two graphical elements are complimentary and make use of
di￿erent mappings. W e use a linear mapping for color. ￿e values are mapped to a
pale yellow-purple-dark blue gradient, being pale yellow and dark blue for lowest
and highest values, respectively. ￿e thickness variable, on the other hand, uses
an exponential scale, emphasizing high values. Finally, we use an arrow, which is
rendered at the end of a trace and scaled proportionally to the thickness of the line,
to represent the direction of the ￿ow (such as shown in Figure ￿ .￿). ￿e orientation
and the size of the arrowhead is determined in three steps. First, the height h and
the width w of the arrow is computed. ￿e w and h are equal to the ￿x and ￿x the
thickness, respectively. Second, the last vertex v of a trace which should be located
at the minimum distance from location of the node c plus its radius r and the h is

circles and squares represent supermarkets and warehouses, respectively. ￿e circles with
crosses inside represent georeferenced supermarkets.
determined. ￿ird, the arrow orientation is de￿ned by the vector pointing from v
to c (as shown in Figure ￿ .￿).

In order to represent di￿erent types of nodes we use di￿erent graphical elements
– squares and circles to represent warehouses and supermarkets, respectively. ￿e
nodes are colored according to the country they represent. Since our data contains
several countries, color could become ine￿ective. In this case, we calculate the
total number of locations, aggregating by country, sort the countries in descendant
order, and consider only the ￿rst two countries, which are Portugal and Spain, and
treat the others as an unique instance. ￿e colors used were green, red and blue for
Portugal, Spain and others, respectively. Finally, the nodes that have geographic
reference were rendered with the graphical shape of an X in the center of the node
(see Figure ￿ .￿).
￿ .￿.￿.￿. Density visualization
Due to the high degree of overlapping traces, it is hard to identify main streams.
For that reason, we proceed with a color-temperature visualization of the ￿ow map.
Using the same graph we apply di￿erent color coding. In this case the traces are
colored according to the total number of overlapping elements. More precisely,
we compute the number of overlapping segments that build-up the traces located
within a de￿ned range. ￿en at the render instance this value is mapped to a color
scale from intense red to a pale green, where red and green mean high and low
degrees of overlaps. ￿is representation is useful to get another perspective of
such complex visualization (as shown in Figure ￿ .￿(a)). Latter, this technique was
improved to couple larger graphs with less impact over the performance. Also,
this improvement was intended to so￿en the color transitions among segments of
traces. In this approach kernel density estimation (Silverman, ￿￿￿￿) was used to
compute the underlying distribution, and consequently the density of overlapping
segments. ￿en, in similar way we color-mapped the values onto the pixels of
canvas, however (such as shown in Figure ￿ .￿(b)).
￿.￿. Comparison With Other T echniques
￿e visualizations shown in this section depict the U.S. migration ￿ow. ￿e data
covers migrations among cities of the United States during the period from ￿￿￿￿
to ￿￿￿￿. ￿e graph consists of ￿￿￿ nodes and ￿￿￿￿ edges. ￿e weight of the edges
correspond to the number of migrants normalized by the maximum value. It is
important to mention that the cities not located in the continental part of the
country were excluded. T o encode the directionality of edges color was used, more
precisely the hue value [￿, ￿￿￿]. ￿e absolute angle of the vector pointing from the
origin node towards the destination was linearly mapped to the hue value (such as
show in Figure ￿ .￿(a)).
With that said, this section proceeds with the comparison among straight line
technique, ￿￿￿￿ method, and selected results of our technique. For the analysis
of our approach we used pairs of low and high values for the parameters such as
maximum speed, maximum force, angle of vision, distance of visionand compatibility
angle. ￿e maximum force can be translated into the agility of vehicles, which is

￿ .￿ Comparison With Other T echniques ￿￿￿
(a) (b)

easier to think of in the context of behavior. Higher maximum forces mean higher

So, we generate all the permutations and identify combinations of values that can
be divided in several groups. From the groups that achieved e￿cient results the
representative images were selected and analyzed in detail.
￿e very ￿rst comparison reveals the e￿ciency of visual clutter reduction using
the edge-bundling technique, comparing to the straight line representation (as can
be seen in Figure ￿ .￿). As can be observed, the ￿￿￿￿ method (see Figure ￿ .￿(b))

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
Figure ￿ .￿Comparison between the techniques (a) straight lines and (b) ￿￿￿￿ generated

edges.
generates less visual clutter in comparison with our approach (see Figure￿ .￿). How-
ever, the algorithms such as ￿￿￿￿ do not account for the presence of nodes, where
traces can be drawn on top of nodes, and some of them neither account for the
directionality of edges. In contrast, these properties are the emergent characteristic
of our method. Since the vehicles in our system attempt to avoid obstacles, the
nodes are clearly visible and do not visually interfere with the lines (see for instance
Figure ￿ .￿(b)). Also, as can be observed in the same ￿gure, there are no traces
with opposite directions that are routed through the same path. ￿e only moment,
where the traces can overlap, is the splitting or the joining of streams. Additionally,
when using swarms, main streams of ￿ow become visually distinct from each other,
leaving enough space for the ones with less impact. Moreover, since our approach
uses the edge weights in the calculation, the generated bundles better express the
data, resulting in a meaningful representation (see Figures ￿ .￿and ￿ .￿￿).
