ingful bundles are clearly de￿ned and distinguishable from each other. However,
the resulting map is highly fragmented in comparison with the examples that
follow. Also, the representation presents constant changes between streams. ￿is
behavior is due to the low compatibility angle. ￿e vehicles try to keep their ve-
locity directions aligned with the destination. Finally, as can be noticed, due to
high velocity and low agility the endings of traces are curly. Such can be useful for
distinguishing ￿ow’ s origin form destination (such as shown on Figure￿ .￿￿).
￿e example shown in Figure ￿ .￿(b), uses high values for all the parameters. In
this example the main streams are clearly distinguishable from other traces. Due to
high speed and low agility, vehicles tend to draw traces in a more organic manner.
However, there is a loss in the detail of trace and overall visualization. Similar to
the previous examples the endings of traces present curly geometry.
Both examples presented in Figure￿ .￿￿use low maximum speed. ￿e distinction
is in the agility: the example on (a) uses low maximum force and the example on
(b) uses high maximum force. As can be observed, the traces in the representation
on (a) change their direction more abruptly than the ones of the image on (b).
Additionally, it is clearly noticeable that curly endings disappear. ￿is is because
of low speed, therefore the vehicles have enough time to turn. Additionally, the
image on (b) includes the trails’ marks to illustrate the e￿ciency of the method to
trace the streams of ￿ow using swarms.
￿e method presented herein still has several limitations. ￿e combination of
high speed and mass with low agility can result in imperceptible visualization. As
can be imagined with these parameters the vehicle advances fast and turns slowly,
therefore making it harder to reach the destination. ￿e vehicle spins around the
destination node during a long time slowing down computational performance
and creating visual artifacts. Another limitation, which can be advantageous in
some cases, is that in highly dense graphs vehicles are unable to draw smooth
traces. In particular, in the areas where nodes form clusters, the vehicles attempt
to avoid the entire cluster, therefore creating visual artifacts.

￿￿￿ Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
￿.￿. Chapter Summary
As previously mentioned, applying ￿ow maps to a large amount of data is chal-
lenging, since it involves dealing with high degrees of visual clutter. In this chapter
we described a method for generating ￿ow maps that overcomes the cluttering
issue in visualization. Our approach relies on a nature-inspired algorithm, which
creates emergent visual patterns. ￿is approach, results in edge bundling, reduc-
ing visual clutter and promoting visual clarity in the representation. In addition,
we explored di￿erent graphical languages applied on the generated graph to give
diverse perspectives over the same dataset.
Our method consists of a set of vehicles that trace a path to represent each
edge in the graph. Each single vehicle follows simple rules through the interaction
with the neighboring trails. ￿e compatibility between the edges and markers
of the trails determines whether vehicles are attracted to markers or not. ￿is
makes the vehicles follow the trails that eventually take them to their destination.
Furthermore, the vehicles not only contribute to the environment by leaving new
markers, they also update existing ones. ￿is is done for logical reasons and to
reduce computational complexity. In addition, the vehicles that represent more
products have higher impact on other members of the system. Finally, every vehicle
attempts to avoid obstacles, i.e., nodes, creating clear visual distinction between
traces and nodes.
W e described two types of graphical representation. W e presented the main
visualization, which depicts transitions of products from warehouses to supermar-
kets. ￿e total amounts of products being transported are represented with color
and line thickness. ￿e directionality of movement is indicated by an arrow at
the end of each trace. ￿e nodes use shape to indicate either if they represent a
warehouse or a supermarket. Finally, the ￿xed nodes are marked with an “X” in the
center of the node. Additionally, the sorting order of the edges re￿ects the emphasis
on low or high values. ￿en, we presented a graphical approach to distinguish
main streams of ￿ow. ￿is is achieved by coloring the edges by their degree of
overlap. In this case, the red and green colors represent a high and low number of
overlapped traces, giving a visual representation of the complexity of the graph.
Finally, we compared our method with the force-directed edge bundling tech-
nique and performed a study of the impact of systems parameters on the visual
output. W e distinguished several groups of parameter combinations that give simi-
lar results. ￿erefore, we established that using high values for maximum speed
results in lower waviness of the streams, and in combination with high values for
mass, the method produces smooth separation of traces from main streams. Also,
using low values for maximum force results in a curly ending of traces, which
can be used as an indicator to distinguish between the origin and destination of
streams. On the other hand, low values of maximum speed result in a compact
and well-de￿ned bundling. However, the changes in the direction of streams be-
come abrupt. Finally, low values for compatibility measure produce fragmented
representation with a higher number of separate streams, but the traces become
less “wavy” and the changes in the separation from main streams are less abrupt.

￿ .￿ Chapter Summary ￿￿￿
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e main contributions of the work presented in this chapter
are the following:
• A novel approach to edge bundling that goes beyond approaches based on
computational geometry, and considers the properties of data. Our model was
inspired on processes found in nature, particularly, swarm behavior and indirect
communication among agents in self-organizing systems.
• A strategy for action selection, which can be applied in other similar swarm-
based systems.
• A model which combines swarm steering and locomotion with stigmergy-like
communication, unlike in traditional swarm-based systems. More precisely,
the proposed method uses the concept of trail composed by marks, which get
forti￿ed, when used by vehicles for navigation, or evaporated, when remain
untouched.
• A method for precise placement and alignment of arrowheads in directional
graphs.
• A visualization method for density representation in bundled graphs.
• A study on the e￿ect of di￿erent parameters in swarm-based edge bundling.

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿￿–￿￿￿
Evolutionary Swarm-Based
Edge Bundling
Edge bundling methods are used in ￿ow maps and graphs to reduce the visual
clutter, which is generated when representing complex and heterogeneous data.
Nowadays, there are many edge bundling algorithms that have been successfully
applied to a wide range of problems in graph representation. However, the majority
of these methods are still di￿cult to use and apply on real world problems by the ex-
perts from other areas. ￿is is due to the complexity of the algorithms and concepts
behind them, as well as a strong dependence on their parametrization. In addition,
the majority of edge bundling methods need to be ￿ne-tuned when applied on
di￿erent datasets. ￿is chapter presents a new approach that helps to ￿nd near-
optimal parameters for solving such issues in edge bundling algorithms, regardless
of the con￿guration of the input graph. Our method is based on evolutionary
computation, allowing the users to ￿nd edge bundling solutions for their needs. In
particular, the presented method is based on the use of single objective and multi-
objective optimization evolutionary algorithm (￿￿￿￿), together with evaluation
metrics speci￿cally designed for automatically assessing the quality of bundled
drawings (white space and curvature) In order to understand the e￿ectiveness of
the evolutionary algorithm in such kind of tasks, we performed experiments with
automatic ￿tness functions, as well as with partially user-guided evolution. W e
tested our approach in the optimization of the parameters of two di￿erent edge
bundling algorithms. Results are compared using objective criteria and a critical
discussion of the obtained graphical solutions. ￿ese show the e￿ectiveness of
the approach: the evolved solutions are better than those obtained when guiding
evolution based on a single metric, and better than those when obtained using
a weighted sum method to combine the di￿erent criteria. Finally, the generated
bundled drawings are easy to read, and clutter free.
￿.￿. Introduction
￿e ever growing amount of available data makes it di￿cult for scientists to extract
relevant information, i.e., it is even hard for the expert users to identify which parts
of the available information are of importance. An example of such data is the
information related with keeping track of moving entities (e.g., people migration,

￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
imports or exports of traded goods). T o aid in the visualization of such type of
datasets we normally use ￿ow maps, that show the ￿ow of information between
data points. An example of application of ￿ow maps to the visualization of moving
entities can be found in Chapter ￿.
￿ere are multiple edge bundling algorithms, which already have been covered
in Section ￿ .￿.￿. Although their goal is to reduce the visual clutter, they vary in
visual results, complexity, approach to the problem, number of variables taken
into account, etc. ￿erefore, for non-expert users de￿ning appropriate parameters
for methods they are not used to working with is an arduous trial-and-error task.
Furthermore, the datasets also in￿uence the parametrization of the algorithms,
and for di￿erent datasets it may be necessary to adapt the parameters. T o overcome
this handcra￿ process we propose the use of Evolutionary Algorithm ( ￿￿), which
are known to work well for parameter optimization problems (Wright, ￿￿￿￿).
￿e current work proposes an automatic search procedure for optimizing the
parametrization of edge bundling algorithms, based on Genetic Algorithm ( ￿￿),
which are well-known for their performance in hyper-parameter tuning tasks. ￿e
proposed method automatically assists in ￿nding parameters for edge bundling
algorithms that generate near-optimal solutions using the proposed metrics. ￿ese
metrics consist of measuring the ink to white space ratio and the edge curvature
of bundled drawings. By maximizing white space and minimizing edge curvature
the gain is twofold: on the one hand, the generated solutions are clutter free and
space use e￿cient; and, on the other hand, solutions present smooth and easy-to-
follow edges and streams of ￿ow. Moreover, our approach allows the users to take
part in the evolutionary cycle, by giving them the possibility to participate in the
evaluation step of the evolutionary process.
Regarding the automatic evolution we address the evaluation based on one, and
multiple metrics simultaneously. T o guide evolution considering more than one
objective we combine them linearly, using a variation of a weighted sum method.
However, we hypothesize that because the evaluation metrics are con￿icting, i.e.,
better performance values in one of the metrics can lead to worse values on others,
it may be better to guide evolution using Pareto-based evolutionary approaches.
Summing up, with this work we intend to move towards objective edge bundling
quality measures, such as, curvature and white space to ink ratio, as well as the
combination of both. Moreover, we test and study the e￿ectiveness of these metrics
in the context of evolutionary optimization applied to Information Visualization.
￿e application of ￿￿ is another important contribution of this work. T o the
best of our knowledge this is the ￿rst work where evolutionary algorithms are
applied in the automatic parametrization of edge bundling procedures. Even if
automatic evolution presupposes the de￿nition of objective criteria, this can always
be complemented by interaction with the user, allowing a creative and subjective
graphical exploration.
In addition to guiding evolution based on the Pareto-optimal set, we also allow
the evolution of multiple sets of parameters, where the parameters of each set are

￿.￿ Background and Related Work ￿￿￿
only applied to speci￿c parts of the dataset. Doing this makes the algorithm able
to cope with heterogeneity in data.
￿e remainder of the chapter is organized as follows. Section ￿.￿ surveys the
background and related works; Section ￿.￿ details the developed evolutionary
framework; Section ￿.￿ presents, analyzes and discusses the conducted experiments;
Section ￿.￿ presents the experiments with the users; Section ￿.￿ discusses our
approach on multiobjective optimization; followed by the results (Section ￿.￿); in
Section ￿.￿ conclusions are drawn and future work is addressed.
￿is chapter proceeds with background and related topics, such as ￿ow maps,
genetic algorithms, and the application of these to the ￿eld of information visualiza-
tion. ￿en we present a detailed explanation of our method, including a description
of the framework and used ￿tness functions. Next, we discuss the results obtained
with the automatic ￿tness metrics, which are followed by user testing. T o end,
conclusions are drawn and future work is addressed.
￿.￿. Background and Related Work
￿is work is focused on the use of ￿ow maps for visualizing data and application
of edge bundling techniques to facilitate the extraction of knowledge from it.
T o do that we need to know how to correctly parametrize the edge bundling
algorithms. ￿erefore, the areas that are concerned with ￿ow maps, visualization,
and evolutionary computation are covered in this section. Since the ￿ow mapping
and techniques for edge-bundling have already been reviewed in Sections ￿.￿.￿ and
￿ .￿.￿, we proceed with evolutionary computation and application of its methods in
information visualization.
￿.￿.￿. Interactive Evolutionary Computation
In typical applications of Evolutionary Computation ( ￿￿) the end goal is clear
and thus a ￿tness function is easy to de￿ne. However, in problems where the
quality of the evolved solutions depends on subjective notions (e.g., aesthetics of
the individual’ s phenotype) the de￿nition of automatic ￿tness functions is harder.
￿at is because de￿ning aesthetics is an open problem, where the notion of pleasing,
and usefulness varies according to the domain of application (Romero et al., ￿￿￿￿).
In order to promote evolution in such subjective domains, we o￿en need to
include the user in the evolutionary cycle. From generation to generation the usual
evolutionary cycle of parent selection and application of the genetic operations is
kept. However, instead of an automatic ￿tness function, the user is asked to rank,
select, or evaluate the generated solutions. ￿e methodology is known as Interactive
Evolutionary Computation (￿￿￿) (Takagi, ￿￿￿￿). If the evolution is guided by the
user, but also aided by automatic ￿tness components the methodology is referred
to as partially interactive (Machado et al., ￿￿￿￿).
As the user is now part of the evolutionary cycle, care must be taken in deciding
the population size, because if the population is too large it may cause user fatigue,
and lack of consistency in the assigned ￿tness values. On the other hand, if the

￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
population is too small, it is hard to keep diversity and thus avoid premature
convergence.
Examples of works where the user is asked to participate in the evaluation of
the generated solutions include NEvAr (Machado and Cardoso, ￿￿￿￿), the work
of Ralley ( ￿￿￿￿) on melodic development through the ￿￿￿,t h ew o r ko fR o o k e
(￿￿￿￿) on evolving images, and the work of Sims ( ￿￿￿￿) on generation of complex
structures, textures and animations in computer graphics.
￿.￿.￿. Application of Genetic Algorithms to Visualization
￿roughout our literature review we found application of ￿￿s in multiple areas of
information visualization, ranging from graph visualization, and schematic transit
maps, to storyline visualization.
￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿s are widely used to solve issues related with graph
representation. One of the aesthetic criteria of graph drawing is the number of
edge crossings (Battista et al., ￿￿￿￿). ￿erefore, a ￿￿ can be used, along with the
di￿erent strategies, for vertices reordering in order to minimize the number of
edge crossings. In most cases the choice of the strategy for ordering plays a crucial
role in such approaches. For instance, in hierarchical digraphs researchers apply a
strategy of sub-graph swapping using vertical and horizontal pivots (Kuntz et al.,
￿￿￿￿). For undirected graph optimization Branke et al. ( ￿￿￿￿) used sub-graph
swapping with spring embedder, and for similar problems one can apply a more
sophisticated approach by representing a graph through a matrix and by swapping
its areas in each genetic cycle (Eloranta and Mäkinen, ￿￿￿￿).
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿ Regarding schematic transit maps, Wu et al. (￿￿￿￿)
presented a zone-based algorithm for placing labels of stations in metro maps.
Using a GA, the placement of labels is optimized, ￿rst, by ￿nding the best location
for the textual labels, and then, the same approach is used for placing images.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e idea of minimizing the number of crossings
can be found in other areas of visualization. For instance, to generate aesthetically
pleasing and legible storyline visualizations Tanahashi and Ma (￿￿￿￿) developed
an automatic approach, which uses a ￿￿ to rearrange streamlines minimizing their
crossings. A particularly interesting aspect of this work is that it uses phenotype to
encode the layout of the storyline visualization.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿s can be used to search for adequate visu-
alization parameters. For instance, House et al. (￿￿￿￿)u s ea ￿￿ and user-guided
evolution to guide evolved designs towards perceptually near-optimal visualiza-
tions taking into account Gestalt principles (W ertheimer,￿￿￿￿).

￿.￿ Background and Related Work ￿￿￿
￿.￿.￿. Multiobjective Optimization
In some evolutionary optimization scenarios it may be useful, or even necessary
to consider multiple objectives that can be even con￿icting with each other. For
example, when tuning the placement of labels on schematic maps (e.g., metro
maps) the goal is to maximize the number of displayed stops, and at the same time
minimize the overlap between labels, and between the labels and the lines that
represent the path from one stop to the next.
T o address Multiobjective Optimization (￿￿￿) problems with ￿￿s it is common
yo combine the various independent objectives in a single function using a weighted
sum (Marler and Arora, ￿￿￿￿):
￿tness(x) =
N
￿
i=￿
wi fi (x), (￿.￿)
where x is the candidate solution to be evaluated, N is the number of objectives to
be considered, and fi is the value of the i-th objective of the candidate solution
x. Examples of the use of the weighted sum method are the works of Rivero et al.
(￿￿￿￿) on the evolution of e￿ective and compact Arti￿cial Neural Networks, and
Machado et al. (￿￿￿￿) on the generation of images that should gather characteristics
from multiple aesthetic measures.
￿e weighted sum method, despite simple, has disadvantages: setting the weights
of each ￿tness component is not straightforward, and even more di￿cult consider-
ing that it may be impossible to scale all metrics to the same range. T o deal with
this issue, we should focus on the use of approaches that consider the multiple
objectives independently, and use the Pareto-optimal set to guide evolution (Coello
et al., ￿￿￿￿). When we only have a single objective the superiority of a solution over
another is tested by comparing the ￿tness values. When we have multiple objectives
we have to compute the Pareto-optimal set, which comprises all the candidate
solutions that are not dominated by any other. In a maximization problem we say
that x￿ dominates x￿ if:
∀ i ∈ {￿, . . . , N} ∶ fi (x￿) ≥ fi (x￿), and
∃ k ∈ {￿, . . . , N} ∶ fk (x￿) > fk (x￿).
(￿.￿)
In addition to solving the previous limitations, approaches based on the Pareto-
front enable the discovery of optimal solutions that are placed in a concave front.
Examples of methods based on this principles are Nondominated Sorting in Ge-
netic Algorithms (￿￿￿￿) (Srinivas and Deb, ￿￿￿￿) or the Strength Pareto Evolu-
tionary Algorithm (￿￿￿￿) (Zitzler and ￿iele, ￿￿￿￿). In the current work we have
opted for using ￿￿￿￿ for one main reason: during evolution an external archive
of non-dominated solutions is continuously updated; therefore, by the end of the
evolutionary process this archive serves as a diverse repository of the best found
solutions.

￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
￿.￿. Edge-Bundling Methods
As mentioned above, there are di￿erent edge bundling algorithms. However, for
this work we selected two: our Swarm-Based Edge Bundling ( ￿w￿￿￿ ), described
earlier in Chapter ￿,a n d ￿￿￿￿ (Holten and Wijk, ￿￿￿￿). ￿e objective of the
evolutionary approach is to search for the optimal parameters that generate bundled
drawings that are clutter free, space e￿cient, and where the bundled edges are
easy-to-follow. As such, the rationale behind the choice of the edge bundling
algorithms for this work was based on how dependent they are on the selected
parameters: ￿w￿￿￿ is highly dependent on the set of parameters, while ￿￿￿￿ is less
sensible. Figure ￿.￿ displays a bundled graph using ￿w￿￿￿ (a) and ￿￿￿￿ (b) with
the parameters recommended by the authors.
(a)
(b)
Figure ￿.￿ Bundled graph resultant from the ￿w￿￿￿ (a) and ￿￿￿￿ (b) algorithms using the
parameters suggested by the authors. Visualizations depict migration ￿ows in the United
States. Nodes and edges represent cities and the migration among cities, respectively. ￿e
dataset is detailed in Section ￿.￿.￿.
Both algorithms use the dataset that represents the migration ￿ows in the
United States in their original papers. Nevertheless, from Figure￿.￿ it is perceptible

￿.￿ Edge-Bundling Methods ￿￿￿
that there is margin for improving the obtained bundled drawings. ￿ere are two
options: improve the results using a trial-and-error iterative process, until better
solutions are found; or develop a heuristic approach. ￿is work is a ￿rst step in the
direction of a heuristic approach; by using a ￿￿, we hypothesize that it is not only
possible to get near-optimal solutions in terms of the de￿ned ￿tness criteria, but it
is also possible to generate a set of intermediate ones that can be interesting from
an aesthetic point of view. Nevertheless, our approach assists in the selection of the
parameters for generating e￿cient solutions using the ￿w￿￿￿ and ￿￿￿￿ algorithms.
￿.￿.￿. Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
Figure ￿.￿ Bundled graphs generated with the ￿w￿￿￿ using di￿erent sets of parameters.
Visualizations depict migration ￿ows in the United States. Nodes and edges represent cities
and the migration among cities, respectively. ￿e dataset is further detailed in Section￿.￿.￿.
As seen in the previous Chapter ￿ depending on the parameters, the ￿w￿￿￿ al-
gorithm can generate di￿erent outputs for the same dataset (such as shown in Fig-
ure ￿.￿). One parametric combination can generate results that resemble schematic-
looking networks, while the other can generate an abstract organic-looking visual-
ization. Moreover, there are combinations of parameters that generate solutions

￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
that are arguably more e￿cient than others. As such, one drawback of this algo-
rithm is that certain combinations of parameters can lead to an imperceptible
and hard-to-read visualizations. ￿at said, our approach assists in the selection
of the parameters for generating e￿cient solutions for the ￿w￿￿￿ edge bundling
algorithm. ￿e objective of the evolutionary approach is to search for the optimal
parameters that generate bundled drawings that are clutter free, space e￿cient,
and where the bundled edges are easy-to-follow. Figure ￿.￿ displays, for the same
dataset, di￿erent bundled graphs that are obtained using distinct parametrization
of the ￿w￿￿￿ algorithm.
An important addition of the current work is a tight integration of the evolu-
tionary engine with the ￿w￿￿￿ algorithm. In the original work the bundles emerge
from the simulation of a ￿ocking system, which accounts not only for geometric
and topological properties of the graph, but also for properties of the data (e.g.,
number of migrations between the cities). T o re￿ect upon the data values, the
￿w￿￿￿ algorithm uses the mass of a vehicle. ￿e behavior of autonomous vehicles
changes in accordance to its mass – the heavier the vehicle, the slower its angular
velocity, and consequently the smoother the traces become. ￿e rationale is that
the agents that encode higher values would create smoother traces, thus setting
the main stream paths, while the agents with lower values would behave in a more
agile way, trying to follow the nearest compatible paths. At the end, the bundled
drawing would show the principal streams of ￿ow with the smaller individual
streams that would join or separate them.
In the proposed approach we are trying to achieve a similar behavior using
the ￿￿. For that we introduce subsets of parameters. Instead of optimizing global
parameters, which are shared among all the agents of the system, we optimize
subsets of parameters, which are used by the agents that encode di￿erent data
intervals. ￿is mechanism allows us to use all the parameters, and not only the
mass, to achieve the behavior described above. Furthermore, as mentioned earlier,
di￿erent combinations of parameters can result in additional visual artifacts, which
can bene￿t the overall visualization (e.g., curly endings, which indicate the direction
of the ￿ow, straight lines in the areas with only one individual trace, which reduce
overall visual clutter, etc.), see Figure ￿.￿. ￿erefore, by introducing subsets of
parameters we hope to achieve visual results that combine bene￿cial artifacts, and
exclude the unwanted ones. However, designing the ￿tness function that objectively
would distinguish bene￿cial from non-bene￿cial visual artifacts becomes even
more challenging.
￿.￿.￿. Force-Directed Edge-Bundling
￿e approach for￿￿￿￿, introduced by Holten and Wijk (￿￿￿￿), relies on the geomet-
ric calculation of bundles, taking into account edge compatibility measurements.
￿e algorithm consists in an iterative subdivision of edges, and incremental transla-
tion of vertices of pairs of edges according to the similarity measure and sti￿ness of
the edges. Basically, in each cycle the algorithm doubles the edge subdivision and
halves the step size of the translation, and performs N iterations for the translation.

￿e parameters for this algorithm are: number of cycles; number of iterations per

the application of the algorithm can result in fragmented geometric looking traces.

￿.￿. Evolutionary Edge-Bundling: Single objective
￿is section details our approach for ￿nding parameters of edge bundling algo-
rithms described in Section ￿.￿. W e start with a description of the evolutionary
engine used, including the framework and ￿tness functions that are used to mea-
sure the quality of the generated bundled drawings. ￿en we present the results
and discuss the testing of the framework with the users.
￿.￿.￿. Framework and Fitness Function
￿e framework proposed in this work is based in a ￿￿, where the genotype encode
the edge bundling algorithm parameters, and therefore are represented as ordered
linear sequences (such as show in Figure ￿.￿). Each position in the linear sequence
encodes a parameter and assumes a value in the range allowed for that speci￿c
parameter. ￿e phenotype is the generated bundle, and the data is the ambient.
Each genotype can generate N distinct phenotypes, depending on the ambient.
In other words, to evaluate the candidate solutions it is necessary to apply the
parameters on the data in order to generate bundles, which are then evaluated.
Finally, to promote evolution we use one-point crossover and per-gene mutation.
De￿ning a metric that automatically assesses the quality of candidate solutions is
hard. On the one hand, we can measure the ratio of the ink to white space (Gansner
and Koren, ￿￿￿￿), or the edge curvature in a bundled drawing. On the other
hand, we can measure the e￿ectiveness by performing a user-guided evolution,
which is strongly dependent on the user perception and on the task and dataset at
hand. Traditional approaches, such as the minimization of edge crossings, are not
applicable because edge bundling favors such crossings. ￿erefore, we consider

￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
the following alternatives: (i) the ink-white-space ratio, (ii) a combination of edge
curvature and white space, and (iii) the user in the loop to assist the evaluation of
bundled drawings. ￿e ￿rst two measures are used as automatic ￿tness functions,
while user in the loop is used in the context of a partially interactive approach.
￿.￿.￿. Automatic Evolution
T o automatically assess the quality of the evolved bundles two metrics are de￿ned:
white space and curvature. ￿ey can be used separately or combined.
￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿ is a ratio that de￿nes the percentage of white pixels in the drawing
canvas. For a bundle to be considered good we want to maximize the white space,
so that the visual clutter is reduced, hopefully increasing the readability of the
representation. As such, the ￿tness function consists of the number of white pixels
in the canvas, which is then normalized using the maximum and minimum number
of pixels. ￿e maximum number of pixels is obtained by multiplying the width
and height of the canvas. Since the canvas is never painted in total, the minimum
value was empirically determined, in order to obtain values in the [￿, ￿] interval.
￿is is not relevant for the evolutionary process when used on its own, although, it
is crucial when combining with the curvature component. Otherwise, the white
space component would not have enough impact in the ￿nal ￿tness value.
￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿ is a measure that comes from di￿erential geometry and that de-
scribes a curve in the Euclidean space (Pressley, ￿￿￿￿). T o compute the curvature,
the edges (E) are de￿ned as a sequence of vertices, E = {v￿ , v￿ ,..., vn}. ￿e cur-
vature, ki = dθ ￿dS , is computed at each vertex vi , starting at v￿ and ￿nishing at
vn−￿, where dθ is the angle between vectors ￿￿vi−￿ − vi ￿￿ and ￿￿vi − vi+￿￿￿,a n ddS is
the Euclidean distance between points vi−￿ and vi+￿. So, the ￿nal curvature is the
average of all the curvatures of an edge, and is equal to

where n is the number of vertices. In simple words, the curvature consists of the
average of the angular velocities along the trace, and it is natural that a straight line
has a curvature of zero. So, in our case the curvature should not be too high, since
smooth curves are easier to follow visually. ￿erefore, we want to minimize the
curvature, but retaining some degree of curviness. Finally, the total curvature of a
bundled graph is a sum of all the edge curvatures, and since the number of edges
is the same within a dataset it is fair to compare di￿erent bundled drawings.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ Both components are used to enhance the results obtained
by the evolutionary framework. By minimizing the curvature the algorithm tends
to use straight lines and the space becomes more ￿lled with ink, therefore reducing
white space. As such, by maximizing the white space and minimizing the curvature,

￿.￿ Results ￿￿￿
we expect to achieve a balanced bundled drawing, where the edges are smooth
enough to be easy to follow, and the ink to white space ratio is low, so that the
visualization is clean and easy to understand. For that, we de￿ne a ￿tness function
in the form of composite _ fi t n e s s= W￿(￿ + C), where W is white space and C is
curvature. W e add￿ to the curvature, because the values of C can be in the [￿, ￿]
interval. ￿e ￿￿ algorithm attempts to maximize the ￿tness function.
￿.￿.￿. User-Guided Evolution
Another aspect of this work is the fact that it allows the user’s preferences to be
incorporated in the evolutionary process. Although preliminary work shows that
the results obtained by automatic evolution are near-optimal, at least in terms of
spatial e￿ciency, there are cases where bundles should be less overlapped to get a
clearer picture. For that, we allow the users to take part in the evaluation of the
generated solutions, so that the evolutionary process is also guided according to
their preferences.
With user-guided evolution the evaluation cycle is divided into two steps. First,
all the individuals are evaluated using one of the automatic ￿tness functions. ￿en,
the user is given a subset of the generated solutions to evaluate: ￿ of the best
solutions of the population, ￿ with intermediate ￿tness scores, and the ￿ worst
solutions (these values were determined empirically). ￿is way, we allow the user
to have an overall view of the population without forcing him/her to evaluate the
entire population. ￿e user gives a score in the range [￿, ￿] to each of the subset
solutions. ￿ese evaluations are then normalized and combined with the automatic
component, weighting both components. In order to give higher impact to the
user evaluations and less to the automatic ￿tness the components are assigned a
weight of ￿￿% and ￿￿%,r e s p e c t i v e l y .
￿e above evaluation procedure is performed using a graphical user interface,
in which the subset individuals are presented in a grid with four columns and
the required number of rows, depending on the number of selected individuals,
which is ￿￿ in our case. ￿e evaluation is given using ￿ve radio buttons on top of
each picture. By pressing the “next generation” button the evolutionary process
proceeds to the next generation. ￿e process ends when the user is satis￿ed with
the obtained results. Figure ￿.￿ displays the ￿￿￿ for guiding the evolution process.
￿.￿. Results
￿is section describes and discusses the experiments conducted with automatic
￿tness functions, which consist of: (i) experimenting with ￿￿￿￿ algorithm using
white space only; (ii) experimenting with ￿w￿￿￿ using white space only; and (iii)
experimenting with ￿w￿￿￿ using composite ￿tness (white space and curvature).
Since the ￿￿￿￿ algorithm ensures smooth curves we have not performed experi-
ments focusing on the evolution towards both components. Preliminary results
with curvature only show that it generates unfeasible results, as minimizing the
curvature promotes the generation of straight lines. Conversely, if curvature was

￿.￿.￿. Discussion
Figure ￿.￿ depicts the evolution of the best individuals across ￿￿￿ generations. Re-
sults are averages of ￿￿ independent runs for each experiment and each chart com-
ponent is normalized by the maximum value found for that component throughout
all performed experiments. Analyzing the charts allows to conclude that the algo-
rithm is able to promote evolution, reaching areas of the search space that contain
the desired solutions. In the experiment where the white space is used to guide the
evolution of the SwBEB algorithm the convergence is faster than when combining
white space with curvature. ￿is result is expected, because promoting evolution
towards multiple objectives makes the task harder.
Focusing on the best solutions found with both approaches, when evolution is
guided by the white space only (Figure ￿.￿, the ￿rst two charts), it is observable
that in both of them the evolutionary process takes around ￿￿ generations to
converge. Moreover, the obtained solutions are of similar quality (in terms of
￿tness), although the ones achieved by ￿w￿￿￿ are slightly better than the ones
obtained by ￿￿￿￿. When promoting the evolution of ￿w￿￿￿ using composite ￿tness
function (Figure ￿.￿, last chart) the evolutionary process seems to be still evolving
at a slow pace by the end of the runs. Additionally, white space appears to be the
￿tness component that most contributes to evolution, as it is the one that more
closely follows ￿tness evolution, with a less erratic behavior. ￿at is, curvature is
less stable than white space during the evolutionary process, which may indicate
that optimizing curvature is an objective that is harder to achieve.
Examples of the results obtained are depicted in Figure ￿.￿, where we select the
best individuals from the initial, intermediate and last generations of a random
evolutionary run, using ￿w￿￿￿ with both automatic ￿tness components. As observ-
able, as evolution proceeds, the artifacts become less cluttered, and the streams of
￿ow become easier to perceive.
Regarding the evolved parameters for the edge bundling algorithms, we have
found that, in general, these yield e￿cient, easy to read solutions. Figure ￿.￿ dis-
plays the best and worst individuals for each experiment using automatic ￿tness
functions. ￿e very ￿rst observation shows that the best solutions (a–c) are visually

distinct from the worst ones (d–f). In general, the worst solutions result in straight
lines (see Figure ￿.￿ (d)) or “hairy” drawings (see Figure ￿.￿ (e, f)). In what regards
spatial e￿ciency, the best results are clean and clutter free. ￿e main streams of
￿ow are distinguishable and well-de￿ned.
When we focus on the comparison of the best results obtained by each approach
(Figure ￿.￿ (a, b, c)), we can observe that the use of ￿w￿￿￿ with composite ￿tness
appears to yield the most cluttered result (Figure ￿.￿ (c)). ￿is is because the
two goals are, to some extent, contradictory. ￿e curvature component of the
￿tness function guides evolution towards solutions with straight lines, and thus,
more ￿lled drawings, which contradicts to white space maximization. Finally,
the application of composite ￿tness results in drawings with smoother bundles
and shorter branches, but generates more streams in comparison with the other
approaches.
In contrast, using ￿w￿￿￿ with white space ￿tness only (Figure ￿.￿ (b)) tends
to result in schematic looking representations, favoring abrupt changes in trace
directions and fewer main streams. Although the schematic nature of the results
has some aesthetic appeal, this type of representation can be disadvantageous
for the representation of large complex graphs, since it is hard to follow paths
with many changes in direction. Finally, we have observed that when using ￿￿￿￿
it is su￿cient to apply white space ￿tness to generate smooth bundles naturally
(Figure ￿.￿ (a)). ￿is is because the ￿￿￿￿ algorithm ensures the smoothness of the
lines, while the evolutionary process searches for spatial e￿cient solutions.

￿.￿. User T esting
￿is section provides detailed description of the experimental setup for the user
testing, as well as a critical discussion of the results. W e also summarize comments

and observations le￿ by the users while exploring the system. ￿e main goals are

￿.￿.￿. Setup
For the current set of experiments the following experimental setup is used: each
user tested the system individually, using the same server machine with an external
monitor, mouse and keyboard; before the test the users indicate their experience in
the ￿eld of Information Visualization using a range of one to ￿ve, where ￿ means
no experience in the ￿eld, and ￿ the opposite, i.e., that the user is an expert in the
￿eld.
In the beginning of the test, a brief explanation is provided to each user in order
to describe what edge bundling is and what is the goal of the task, i.e., ￿nding an
adequate edge bundling solution for the data. ￿en, we ask him/her to perform the

￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
same task using two di￿erent approaches: (i) direct manipulation of parameters
(￿￿), and (ii) partially user guided evolution ( ￿￿). A￿er the test the users evaluate
each approach indicating a preference score in the range of one to ￿ve, meaning
not desirable and highly preferable, respectively.
In order to measure the performance of each approach, we used three criteria:
￿. Useful time – duration of the task execution excluding the processing time,
since di￿erent edge bundling algorithms may have di￿erent execution times;
￿. Total timeof task execution;
￿. Accuracy of the found solutions in the range [￿, ￿], meaning poor and near-
optimal, respectively, within the possibilities of the given edge bundling algo-
rithm.
T o measure the accuracy of the edge bundling solutions we established the
following criteria: compactness of edges, curvature of bundled edges, waviness of
edges, aesthetics, e￿ciency of the ￿ow representation, and a subjective evaluation
taking into account our opinion. ￿e ￿nal accuracy score is the average of all these
items.

In what concerns task execution time, the results indicate that through the
(￿￿) the users ￿nd the solution in half of the time of the ( ￿￿). Since parametric
search happens in real time the users ￿nd a solution in shorter time. Also, since
no reference image is provided, their choices are based on the previous solutions.
In contrast, when using the ( ￿￿) the users are confronted with a variety that
they analyze and evaluate. ￿rough (￿￿) most users tend to conclude the task in
approximately the same time, hence the low standard deviation. In contrast, the

￿.￿ Evolutionary Edge Bundling: Multiobjective ￿￿￿
duration of the task performance using the (￿￿) varies drastically, because, despite
the initial population being the same, the users can take di￿erent paths as they
assign di￿erent ￿tness values. Moreover, while some users appear to be focused
on reaching a speci￿c goal, others assume an exploratory approach. ￿ey invest
more time in exploration, although, it is not wasted time, as indicated by both
better accuracy and preference in comparison to ( ￿￿). Additionally, the useful
time for interaction with the (￿￿) is smaller than the total time, as the system takes
some time to generate the solutions, which depends on the used edge bundling
algorithm performance.
Regarding the accuracy of the solutions found, the ( ￿￿) performs better than
(￿￿), which is expected as instead of a single solution we are evolving a diverse set,
and thus covering a broader area of the search space. ￿is quality is also more consis-
tent in the (￿￿) than in the (￿￿), which is re￿ected in the lower standard deviation.
￿us, the results indicate that (￿￿) is more reliable, systematically resulting in high
quality results, which is an important ￿nding. Moreover, and perhaps even more
importantly, the (￿￿) clearly outperforms (￿￿) in terms of user preference. ￿e
majority of the users ￿nd the ( ￿￿) encouraging for exploration, and easier to use
and understand. However, user feedback indicates that the (￿￿) should preserve at
least one evaluated solution in the next generation, or provide an archive, so the
user can compare the current solutions with the ones he/she preferred earlier.
Our observations indicate that most users do not favor the solutions that would
be best according to the considered automatic ￿tness functions. Interestingly,
they tend to opt for the solutions that have poor automatic ￿tness. In particular,
users prioritize solutions that generate unnecessary, and o￿en “undesirable” , visual
artifacts, such as curly endings, spirals, etc. Regarding user interaction with the
user guided system, some users evaluate all the solutions, and other evaluate only
the ones they like most. Also, we observe that a￿er ￿nding a solution some users
continue exploring the system, justifying it by their curiosity regarding of what
may come next. ￿is, obviously, in￿ates useful and total time.
￿.￿. Evolutionary Edge Bundling: Multiobjective
￿is section details our approach for ￿nding parameters of edge bundling algo-
rithms using multiobjective methods. ￿e edge bundling algorithm used in this
part of the work is our ￿w￿￿￿ presented in previous Chapter ￿. ￿is section pro-
vides a description of the evolutionary engine, including the framework and ￿tness
functions that are used to measure the quality of the generated bundled drawings,
as well as the discussion of the results.
￿.￿.￿. Framework
￿e goal of this work is to automatically search for the optimal parameters for the
￿w￿￿￿ algorithm. W e adapt the representation described in previous Section￿.￿ to
deal with the evolution of multiple groups of parameters. T o guide evolution we
investigate metrics to automatically assess the quality of the candidate solutions.

Figure ￿.￿ Example of a genotype of a candidate solution for the￿w￿￿￿ algorithm. ￿e two
parameters are global followed by sets of parameters, where N is the established number

However, de￿ning good bundles is hard, because it is a subjective task, like most de-
sign problems which are linked to usability, perception and aesthetics. T o that end,
as previously, we de￿ne two metrics: white space and curvature (see Section ￿.￿.￿).
Using the de￿ned evaluation metrics we ￿rst test each of them separately, and then
combined, using the principles of the ￿￿￿￿ algorithm (Zitzler and ￿iele, ￿￿￿￿)
(see Section ￿.￿ .￿.￿).
￿.￿ .￿.￿. Representation
￿e candidate solutions represent valid parametrization for the SwBEB algorithm.
Each candidate solution is encoded as an ordered linear sequence of real-values,
that represent the parameters to be used by the edge bundling algorithm. For each
parameter we de￿ne a valid range, that must be respected upon the application of
the genetic operators.
W e allow the simultaneous evolution of multiple sets of parameters, i.e., the
dataset instances are partitioned in multiple disjoint sets and the parameters tuned
for each group. ￿is way, it is possible to discover optimal solutions for each group
of data points, enabling the generation of easy to apprehend bundled graphs. ￿e
parameters to be optimized can be part of the groups (i.e., speci￿c to each dataset
partition), or global (i.e., the same value for all groups). For the SwBEB algorithm
the stabilization rate and similarity angle parameters are global; all the remaining
parameters are optimized for each dataset partition.
Figure ￿.￿ depicts an example of the genotype of a candidate solution for the
￿w￿￿￿ algorithm.
￿.￿ .￿.￿. Multiobjective Evolution
As previously mentioned, the proposed ￿tness metrics are con￿icting: by minimiz-
ing the curvature the algorithm tends to straight lines, and consequently the space
becomes more ￿lled with ink, reducing the white space. ￿erefore, by promoting
the optimization of both components we expect to achieve balanced bundled draw-
ings, where the edges are smooth enough to be easy to follow, and the ink to white
ratio is low, so that the visualization is clean and easy to understand.
We us e t he￿￿￿￿ algorithm, which has two main di￿erences when compared
with common evolutionary algorithm approaches:

￿.￿ Results ￿￿￿
• ￿ere is an external population (archive) to preserve the non-dominated so-
lutions found during evolution. By the end of the evolutionary process this
archive contains all the discovered dominating solutions;
• Solutions are compared using the notion of dominance (Equation ￿.￿), which
is mapped to the strength value – proportion of the population individuals a
member of the archive dominates (it is a ￿tness metric).
￿e individuals in the population are ranked by summing the strengths of all
the archive solutions that dominate it, plus one. ￿e parents are selected using a
binary tournament, where both the current population and the archive members
participate; the probability of selecting each of the solutions to be part of the
tournament is proportional to the ￿tness value, where the lower the ￿tness the
higher the probability of being selected.
￿e archive works as a diversity preserving mechanism, and is updated at
the end of each evolutionary generation: the non-dominated solutions of the
population are added to the archive. A￿er adding the Pareto-front of the population
to the archive it is necessary to check whether or not there are dominated solutions
in the archive; if so, they are removed. T o control the archive size the authors detail
a clustering approach, where only the centroid of each cluster is preserved in the
archive. However, from preliminary experiments the size of our archive did not
reach values that would slow the evolutionary process, and therefore we do not
implement any archive size control measures.
S￿￿￿ does not impose any restrictions on the crossover and mutation opera-
tors. W e use the same genetic operators as previously described in Section￿.￿.￿:
one-point crossover, and per-gene mutation where each real-value as a uniform
probability of being replaced by another valid one.
￿.￿. Results
￿is section describes the experiments conducted for the automatic optimization
of the parameters of the ￿w￿￿￿ algorithm. In the next sub-sections we detail the
experimental setup (Section ￿.￿.￿), followed by the results and discussion (Sec-
tion ￿.￿.￿).
￿.￿.￿. Experimental Setup
As previously, the dataset for this work comes from the U.S. Census Bureau. ￿e
data consists of migration ￿ows among big cities in the U.S. from ￿￿￿￿ to ￿￿￿￿,
and was partitioned into two disjoint sets: train and test. ￿e train set is used
during the evolutionary process to ￿nd a suitable set of parameters that yield a
clean representation of the available data; the test set is kept out of the evolutionary
process and is used for validation purposes to assess if the found parameters
perform well beyond the training data, i.e., to assess the generalization ability of
the found parametrization.
￿e parameters used for the conducted experiments are detailed in Table ￿.￿,
and are divided into two main sets: those parameters associated with the evolu-

￿.￿.￿. Experimental Results
W e divide the conducted experiments into two sets. First we focus on the analysis of
each of the designed evaluation metrics, and conduct experiments guided by white
space, and curvature, in isolation. Next, we promote the evolution of solutions that
should ￿nd a trade-o￿ between the two metrics, in a multiobjective fashion.
￿.￿.￿.￿. Assessment of the Evaluation Metrics E￿ectiveness
T o better understand if the designed evaluation metrics are capable of generating
solutions that are in accordance with our initial suppositions we promote the
evolution of each of them separately. Figure ￿.￿￿ shows the evolution of the value
of the two proposed metrics: white space (le￿), and curvature (right). Recall that
while the goal of evolution is to maximize white space, the curvature is to be

A perusal of the results shows that throughout generations evolution is able
to ￿nd better solutions in terms of the ￿tness values of the candidate solutions.
Nevertheless, from one generation to the next, sometimes the ￿tness degrades.
￿is happens because although we use elitism, the copied candidate solutions are
re-evaluated, and due to the stochastic components of ￿w￿￿￿ di￿erent evaluations
can generate slightly di￿erent bundled drawings that provide di￿erent values for
the evaluation metrics. Nonetheless, with both metrics it is possible to ￿nd close to
perfect solutions: in the case of the white space the optimal value is the number of
pixels of the canvas (￿￿￿￿ × ￿￿￿ = ￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ); the optimal value of the curvature
is ￿. ￿e value of the white space metric on the last generation is further from the
optimal than the curvature; this is an expected result because although candidate
solutions can ￿nd bundled drawings composed only of straight lines, it is not
possible to generate completely empty bundled drawings.
Regardless of the ￿tness values, we want to check whether or not the generated
solutions provide good bundled drawings. For that we inspect the visual results.
Figure ￿.￿￿ depicts for each of the metrics the best and worst individuals. ￿e
very ￿rst observation shows that the best solutions (a, b) are distinct from the
worst ones (c, d). Focusing on the best solutions, when evolution is guided by the
white space only (a, c), it is observable that, in general, these yield e￿cient, easy to
read solutions. In general, the worst solutions result in straight lines (c) or “curly”
drawings (d). In what regards spatial e￿ciency, the best results that use white space
are clean and clutter free. ￿e main streams of ￿ow are distinguishable and well-
de￿ned. Also, using ￿w￿￿￿ with the white space metric tends to result in schematic
looking representations, favoring abrupt changes in trace directions and fewer main
streams. Although the schematic nature of the results has some aesthetic appeal,
this type of representation can be disadvantageous for the representation of large
complex graphs, since it is hard to follow paths with many changes in direction.
￿e solutions for curvature only yield straight line representations, which is on
its own contradictory to the logic of edge bundling. With that said, using only

￿.￿.￿.￿. Multiobjective Evolution
W e start by establishing a comparison between the results reported by two multi-
objective approaches: (i) the single objective method described in previous Sec-
tion ￿.￿.￿; and (ii) the ￿￿￿￿ algorithm (Section ￿.￿ .￿). In this ￿rst experiment we
only consider one set of parameters, i.e., the same value for each parameter is ap-
plied to the entire dataset. ￿is is done to enable the comparison with the previous
single objective method, where there was no notion of sets of parameters. Whilst
in the single objective method it is easy to select the ￿ttest individuals (there is
only one), in the ￿￿￿￿ algorithm there is no notion of what is the best solution,
but we rather have a set of Pareto-optimal solutions. For that reason, for the ￿￿￿￿
algorithm we focus on the analysis of the archive.
Figure ￿.￿￿ shows the evolution of each of the evaluation metrics of the best
individuals for both approaches, across generations. Results are averages of ￿￿
independent runs, and show that promoting evolution using the ￿￿￿￿ algorithm
(Pareto-based evolution) generates better results using curvature component than
with the previous single objective approach. ￿e best solutions are superior in
curvature, however inferior in the white space metrics. Examples of the generated
bundled drawings are shown in Figure ￿.￿￿. It is important to note that the results
of the Pareto-guided evolution comprise very similar characteristics, tending to
the straight line representations (such as shown in Figure ￿.￿￿(b)). ￿is is due to
the fact that the curvature objective has a major impact in the evaluations, since

A￿er this initial experiment, we focus on the impact of allowing the evolution
of multiple sets of parameters using the ￿￿￿￿ algorithm. T o analyze the quality of
the evolved solutions we check the performance of those individuals that belong
to the archive that contains the non-dominated solutions. It is expected that as

algorithm is elitist, and thus solutions on the archive are only removed if dominated
by others. W e are well aware that the solutions that show the best performance
in the white space objective are not necessarily the best ones on the curvature,

at the average trends of the archive, i.e., instead of looking only for the solutions
that provide the best value for each of the metrics we average all the Pareto-front
individuals.
