￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
distance to the target curvature. ￿is way it is possible to identify clusters of bundled
drawings visually. As can be observed, the edge bundling solutions improved
in comparison to the previous experiments. ￿e bundles are well compacted,
depicting the graph in a clutter free form. ￿e streams are visually separable,
providing the notion of the main streams of ￿ow. ￿e traces are smooth enough to
follow visually.
￿.￿. Chapter Summary
In this chapter we have discussed our experimental approach to tackle the issue of
parametrization in edge bundling problems. Our methodology is based on a genetic
algorithm to search the parameter’s space. Each candidate solution encodes the
parameters that need to be de￿ned so that the edge bundling algorithm generates
feasible solutions according to the de￿ned quality metric.
Since it is hard to de￿ne criteria to objectively measure the quality of edge
bundling, we propose two automatic metrics: the ratio between ink and white space,
and the curvature of bundled drawings. ￿ese metrics can be used independently or
combined. Moreover, we also allow the user to be included in the ￿tness assessment
stage, to measure the quality of the evolved drawings, guiding the evolutionary
process according to his/her preferences.
￿e results obtained focusing on the automatic parametrization of the edge
bundling methods show that the approach is capable of ￿nding solutions that
generate adequate visual results, where path ￿ows are gathered, reducing visual
clutter and increasing readability. ￿e results seem to generalize well, i.e., solutions
found using the train data produce good results when applied to the test data.
However, further experiments need to be conducted to assess the generalization
ability of the approach. More precisely, we plan to measure the quality of the test
solutions during evolution; despite not playing a direct role in the evolutionary
process, this analysis would allow us to verify if the increases in the train ￿tness
are or not closely followed by increases in the ￿tness measured on the test set.
T o better understand the e￿ectiveness of the approach we perform user testing,
where direct manipulation (which is commonly used in edge bundling ￿ne-tuning)
is compared with the interactive evolutionary approach. In this experiment, users
with di￿erent backgrounds and areas of expertise were asked to perform a unique
task and to evaluate both methods. ￿e results were compared between the two
methods, as well as, with the automatic approach, using a combination of objective
and subjective criteria. Analyzing the results and users’ feedback we conclude that
user guided evolution outperforms the direct manipulation in what regards to the
quality of the produced visualizations and user satisfaction. Using this method
users take twice the time to ￿nd the desired solutions, which can be explained not
only by the need of performing more image evaluations but also by the desire to
further explore the system, even when a satisfactory solution was already found.
As observed during the experiments, the creation of high quality visual results is
dependent on the chosen ￿tness function. ￿erefore, we plan to expand the current
work by exploring and investigating new ￿tness functions, capable of addressing

￿.￿ Chapter Summary ￿￿￿
Figure ￿.￿￿ Parallel coordinate plot of the Pareto-optimal solutions from last generation of
all ￿￿ evolutionary runs targeted at maximizing the white space and reducing the distance
to a target curvature of ￿.￿. ￿e actual values presented were computed as (target −
curvature ) ∗ ￿￿￿￿ to change the scale and invert the logic of minimization. Each group of
solutions is colored di￿erently. Examples of a bundled drawings for each group presented
bellow. ￿e graphic uses low opacity, making overplotted lines visible.

￿￿￿ Evolutionary Swarm-Based Edge Bundling
some of the questions that were raised by the users when using the application.
Moreover, during the user tests, it was noticeable that o￿en the user rankings
do not match the automatic ￿tness function rankings (moreover, di￿erent users
likely give di￿erent rankings to the same solution). As such, it is our intention
to allow evolution to be guided only by the user opinion, without any automatic
￿tness function component to assist the process. Additionally, we plan to study
the trade-o￿ between aesthetics, ornamentation and functionality, since the users
demonstrate interest in ornamented appealing representations.
Additionally, the presented work builds upon this single objective approach
with the addition of the following properties: (i) multiobjective evolution based
on a Pareto-based approach, ￿￿￿￿; and (ii) change of the curvature metric to
approximate a target value instead of just minimizing.
Results show that guiding evolution considering multiple objectives leads to
the discovery of bundled drawings that are more clutter free, and easy to read, than
if considering only a single objective. Moreover, the use of a Pareto-front method,
such as ￿￿￿￿, allows for more interesting results than those obtained when using
a single objective to combine the various evaluation metrics. ￿is is evident not
only in what concerns the values of the metrics, but also by visual inspection of the
generated bundled drawings. Lastly, we conclude that minimization is not the best
way to deal with curvature. A curvature of ￿ represents straight lines, and thus a
compromise between straight lines and high values of white space is hard to attain.
In addition, straight lines make it hard to reduce the number of intersections, and
consequently reduce the readability of the bundled drawings. ￿e minimization of
the distance to a target curvature of ￿.￿ signi￿cantly enhanced the visual quality
of the bundled drawings.
Designing and adding more objectives to be considered during evolution can
signi￿cantly enhance the results. In the future we plan to test more metrics that can
target two di￿erent levels: (i) the analysis of the bundled drawings; and/or (ii) the
analysis of the swarm properties. In addition to the white space and curvature of the
bundled drawings we can also consider other raster and geometric properties (e.g.,
compactness). Regarding the swarm we want to analyze the response of each agent
to the data it codi￿es. ￿e majority of these metrics were still not implemented
due to the high computational cost they require.
￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿￿ ￿e contributions described in this chapter are the following:
• A novel method for automatic search of parameters of an edge bundling algo-
rithm based on Genetic Algorithm. Our method is tightly integrated with the
measurement of bundling quality.
• Metrics for measuring the quality of bundling in graphs, namely: white space
and curvature, and the combination of both.
• A framework and the study for two approaches of parameter optimization:
single objective and multiobjective.
• A method for guiding evolutionary system based on user’s preferences while
searching for edge bundling solution.

Chapter ￿ pp. ￿￿￿–￿￿￿
Conclusions
￿ematic maps, and geovisualization in general, are an e￿ective tool for communi-
cating di￿erent types of information (e.g., usage of urban space, tra￿c conditions,
weather conditions, social distribution, among others) helping in analysis, decision-
making and communication of high level information. T echnological advances in
graphics and computational power, as well as the dissemination of￿￿￿ devices that
generate large amounts of geo-referenced data, have made geovisualization highly
demanded, and on the other hand, have brought new challenges in analyzing and
communicating such complex and large data. However, the true challenge for Big
Data is its visualization, which heavily relies on the human factor (e.g., perception,
cognition).
￿e ￿eld of Information Visualization and the related ￿elds of Visual Analytics
and Geographic visualization investigate the human processing of visual infor-
mation, providing methods and techniques to explore, analyze and present data,
supporting the process of high-level information acquisition, insight gathering,
and decision-making. While information visualization focuses on the questions of
how to represent data visually that amplify cognition to support the information
acquisition process (Card et al., ￿￿￿￿), Visual Analytics investigates the transfor-
mation of data to provide the possibility to detect expected patterns and discover
unexpected knowledge (Keim et al., ￿￿￿￿). Furthermore, geovisualization inte-
grates approaches from multiple research ￿elds to provide methods and tools for
visual exploration, synthesis, and presentation of geospatial data (MacEachren and
Kraak, ￿￿￿￿).
In these research ￿elds the data and the end user play a key role, providing
methods and techniques to visualize and present data in a user-centered man-
ner. ￿e processing of information acquisition, though, still proceeds in a more
exploratory way. T o face the problem of “meaningful” information visualization
and acquisition a new interdisciplinary area of adaptive information visualization
emerged. Adaptive visualization integrates techniques and methods from the ￿elds
of adaptive systems and information visualization, and lately has expanded to the
￿eld of geovisualization.
Adaptive visualization provides personalized and meaningful data representa-
tion and information acquisition. Although recent research on adaptive visualiza-
tion shows promising ways of representing data dynamically in response to the
variation of user pro￿les, context, and the data, some important aspects are under-
explored and under-evaluated. T o our best of knowledge, adaptive visualization

￿￿￿ Conclusions
systems are meant to be applied on a broad range of data disregarding domain
speci￿c characteristics, which limits application to sophisticated information visu-
alization designs. ￿e second limitation of adaptive systems is the fact that data
tends to be depicted using established visualization methods, visual layouts, and
graphical elements for the data type at hand. T o our best knowledge research on
adaptive visualizations did not investigate novel approaches, designed speci￿cally
for domain speci￿c goals, which can be used in adaptive visualization systems.
Last, but not least, the transformation of data to visual representation in adaptive
visualization approaches does not consider in all its capacity, the studies on human
perception, the e￿ects of visual methods on human perception, and the majority
of the work of past decades (e.g., Bertin ( ￿￿￿￿), Card et al. (￿￿￿￿), and Cleveland
and McGill (￿￿￿￿)). Additionally, our review clearly signals that little research was
done in the area of adaptive geovisualization, which de￿nes the starting point of
this thesis.
In this thesis we investigated diverse approaches for adaptive geographic vi-
sualization applied to Big Data. In contrast to existing approaches for adaptive
visualization and the related ￿elds of adaptive geovisualization, we identi￿ed the
need for investigating speci￿c geovisualization techniques capable of working in
adaptive systems. In particular, geovisualization methods and thematics maps need
to be investigated in terms of adaptable objects, developing techniques that adapt
visual variables and/or its layout in response to in￿uencing factors (e.g., user, data,
and context).
￿.￿. Summary
T o achieve our goals, we started with a literature review (see Part I). Our overview
of the state of the art goes beyond the main pillars of this thesis, which are adaptive
visualization and adaptive geovisualization, also including key topics for informa-
tion visualization, geovisualization and visual analytics, providing an overview of
the modern de￿nitions, taxonomies, and methods for each corresponding area. In
structured and comprehensive manner we put together theoretical background on
information visualization, visual analytics, and geographic visualization. ￿e per-
ceptual characteristics of visual mapping and the semiology were reviewed, as well
as the tasks and visual interaction in information visualization. Finally, detailed
revision of the adaptive visualization and geovisualization, including de￿nitions,
taxonomies and methods, was provided.
￿e second part of this thesis introduced our studies on methods for adaptive
geovisualization. In the ￿rst phase we explored semantic zoom applied to transit
maps (see Chapter ￿). Based on the reading level and the tasks performed at certain
zoom level the proposed method transforms, in continuous and animated fashion,
a transit map. In particular, a metro map layout, originally proposed by Harry
Beck, transforms into a geographically accurate map, retaining visual connection
of the stations in both representations. ￿e method takes zoom level and the
bounding box as the parameters, and uses a prede￿ned set of high-level tasks
that are performed at di￿erent instances of representation (e.g., identifying daily

￿.￿ Summary ￿￿￿
patterns of the ￿ows of passengers, or analyzing the usage of a speci￿c metro
station).
Another approach that adapts to the user inputs was investigated in the context
of a dot-map application (see Chapter ￿). A visualization method for multi-variate
georeferenced data to display its distribution on the geographical space through
the means of hexagonal binning was proposed. ￿e method uses hierarchical
partition of space and adaptive granularity adjustments, providing balanced trade-
o￿ between the e￿ectiveness of reading and graphical error, as well as the rendering
performance, at each zoom level. Instead of abruptly changing the representation
with the zoom, our method continuously adjusts the grid, so that the changes of
the layout are hardly perceptible, allowing smooth experiences of data analysis
and exploration. ￿is method was implemented, alongside with additional visual
techniques detailed in Chapter ￿, as an analytical tool.
Later in the dissertation we investigated the possibility of the application of the
methods inspired on nature, in particular on swarm and ant colony algorithms,
and evolutionary algorithms. In the ￿rst part, we developed a method for edge
bundling applied to ￿ow maps (see Chapter ￿). In contrast to most of the modern
edge bundling techniques, which compute bundles based on geometric properties
of graphs and/or the underlying data, the proposed method takes advantage of
steering model proposed by Reynolds and the ant-colony optimization mecha-
nisms. ￿e proposed iterative method adapts the generation of bundles based on
the data characteristics, the topology and geometry of the graph, and the layout at
each iteration instance, up to convergence to the ￿nal result. Finally, our approach
was tested on di￿erent datasets, including non-georeferenced graphs.
Additionally, we developed a method based on evolutionary algorithm for
edge-bundling (see Chapter ￿). As known, the parameters for swarm-based edge
bundling yield di￿erent results for the ￿nal layout, ranging from rigorous geometric-
looking visualization of the graph, to expressive, data-art like, representations. In
order to approximate the layout to the desired outcome, which depends on the
user intentions or target public, we built a system based on Genetic Algorithms
that is capable to learn from the preferences of the user, and ￿nd parameters that
lead to desired representation style. W e tested the algorithm on our swarm-based
edge bundling method, as well as, on the Force-Directed Edge-bundling (Holten
and Wijk, ￿￿￿￿). Last, but not least, we implemented and tested the system with
single- and multi-objective approaches, and evaluated the performance of each
one.
Finally, the work of the present thesis was developed under the context of two
areas of application of geographic visualization and Visual Analytics: (i) Urban
Mobility and (ii) Business Intelligence. In what concerns urban mobility, we built
on previous work on public transportation visualization and analysis. ￿e initial
blob-based model was improved by the addition of layout adaptation mechanisms
and adaptive scaling of visual elements. Furthermore, the model was used as a part
of a visual analytics application for studying the use of public transportation net-
works. ￿e visualization was tested with the data provided by the Transportation

￿￿￿ Conclusions
for London (TfL) ￿￿￿. In regard to business intelligence, the work was developed
under the project with the biggest retail chain in Portugal – ￿￿￿￿￿. Provided with
anonymized data of customers’ transactions, we were able to develop and test mul-
tiple visualization models, each one having its goals and purpose of application in
the analysis of customer behavior and relation between the locations of customers
residence and the supermarkets. It is to note that urban mobility is the only context
in which we could work on transit maps, while the other types of thematic maps
were studied under the ￿￿￿￿￿ project.
￿.￿. Contributions
￿e purpose of the research presented in this thesis is to explore the possibilities
of thematic maps in dynamic context applied on Big Data. Although adaptive
visualization and the related ￿eld of adaptive geovisualization show promising
approaches for representing complex data, there is still a need for investigating
speci￿c geovisualization techniques capable of working in adaptive systems, and
our work is an initial step to overcome this situation. In particular, geovisualization
methods and thematic maps were investigated in terms of adaptable objects, +and
di￿erent techniques that adapt visual variables or layout in response to in￿uenc-
ing factors were proposed. ￿is includes the development of novel visualization
techniques; application of principles of dynamic visualization, particularly in what
concerns dynamic queries and real-time representation of data; visualizations
capable to adapt graphical representation to a given context (e.g., given data types,
zoom levels, time-intervals, etc.) using methods of adaptive visualization and geo-
visualization. Last, but not least, the secondary goal of this work is to explore
di￿erent from semantic adaptation methods inspired on nature and those based
on computational geometry approaches.
As such, the overall objective of the present thesis is to explore alternative
approaches for thematic maps, speci￿cally adaptive layout and adaptive visual
variables, used in adaptive geovisualization within the context of visual analytics
applications. ￿is research explores di￿erent nature-inspired approaches, and ap-
proaches based on computational geometry for adaptive visualization, in addition
to the existing semantic based methods. T o achieve this goal the research work
was divided into several objectives:
￿. Provide su￿cient background on the areas of information visualization, Vi-
sual Analytics, geographic visualization and thematic mapping, and provide
extensive review of the methods for adaptive visualization and geovisualization,
including de￿nitions, taxonomies, and processes of adaptation;
￿. Develop adaptation methods for thematic maps, in speci￿c, adaptive layout
and visual variables applied in thematic mapping. T o ful￿ll this objective we
de￿ne following speci￿c objectives:
￿. Identify alternative mechanisms for adaptation, di￿erent from the ones
based on semantics;

￿.￿ Contributions ￿￿￿
￿. Revise exiting adaptation methods applied in maps (e.g., semantic zooming),
and develop methods for a more context-aware visualization;
￿. Develop and demonstrate the potential of the application of nature-inspired
methods and those based on computational geometry to adaptive visualiza-
tion;
￿. Assess the impact of developed methods both in terms e￿ciency, e￿cacy
and user experience.
￿is thesis ful￿lls these objectives by making the following key contributions:
• A visualization method for continuous adaptation of hexagonal maps, which
addresses the goals ￿.￿. and partially ￿.￿ (Chapter￿). In particular, we proposed a
novel approach to hexagonal binning. Our algorithm can: (i) be used to provide
hierarchical spatial structure; (ii) provide geometrical description of bins at their
corresponding level; (iii) be applied in spacial subpartition, or (iv) be used as an
alternative to Quadtrees or similar spatial structures. Additionally, we propose
a novel method for adapting V oronoi diagram to geographic features applied
to determine territorial boundaries of supermarkets. Finally, we describe an
approach for adaptive zooming and the study of the e￿ect of the resultant
graphical error, as well as dynamic adjustment of the granularity in Choropleth
maps, which is controlled by adaptive zooming. ￿is work was published in:
Polisciuc, E., Maçãs, C., Assunção, F ., and Machado, P . (￿￿￿￿)Hexagonal
gridded maps and information layers: a novel approach for the exploration
and analysis of retail data. In SIGGRAPH ASIA ￿￿￿￿ Symposium on Visual-
ization (SA ’￿￿). ACM, New Y ork, NY , USA, Article ￿, ￿ pages.
• A visualization method for adaptation of transit maps, which transforms, in con-
tinuous and animated fashion, a transit map based on the reading level and the
tasks performed at certain zoom level (Chapter ￿). ￿is contribution addresses
the goals ￿.￿, ￿.￿, and partially ￿.￿, proposing several innovative methods for:
(i) point representation aiming at working with point-based geographical data
in real-time; (ii) computing graphical error in point representation; (iii) metro
map transformation through morphing between geographical and schematic
metro maps; (iv) splitting overlapped segments; (v) representing point-based
data with varying scalings of graphical elements. ￿ese methods were incorpo-
rated into an interactive application that integrates map representation along
with circular diagram, suited for analysis of daily ￿ows of passengers in metro.
￿is work resulted in a paper:
Polisciuc, E. and Machado, P . (￿￿￿￿). Smart City Portrayal – Dynamic Visu-
alization Applied to the Analysis of Underground Metro. In A. Alavi and
W . Buttlar (Eds.),Data Analytics for Smart Cities (pp. ￿￿-￿￿￿). New Y ork:
Auerbach Publications
• A method for adaptive edge bundling applied to ￿ow maps based on models
inspired on nature, which corresponds to the goals identi￿ed in ￿.￿, ￿.￿, and
partially ￿.￿ (Chapter ￿). Speci￿cally, we proposed a model for edge bundling

￿￿￿ Conclusions
that goes beyond approaches based on computational geometry, and which
considers the properties of data and senses the context. Our model was inspired
on processes found in nature, specially swarm steering and locomotion and
stigmergy, and combines them in a coherent framework. A method for pre-
cise placement and alignment of arrowheads in directional graphs rendered
in dynamic context. Finally, we described a visualization method for density
representation in bundled graphs. ￿is work resulted in following publications:
Polisciuc, E. and Machado, P . (￿￿￿￿). Swarm-Based Edge Bundling Applied to
Flow Mapping. In Braz J. et al. (eds),Computer Vision, Imaging and Computer
Graphics ￿eory and Applications. VISIGRAPP ￿￿￿￿. Communications in
Computer and Information Science, vol ￿￿￿. Springer, Cham.;
Polisciuc, E., Cruz, P ., Amaro, H., Maçãs, C., and Machado, P . (￿￿￿￿).Flow
Map of Products Transported among Warehouses and Supermarkets. In VISI-
GRAPP (￿: IV APP) (pp. ￿￿￿-￿￿￿).
• Algorithm for automatic search of parameters in edge bundling, which address
the goals ￿.￿, ￿.￿, ￿.￿, and partially ￿.￿ (Chapter￿). ￿e proposed method is based
on Genetic Algorithms. Our method is tightly integrated with the measurement
of bundling quality. W e propose several metrics for measuring the quality of
bundling in graphs, namely white space, curvature, and the combination of
both. Also, a framework for two approaches of parameter optimization was
proposed. Finally, we describe a method for guiding evolutionary system based
on user’ s preferences while searching for edge bundling solution. ￿is work was
disseminated by the following publications:
Polisciuc, E., Assunção, F ., and Machado, P . (￿￿￿￿)T owards Partially Auto-
matic Search of Edge Bundling Parameters. In International Conference on
Computational Intelligence in Music, Sound, Art and Design (pp. ￿￿￿-￿￿￿).
Springer, Cham.;
Polisciuc, E., Cruz, A., Machado, P ., and Arrais, J. P . (￿￿￿￿).On the role of
aesthetics in genetic algorithms applied to graph drawing. In Proceedings of
the Genetic and Evolutionary Computation Conference Companion (pp.
￿￿￿￿–￿￿￿￿). ACM.
In summary, the results show that using methods inspired on nature and those
based on computational geometry, along with well-known semantic based ap-
proaches, can be a viable solution for adaptive geovisualization. ￿is thesis focuses
on several possible approaches, rising the awareness towards the need for a deeper
study on thematic mapping applied in dynamic contexts. W e consider that widen-
ing the range of adaptive techniques of thematic mapping will open new horizons in
representation of complex data in intelligent and human-centered ways, promoting
an e￿cient and enjoyable experience.
